---
<!-- defin-1: body_md -->
\label{TransportationCost}
	A \textbf{transportation cost} is a non-decreasing, lower semicontinuous, and subadditive function $\tau:\mathbb{R}_+\to\mathbb{R}_+$ with $\tau(0)=0$.
<!-- end defin-1 -->
<!-- assump-1: body_md -->
\label{GrowthCondition}
	There exists $C\in\mathbb{R}_+^*$ such that $Cm\leq\tau(m)$ for all $m\in\mathbb{R}_+$.
<!-- end assump-1 -->
<!-- assump-2: body_md -->
\label{ZeroBoundary}
	The transportation network $T_0$ is closed as a current: $\partial T_0=0$.
<!-- end assump-2 -->
<!-- assump-3: body_md -->
\label{IntegralityT0}
	The initial datum $T_0$ is integral: $T_0\in\mathcal I_1(\mathbb{R}^n)$.
<!-- end assump-3 -->
<!-- thm-1: body_md -->
\label{LimitCurrent}
	Consider the setup from above, see \cref{Prop6.1,Prop6.4}. Let \cref{GrowthCondition,ZeroBoundary} be satisfied.
	Then, possibly after refining the subsequence $(j_k)$, there exists a family of normal $1$-currents $(T_t)_{t\in\mathbb{R}_+}\subset\mathcal N_1(\mathbb{R}^n)$ with 
	\begin{equation*}
	\textup{supp}(T_t)\subset\Omega\qquad\textup{and}\qquad T_t^{j_k}\stackrel{*}{\rightharpoonup} T_t
	\end{equation*}
	for $k\to\infty$ for all $t\in\mathbb{R}_+$.
	Moreover, the map $t\mapsto T_t$ satisfies the Hölder condition
	\begin{equation*}
	|T_s-T_t|^\flat\leq C_0|s-t|^{1/2}
	\end{equation*}
	 for all $s,t\in\mathbb{R}_+$ and some constant $C_0\in\mathbb{R}_+$, where $|\cdot|^\flat$ denotes the flat norm, see \cref{Currents}. If \cref{IntegralityT0} is satisfied, then $T_t\in\mathcal I_1(\mathbb{R}^n)$ for all $t\in\mathbb{R}_+$.
<!-- end thm-1 -->
<!-- thm-2: body_md -->
\label{rectifiability}
	Let the setup be as above, see \cref{Prop6.1,Prop6.4}. Then the following holds under \cref{GrowthCondition,IntegralityT0}: For a.e.\ $t\in\mathbb{R}_+$ the induced varifolds
	\begin{equation*}
		V_t^{j_{k}}=v(\tau(|\theta_t^{j_{k}}|),S_t^{j_{k}})\in\mathcal{M}_+(\mathbb{R}^n\times G(n,1))
	\end{equation*}
	satisfy $V_t^{j_{k}}\stackrel{*}{\rightharpoonup} V_t\in \mathcal{M}_+(\mathbb{R}^n\times G(n,1))$ for $k\to\infty$ with $V_t$ fulfilling all the properties in \cref{AllardRect} $=$ Allard's rectifiability theorem \cite[Sec.\ 5.5, Thm.\ 1]{A72}, in particular $V_t$ is $1$-rectifiable. Note that automatically $\| V_t \|=\mu_t$, see above or \cref{Prop6.4}.
	Further, for a.e.\ $t\in\mathbb{R}_+$, there is a generalized curvature vector field $h_{V_t}\in L^2(\| V_t\|;\mathbb{R}^n)$ with $\delta V_t(g)=-\int g\cdot h_{V_t}\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\| V_t \|$ for all $g\in C_c^1(\mathbb{R}^n;\mathbb{R}^n)$.
	Moreover, we have
	\begin{equation}
		\label[ineq]{in1:timeIntmean}
		\int_{[0,R]}\int |h_{V_t}|^2\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\| V_t \|\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}(t)<\infty
	\end{equation}
	for all $R\in\mathbb{R}_+$.
<!-- end thm-2 -->
<!-- thm-3: body_md -->
\label{MotionLaw}
	Let the setup be as above, see \cref{Prop6.1,Prop6.4}, and $V_t, h_{V_t}$ as in \cref{rectifiability} for a.e.\ $t\in\mathbb{R}_+$. Under \cref{GrowthCondition,IntegralityT0}, the following holds for a.e.\ $(t_1,t_2)\in\mathbb{R}_+^2$ with $t_1<t_2$:
	\begin{multline*}
		\| V_{t_2} \|(\phi(.,t_2))-\| V_{t_1} \|(\phi(.,t_1)) \leq-\int_{[t_1,t_2]}\int |h_{V_t}|^2\phi\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\| V_t\|\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}(t)\\
		+\int_{[t_1,t_2]}\int h_{V_t}(x)\cdot (P^\perp\nabla\phi(x))\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}} V_t(x,P)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}(t)
		+\int_{[t_1,t_2]}\| V_t\|(\partial_t\phi(.,t))\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}(t)
	\end{multline*}
	for all $\phi\in C_c^\infty(\mathbb{R}^n\times\mathbb{R}_+;\mathbb{R}_+)$.
<!-- end thm-3 -->
<!-- defin-2: body_md -->
The space of \textbf{$k$-currents} in $U$, denoted by $\mathcal{D}_k(U)$, is defined as the topological dual space of $\mathcal{D}^k(U)$.
<!-- end defin-2 -->
<!-- defin-3: body_md -->
\label{ProdCurrents}
Let $V\subset\mathbb{R}^m$ and $T_U\in\mathcal{D}_k(U),T_V\in\mathcal{D}_\ell(V)$. Write $p_U:U\times V\to U$ and $p_V:U\times V\to V$ for the natural projections. Then there exists a unique $(k+\ell)$-current $T_U\times T_V\in\mathcal{D}_{k+\ell}(U\times V)$, called the \textbf{cartesian product of $T_U$ and $T_V$}, characterized by the property\footnote{If $W\subset\mathbb{R}^n$ open and $\omega\in\mathcal{D}^p(W),\widetilde{\omega}\in\mathcal{D}^q(W)$, then $\omega\wedge\widetilde{\omega}\in\mathcal{D}^{p+q}(W)$ is defined by $(\omega\wedge\widetilde{\omega})(x)=\omega(x)\wedge\widetilde{\omega}(x)$ for all $x\in W$ \cite[Sec.\ 4.1.6]{F}. Moreover, we have $p_U^\#\omega_1\in\mathcal{D}^i(U\times V)$ with $\langle\xi,(p_U^\#\omega_1)(u,v)\rangle=\langle (\mathrm{D} p_U(u,v))_\#\xi,\omega_1(p_U(u,v)) \rangle=\langle u_1\wedge\ldots\wedge u_i,\omega_1(u)\rangle$ for all $(u,v)\in U\times V$ and $\xi=(u_1,v_1)\wedge\ldots\wedge (u_i,v_i)\in\Lambda_i(\mathbb{R}^n\times\mathbb{R}^m)$ and similar for $p_V^\#\omega_2$ \cite[Secs.\ 1.3.1,1.4.1, 4.1.6]{F}.}
\begin{equation*}
(T_U\times T_V)(p_U^{\#}\omega_1\wedge p_V^{\#}\omega_2)=\begin{cases*}
\langle\omega_1,T_U\rangle\langle\omega_2,T_V\rangle&if $i=k$,\\
0&else,
\end{cases*}
\end{equation*}
for all $\omega_1\in\mathcal{D}^i(U),\omega_2\in\mathcal{D}^{k+\ell-i},i=0,\ldots,k+\ell$.
<!-- end defin-3 -->
<!-- defin-4: body_md -->
\label{CountablyHkrectifiable}
A set $S\subset U$ is called \textbf{countably $\mathcal{H}^k$-rectifiable} if it is $\mathcal{H}^k$-measurable (in the sense of Carath\'eodory) and
\begin{equation*}
	\mathcal{H}^k\left( S\setminus\bigcup f_i(\mathbb{R}^k) \right)=0,
\end{equation*}
where the union is over countably many Lipschitz maps $f_i:\mathbb{R}^k\to U$.
<!-- end defin-4 -->
<!-- defin-5: body_md -->
Let $S\subset U$ be countably $\mathcal{H}^k$-rectifiable and locally $\mathcal{H}^k$-finite and $f\in C_c^1(U;\mathbb{R}^n)$. Then the \textbf{divergence of $f$ relative to $S$} is defined by
	\begin{equation*}
		\textup{div}_S(f(x))=P(x):\mathrm{D} f(x)
	\end{equation*}
	for $\mathcal{H}^k$-a.e.\ $x\in S$, where $P(x)=\sum_{i=1}^{k}e_i\otimes e_i$ for any orthonormal basis $e_1,\ldots,e_k\in \textup{Tan}^k(\mathcal{H}^k\mathbin{\vrule height 1.3ex depth 0pt width 0.13ex\vrule height 0.13ex depth 0pt width 1ex} S,x)$.
<!-- end defin-5 -->
<!-- defin-6: body_md -->
Let $S\subset U$ be countably $\mathcal{H}^k$-rectifiable and locally $\mathcal{H}^k$-finite. If $\theta\in L_{loc}^1(\mathcal{H}^k\mathbin{\vrule height 1.3ex depth 0pt width 0.13ex\vrule height 0.13ex depth 0pt width 1ex} S;\Lambda_k\mathbb{R}^n)$, then $\theta$ is \textbf{tangential to $S$} if 
	\begin{equation*}
		\theta(x)=v_1(x)\wedge\ldots\wedge v_k(x)
	\end{equation*}
	for certain $v_i(x)\in\textup{Tan}^k(\mathcal{H}^k\mathbin{\vrule height 1.3ex depth 0pt width 0.13ex\vrule height 0.13ex depth 0pt width 1ex} S,x)$ for $\mathcal{H}^k$-almost every $x\in S$.
<!-- end defin-6 -->
<!-- defin-7: body_md -->
\label{RectCurrent}
	Let $T\in\mathcal{D}_k(U)$. Then $T$ is a \textbf{(real) rectifiable $k$-current} if there exists a countably $\mathcal{H}^k$-rectifiable and locally $\mathcal{H}^k$-finite set $S\subset U$ and $\theta\in L_{loc}^1(\mathcal{H}^k\mathbin{\vrule height 1.3ex depth 0pt width 0.13ex\vrule height 0.13ex depth 0pt width 1ex} S;\Lambda_k\mathbb{R}^n)$ tangential to $S$ such that $T=\theta\mathcal{H}^k\mathbin{\vrule height 1.3ex depth 0pt width 0.13ex\vrule height 0.13ex depth 0pt width 1ex} S$, that is
	\begin{equation*}
		\langle \omega,T\rangle=\int_S\langle\theta(x),\omega_x\rangle\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{H}^k(x)
	\end{equation*}
	for all $\omega\in\mathcal{D}^k(U)$.
	We write $\mathcal{R}_k(U)$ for the set of rectifiable $k$-currents in $U$.
	If $T=\theta\mathcal{H}^k\mathbin{\vrule height 1.3ex depth 0pt width 0.13ex\vrule height 0.13ex depth 0pt width 1ex} S\in \mathcal{R}_k(U)$ with $|\theta(x)|_{\textup{vol}}\in\mathbb{N}$ for $\mathcal{H}^k$-a.e.\ $x\in S$, then we say that $T$ is an \textbf{integer-multiplicity rectifiable $k$-current}.
<!-- end defin-7 -->
<!-- defin-8: body_md -->
Let $T\in\mathcal{D}_k(U)$. If both $T$ and $\partial T$ are integer-multiplicity rectifiable currents, then $T$ is called an \textbf{integral $k$-current}. We write $\mathcal I_k(U)$ for the set of integral $k$-currents in $U$.
<!-- end defin-8 -->
<!-- defin-9: body_md -->
The subspace of \textbf{normal $k$-currents} $\mathcal{N}_k(U)\subset\mathcal{D}_k(U)$ is given by
\begin{equation*}
	\mathcal{N}_k(U)=\mathcal{D}_k(U)\cap\{\textup{supp}\textup{ compact}\}\cap\{\mathbb{M}+\mathbb{M}\circ\partial<\infty\}.
\end{equation*}
<!-- end defin-9 -->
<!-- defin-10: body_md -->
For compact $K\subset U$ let $\mathbb F_{k,K}(U)$ be the closure of $\mathcal{N}_k(U)\cap\{ \textup{supp}\subset K \}$ with respect to $|\cdot|_K^\flat$. Then the space of \textbf{flat $k$-chains} in $U$ is defined by
\begin{equation*}
\mathbb F_k(U)=\bigcup_{K}\mathbb F_{k,K}(U),
\end{equation*}
where the union is over all compact $K\subset U$.
<!-- end defin-10 -->
<!-- defin-11: body_md -->
A \textbf{transportation network} is any $T=\theta\mathcal{H}^1\mathbin{\vrule height 1.3ex depth 0pt width 0.13ex\vrule height 0.13ex depth 0pt width 1ex} S\in\mathcal{N}_1(\mathbb{R}^n)\cap\mathcal{R}_1(\mathbb{R}^n)$. We write $\mathcal{T}_1(\mathbb{R}^n)$ for the space of transportation networks.
<!-- end defin-11 -->
<!-- rem-1: body_md -->
Let $T\in\mathcal{T}_1(\mathbb{R}^n)$. Since $\mathbb{M}(\partial T)<\infty$, the $0$-current $\partial T$ can be represented by a Radon measure in $\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{R}^n)$ (Riesz representation theorem). The Hahn decomposition theorem implies $\partial T=\mu_+-\mu_-$ with $\mu_-,\mu_+\in\mathcal{M}_+(\mathbb{R}^n)$. By $\partial T(\mathbb{R}^n)=0$ we have $\mu_-(\mathbb{R}^n)=\mu_+(\mathbb{R}^n)$. Therefore, we can interpret $T$ as a \textit{mass flux} from the source $\mu_-$ to the sink $\mu_+$. Two important structure theorems can be found in \cite[Thm.\ 5.5]{Sil} and \cite[Thm.\ C]{S}.
<!-- end rem-1 -->
<!-- defin-12: body_md -->
\label{BTC}
	Let $T=\theta\mathcal{H}^1\mathbin{\vrule height 1.3ex depth 0pt width 0.13ex\vrule height 0.13ex depth 0pt width 1ex} S\in\mathcal R_1(\mathbb{R}^n)$. The \textbf{branched transport cost} of $T$ inside $B\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^n)$ is defined by
	\begin{equation*}
		|T|_\tau(B)=\int_{B\cap S}\tau(|\theta|)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{H}^1\in[0,\infty].
	\end{equation*}
<!-- end defin-12 -->
<!-- defin-13: body_md -->
\label{TauWeight}
	Let $T=\theta\mathcal{H}^1\mathbin{\vrule height 1.3ex depth 0pt width 0.13ex\vrule height 0.13ex depth 0pt width 1ex} S\in \mathcal{R}_1(\mathbb{R}^n)$. We write $|T|_\tau(\phi)=\int_S\phi\tau(|\theta|)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{H}^1$ for $\phi\in C(\mathbb{R}^n;\mathbb{R}_+)$. If $|T|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)<\infty$, then the \textbf{$\tau$-weight measure} $|T|_\tau\in\mathcal{M}_+(\mathbb{R}^n)$ is defined by
	\begin{equation*}
		|T|_\tau(\phi)=\int_S\phi\tau(|\theta|)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{H}^1
	\end{equation*}
	for all $\phi\in C_c(\mathbb{R}^n)$. If $\tau(m)=m$, then we write $|T|(\mathbb{R}^n)=|T|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)$ respectively $|T|(\phi)=|T|_\tau(\phi)$ if $|T|(\mathbb{R}^n)<\infty$ and $\phi\in C_c(\mathbb{R}^n)$, which just indicates the usual (total) variation.
<!-- end defin-13 -->
<!-- defin-14: body_md -->
Let $X,Y$ be normed vector spaces, $\mu$ outer measure on $X$, and $A\subset X$. A map $f:A\to Y$ is called \textbf{$(\mu,k)$-approximately differentiable at $x\in A$} if there exist $r\in\mathbb{R}_+^*$ and map $g:B_r(x)\to Y$ such that $g$ is Fr\'echet differentiable in $x$ and $\Theta^k(\mu\mathbin{\vrule height 1.3ex depth 0pt width 0.13ex\vrule height 0.13ex depth 0pt width 1ex} (B_r(x)\cap\{ f\neq g \}),x)=0$. If $f$ is $(\mu,k)$-approximately differentiable at $x$, then we write
\begin{equation*}
	(\mu,k)\text{-}\mathrm{ap\,D} f(x)=\mathrm{D} g(x)\bigg|_{\textup{Tan}^k(\mu,x)}.
\end{equation*}
<!-- end defin-14 -->
<!-- prop-1: body_md -->
\label{FedererPushforward}
	Let $T=\theta\mathcal{H}^1\mathbin{\vrule height 1.3ex depth 0pt width 0.13ex\vrule height 0.13ex depth 0pt width 1ex} S\in \mathcal{R}_1(\mathbb{R}^n)$ be compactly supported. Further, suppose that $f:\mathbb{R}^n\to\mathbb{R}^n$ is locally Lipschitz. Then $f_\# T=\eta\mathcal{H}^1\mathbin{\vrule height 1.3ex depth 0pt width 0.13ex\vrule height 0.13ex depth 0pt width 1ex} f(S)\in \mathcal{R}_1(\mathbb{R}^n)$. In particular, if $T\in\mathcal{T}_1(\mathbb{R}^n)$, then $f_\# T\in\mathcal{T}_1(\mathbb{R}^n)$.
	More specifically, the following is satisfied.
	\begin{enumerate}
		\item For all $\omega\in\mathcal{D}^1(\mathbb{R}^n)$ we have
		\begin{equation*}
			\langle\omega, f_\# T\rangle=\int_S\langle \mathrm{ap\,D}(f|_S)(x)\theta(x),\omega_{f(x)}\rangle\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{H}^1(x).
		\end{equation*}
		\item There exist representatives $\bar\theta,\bar\eta$ such that\footnote{Summation takes into account that disconnected transportation paths in $T$ can be joined through application of $f_\#$. }
		\begin{equation*}
			\bar\eta(y)=\sum_{x\in(f|_S)^{-1}(y)}\mathrm{ap\,D}(f|_S)(x)\bar\theta(x)/\mathrm{ap\,J}(f|_S)(x)
		\end{equation*}
		for $\mathcal{H}^1$-a.e.\ $y\in f(S)$. Further, we have\footnote{Divisions by $\mathrm{ap\,J}(f|_S)(x)$ ensure that $\bar\eta(y)$ has correct multiplicity $\sum_{x\in(f|_S)^{-1}(y)}(\pm)_x|\bar\theta(x)|$, where signs $(\pm)_x$ model possible cancellations due to opposite orientation.}
		\begin{equation*}
			\mathrm{ap\,D}(f|_S)(x)\bar\theta(x)/\mathrm{ap\,J}(f|_S)(x)=\pm|\bar\theta(x)|\bar\eta(y)/|\bar\eta(y)|
		\end{equation*}
		if $x\in(f|_S)^{-1}(y)$ and $\bar\eta(y)\neq 0$ for $\mathcal{H}^1$-a.e.\ $y\in f(S)$.
	\end{enumerate}
<!-- end prop-1 -->
<!-- coro-1: body_md -->
\label{EnergyPushforward}
	Let $T\in\mathcal{T}_1(\mathbb{R}^n)$. Consider a vector field $g\in C_c^1(\mathbb{R}^n;\mathbb{R}^n)$ and set $f_t(x)=x+tg(x)$ for $t\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{ 0\}$ and $x\in\mathbb{R}^n$.
	Then, for $|t|$ sufficiently small, we have
	\begin{equation*}
		|(f_t)_\# T|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)=\int_S\tau(|\theta|)\mathrm{J} f_t\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{H}^1.
	\end{equation*}
	In particular, if $|T|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)<\infty$, then
	\begin{equation*}
		\partial_t|(f_t)_\# T|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)\bigg|_{t=0}=\int_S\tau(|\theta|)\textup{div}_S(g)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{H}^1.
	\end{equation*}
<!-- end coro-1 -->
<!-- proof-1: body_md -->
Note that $f_t$ is proper and $\textup{det}(\mathrm{D} f_t)\neq 0$ for $|t|$ sufficiently small, hence $f_t$ is a diffeomorphism for $|t|$ sufficiently small (Hadamard\textendash Cacciopoli theorem).
	By the first bullet point in \cref{FedererPushforward}, the area formula \cite[Cor.\ 3.2.20]{F}, and the second bullet point in \cref{FedererPushforward} we get
	\begin{equation*}
	|(f_t)_\# T|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)=\int_{f_t(S)}\tau(|\eta|)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{H}^1=\int_S\tau(|\eta(f_t(x))|)\mathrm{J} f_t\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{H}^1=\int_S\tau(|\theta|)\mathrm{J} f_t\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{H}^1,
	\end{equation*}
	which shows the first statement.
	We have $\mathrm{J} f_t=|e+t\mathrm{D}ge|$, where $e(x)\in T_xS$ for $\mathcal{H}^1$-a.e.\ $x\in S$. Thus
	\begin{equation*}
		\partial_t\mathrm{J} f_t\bigg|_{t=0}=\left(\frac{(e+t\mathrm{D}ge)^\intercal}{|e+t\mathrm{D}ge|}\mathrm{D}ge\right)\bigg|_{t=0}=\textup{div}_S(g)
	\end{equation*}
	$\mathcal{H}^1$-a.e.\ on $ S$.
	The second statement follows from the Leibniz integral rule:
	Since $|T|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)<\infty$ and $\mathrm{J} f_t$ is bounded, we have $\tau(|\theta|)|e+t\mathrm{D}ge|\in L^1(\mathcal{H}^1\mathbin{\vrule height 1.3ex depth 0pt width 0.13ex\vrule height 0.13ex depth 0pt width 1ex} S;\mathbb{R})$ for all $t\in\mathbb{R}$.
	Further, for $|t|$ sufficiently small, we get $|\partial_t\tau(|\theta|)\mathrm{J} f_t|\leq \tau(|\theta|)(1+|\mathrm{D}g |_2)\in L^1(\mathcal{H}^1\mathbin{\vrule height 1.3ex depth 0pt width 0.13ex\vrule height 0.13ex depth 0pt width 1ex} S;\mathbb{R})$. Therefore
	\begin{equation*}
		\partial_t|(f_t)_\# T|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)\bigg|_{t=0}=\int_S\partial_t(\tau(|\theta|)\mathrm{J} f_t)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{H}^1\bigg|_{t=0}=\int_S\tau(|\theta|)\textup{div}_S(g)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{H}^1.\qedhere
	\end{equation*}
<!-- end proof-1 -->
<!-- defin-15: body_md -->
\label{TauVariation}
	Let $T\in\mathcal{T}_1(\mathbb{R}^n)$ with $|T|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)<\infty$. The \textbf{$\tau$-variation} $\delta_\tau T$ of $T$ is defined by
	\begin{equation*}
		\delta_\tau T(g)=\int_S\textup{div}_S(g)\tau(|\theta|)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{H}^1
	\end{equation*}
	for all $g\in C_c^1(\mathbb{R}^n;\mathbb{R}^n)$.
<!-- end defin-15 -->
<!-- defin-16: body_md -->
\label{phiWeightedTauVariation}
	Let $T=\theta\mathcal{H}^1\mathbin{\vrule height 1.3ex depth 0pt width 0.13ex\vrule height 0.13ex depth 0pt width 1ex} S\in\mathcal{R}_1(\mathbb{R}^n)$ with $|T|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)<\infty$ and $\phi\in C^1(\mathbb{R}^n;\mathbb{R}_+)$. The \textbf{$\phi$-weighted $\tau$-variation} of $T$ is defined by
	\begin{equation*}
		\delta_\tau (T,\phi)(g)=\int_S(\phi\textup{div}_S(g)+\nabla\phi\cdot g)\tau(|\theta|)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{H}^1
	\end{equation*}
	for all $g\in C_c^1(\mathbb{R}^n;\mathbb{R}^n)$.
<!-- end defin-16 -->
<!-- defin-17: body_md -->
\label{Smoothed}
	Let $T=\theta\mathcal{H}^1\mathbin{\vrule height 1.3ex depth 0pt width 0.13ex\vrule height 0.13ex depth 0pt width 1ex} S\in\mathcal{R}_1(\mathbb{R}^n)$ with $|T|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)<\infty$ and $\Psi\in C_c^\infty(\mathbb{R}^n)$. Define
	\begin{equation*}
		(\Psi\ast\delta_\tau T)(y)=\int_ST_xS\nabla\Psi(x-y)\tau(|\theta(x)|)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{H}^1(x)\qquad\textup{and}\qquad (\Psi\ast |T|_\tau)(y)=\int\Psi(x-y)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}|T|_\tau(x)
	\end{equation*}
	for all $y\in\mathbb{R}^n$.
<!-- end defin-17 -->
<!-- defin-18: body_md -->
A \textbf{$k$-varifold} in $U$ is any $[0,\infty]$-valued Radon measure on $\mathcal G_k(U)$. We write $\mathcal V_k(U)$ for the set of $k$-varifolds in $U$.
The \textbf{weight measure} $\| V\|:\mathcal{B}(U)\to[0,\infty]$ of $V\in\mathcal V_k(U)$ is defined by
\begin{equation*}
	\langle \phi, \| V\|\rangle =\int \phi(x)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}} V(x,P)
\end{equation*}
for all $\phi\in C_c(U)$.
<!-- end defin-18 -->
<!-- defin-19: body_md -->
\label{inducedV}
Let $T=\theta\mathcal{H}^k\mathbin{\vrule height 1.3ex depth 0pt width 0.13ex\vrule height 0.13ex depth 0pt width 1ex} S\in\mathcal R_k(U)$. Then the \textbf{induced varifold} $V=v(|\theta|, S)\in \mathcal V_k(U)$ by $T$ is given by
\begin{equation*}
\langle\phi,V\rangle=\int_S \phi(x,\textup{Tan}^k(\mathcal{H}^k\mathbin{\vrule height 1.3ex depth 0pt width 0.13ex\vrule height 0.13ex depth 0pt width 1ex} S,x))|\theta(x)|\mathcal{H}^k(x)
\end{equation*}
for all $\phi\in C_c(\mathcal G_k(U))$.
<!-- end defin-19 -->
<!-- notat-1: body_md -->
We set
	\begin{equation*}
		\mathcal F_j^+=\{ \phi\in C^2(\mathbb{R}^n;[0,1])\:|\:|\nabla\phi|\leq j\phi\textup{ and }|\nabla^2\phi|_2\leq j\phi\textup{ in }\mathbb{R}^n \}
	\end{equation*}
	and
	\begin{equation*}
		\mathcal F_j^n=\{ g\in C^2(\mathbb{R}^n;\mathbb{R}^n)\:|\:|g|\leq j,|\mathrm{D} g|_2\leq j,|\mathrm{D}^2g|_{2,2}\leq j\textup{, and }|g|_{L^2}\leq j \},
	\end{equation*}
	where $|g|_{L^2}=\left(\int|g|^2\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n\right)^{1/2}$.
<!-- end notat-1 -->
<!-- lem-1: body_md -->
\label{propertiesF}
	Let $\phi\in\mathcal F_j^+$. Then
	\begin{multline*}
		\phi(x)\leq\phi(y)e^{j|x-y|},\qquad|\phi(x)-\phi(y)|\leq j|x-y|\phi(x)e^{j|x-y|},\qquad\textup{and}\\|\phi(x)-\phi(y)-\nabla\phi(y)\cdot(x-y)|\leq j|x-y|^2\phi(y)e^{j|x-y|}
	\end{multline*}
	for all $x,y\in\mathbb{R}^n$.
<!-- end lem-1 -->
<!-- lem-2: body_md -->
\label{propertiesFn}
	Let $g\in\mathcal F_j^n$. Then
	\begin{equation*}
		|\mathrm{D} g(x)-\mathrm{D} g(y)|_2\leq j|x-y|
	\end{equation*}
	for all $x,y\in\mathbb{R}^n$.
<!-- end lem-2 -->
<!-- proof-2: body_md -->
We have (recall that $(Mx)_{i,j}=\sum_kM_{i,j,k}x_k$ for all $M\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n\times n}$)
	\begin{equation*}
		\mathrm{D} g(x)-\mathrm{D} g(y)=\int_{[0,1]}\partial_t(\mathrm{D} g(y+t(x-y)))\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}(t)=\int_{[0,1]}\mathrm{D}^2g(y+t(x-y))(x-y)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}(t). 
	\end{equation*}
	Using this one easily estimates
	\begin{equation*}
		|\mathrm{D} g(x)-\mathrm{D} g(y)|_2\leq\int_{[0,1]}|\mathrm{D}^2g(y+t(x-y))|_{2,2}\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}(t)|x-y|\leq j|x-y|
	\end{equation*}
	because $|\mathrm{D}^2g|_{2,2}\leq j$.
<!-- end proof-2 -->
<!-- defin-20: body_md -->
\label{AdmDeform}
	Fix a transportation network $T\in\mathcal{T}_1(\mathbb{R}^n)$ and $j\in\mathbb{N}$. Assume that $|T|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)<\infty$. Then we write $\mathcal{A}_j(T)$ for the set of \textbf{admissible deformations} $f:\mathbb{R}^n\to\mathbb{R}^n$ which are required to satisfy (recall \cref{ProdCurrents})
	\begin{itemize}
		\item $f$ locally Lipschitz with $|f-\textup{id}|_\infty\leq j^{-2}$,
		\item $\mathbb{M}\left((h_f)_\#(\llbracket 0,1\rrbracket\times T)\right)\leq j^{-1}(|T|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)-|f_\# T|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n))$ and $|f_\# T|_\tau(\phi)\leq|T|_\tau(\phi)$ for all $\phi\in \mathcal F_j^+$.
	\end{itemize}
	If $B\subset \mathbb{R}^n$ is closed, then we also write
	\begin{equation*}
		\Delta_j|T|_\tau(B)=\inf_f|f_\#T|_\tau(B)-|T|_\tau(B)\in\mathbb{R}_-,
	\end{equation*}
	where the infimum is over $f$ in
	\begin{equation*}
		\mathcal{A}_j(B,T)=\{ f\in\mathcal{A}_j(T)\:|\:\{ x\:|\:f(x)\neq x \}\cup\{ f(x)\:|\:f(x)\neq x \}\subset B\}.
	\end{equation*}
<!-- end defin-20 -->
<!-- examp-1: body_md -->
\label{RelocTriple}
Let $j\in\mathbb N$, $r=2j^{-2}$, and $x_k=r(\cos(\alpha_k),\sin(\alpha_k))$ for $\alpha_k=2k\pi/3$ and $k=1,2$. 
Further, set $y=(r,0)$ and $\tilde y=(r/2,0)$.
Now define $K=\textup{conv}(\{ x_1,x_2,y \})$ and $\widetilde K=\textup{conv}(\{ x_1,x_2,\tilde y \})$.
Note that $\widetilde K\subset K$.
Assume that $T\in\mathcal{T}_1(\mathbb{R}^n)$ is given by $T=\vec{e}_1\mathcal{H}^1\mathbin{\vrule height 1.3ex depth 0pt width 0.13ex\vrule height 0.13ex depth 0pt width 1ex} e_1+\vec{e}_2\mathcal{H}^1\mathbin{\vrule height 1.3ex depth 0pt width 0.13ex\vrule height 0.13ex depth 0pt width 1ex} e_2$, where $e_i=[x_i,y]$ and $\vec{e}_i=(y-x_i)/|y-x_i|$ for $i=1,2$.
See \cref{fig2} for a sketch.
It may also be seen as the local picture of a transportation network inside a closed ball of radius $r$.
Let the transportation cost $\tau$ be given by $\tau(m)=m^\alpha$ for some $\alpha\in(0,\log_2(4\sqrt{3}-2\sqrt{7}))$.
We will define a locally Lipschitz map $f:\mathbb{R}^n\to\mathbb{R}^n$ such that $f\in\mathcal A_j(T)$ for $j$ sufficiently large.
It is enough to specify the definition of $f$ on $A=\textup{clos}(K\setminus \widetilde K)$ since $\textup{supp}(T)\subset A$.
Let $f|_A$ be given as the orthogonal projection onto $\widetilde K$.
Then we may extend $f$ to locally Lipschitz $f:\mathbb{R}^n\to\mathbb{R}^n$ such that $|f-\textup{id}|_\infty\leq j^{-2}$.
The homotopy formula for currents \cite[p.\ 363]{F} implies
\begin{equation*}
f_\# T-T=\partial (h_f)_\#(\llbracket 0,1\rrbracket\times T)+(h_f)_\#(\llbracket 0,1\rrbracket\times \partial T).
\end{equation*}
In \cref{fig2}, the current $(h_f)_\#(\llbracket 0,1\rrbracket\times T)$ is highlighted in gray.
Note that its boundary contains the term $-(h_f)_\#(\llbracket 0,1\rrbracket\times \partial T)$ by definition of $f$.
Next, let us show that $f$ is admissible for $j$ sufficiently large.
The mass of $(h_f)_\#(\llbracket 0,1\rrbracket\times T)$ can be calculated as the area of the parallelogram induced by $[\tilde y,y]$ and $[x_1,\tilde y]$,
\begin{equation*}
\mathbb{M}\left((h_f)_\#(\llbracket 0,1\rrbracket\times T)\right)=|\tilde y-y|r\sin\left( \frac{2\pi}{3} \right)=j^{-4}\sqrt{3}.
\end{equation*}
Moreover, we have 
\begin{multline*}
|T|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)=2\tau(1)\mathcal{H}^1([x_1,y])=j^{-2}4\sqrt{3}\qquad\textup{and}\qquad |f_\#T|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)=2\tau(1)\mathcal{H}^1([x_1,\tilde y])+\tau(2)\mathcal{H}^1([\tilde y,y])\\
=j^{-2}4\frac{\sqrt{7}}{2}+j^{-2}4\frac{2^\alpha}{4}.
\end{multline*}
Thus, the inequality
\begin{equation*}
\mathbb{M}\left((h_f)_\#(\llbracket 0,1\rrbracket\times T)\right)\leq j^{-1}(|T|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)-|f_\# T|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n))\qquad\Leftrightarrow\qquad j^{-1}\frac{\sqrt{3}}{4}\leq \sqrt{3}-\frac{\sqrt{7}}{2}-\frac{2^\alpha}{4}
\end{equation*}
is true for $j$ sufficiently large---parameter $\alpha<\log_2(4\sqrt{3}-2\sqrt{7})$ ensures that the right-hand side is positive, otherwise $f_\#$ would increase the branched transport cost.
The triple junction $\tilde y$ is optimally placed if 
\begin{equation*}
\frac{\tilde y-x_1}{|\tilde y-x_1|}+\frac{\tilde y-x_2}{|\tilde y-x_2|}=2^\alpha\frac{y-\tilde y}{|y-\tilde y|}.
\end{equation*}
This follows from the well-known ``momentum conservation law'' \cite[p.\ 255]{X}.
This equality is satisfied for $\alpha=\log_2(4/\sqrt{7})$.
Otherwise, we have
\begin{equation*}
\begin{cases*}
\textup{the optimal triple junction lies on $(0,\tilde y)$}&if $\alpha<\log_2(4/\sqrt{7})$,\\
\textup{the optimal triple junction lies on $(\tilde y,y)$}&if $\alpha\in(\log_2(4/\sqrt{7}),\log_2(4\sqrt{3}-2\sqrt{7}))$,\\
\textup{the junction $y$ is already optimal (locally)}&if $\alpha\in[\log_2(4\sqrt{3}-2\sqrt{7}),1)$.
\end{cases*}
\end{equation*}
In the limit case $\alpha=0$ respectively $\tau(m)=1_{\mathbb{R}_+^*}(m)$, the optimal triple junction would be equal to the centre of the circle (Steiner tree).
Finally, we need to show $|f_\# T|_\tau(\phi)\leq|T|_\tau(\phi)$ for all $\phi\in \mathcal F_j^+$.
We use a trick similar to \cite[Sec.\ 4.10]{B78}, see also \cite[Lem.\ 4.12]{KT17},
\begin{equation*}
|f_\# T|_\tau(\phi)-|T|_\tau(\phi)\leq \max_A\phi|f_\# T|_\tau(1_A)-\min_A\phi |T|_\tau(1_A)\leq \min_A\phi e^{1/j}|f_\# T|_\tau(1_A)-\min_A\phi |T|_\tau(1_A)\leq 0
\end{equation*}
for $j$ sufficiently large (independent of $\phi$), where \cref{propertiesF} was used in the second inequality.
This shows $f\in\mathcal A_j(T)$ for $\alpha\in (0,\log_2(4\sqrt{3}-2\sqrt{7}))$ and $j$ sufficiently large.
<!-- end examp-1 -->
<!-- lem-3: body_md -->
\label{EstimatesNabla}
	There exists $c\in\mathbb{R}_+^*$ such that
	\begin{equation*}
		|\nabla\Psi_\varepsilon(x)|\leq\frac{|x|}{\varepsilon^2}\Psi_\varepsilon(x)+c 1_{\overline B_1(0)\setminus\overline B_{1/2}(0)}(x)e^{-1/\varepsilon}
	\end{equation*}
	and
	\begin{equation*}
		|\nabla^2\Psi_\varepsilon(x)|_2\leq\frac{|x|^2}{\varepsilon^4}\Psi_\varepsilon(x)+\frac{c}{\varepsilon^2}\Psi_\varepsilon(x)+c 1_{\overline B_1(0)\setminus\overline B_{1/2}(0)}(x)e^{-1/\varepsilon}
	\end{equation*}
	for all $x\in\mathbb{R}^n$ for $\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}_+^*$ sufficiently small.
<!-- end lem-3 -->
<!-- defin-21: body_md -->
\label{ApproxCurveVF}
	Let $\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}_+^*$ and $T=\theta\mathcal{H}^1\mathbin{\vrule height 1.3ex depth 0pt width 0.13ex\vrule height 0.13ex depth 0pt width 1ex} S\in\mathcal{R}_1(\mathbb{R}^n)$ with $|T|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)<\infty$. We write
	\begin{equation*}
		h_{\tau,\varepsilon}(T)=-\Psi_\varepsilon\ast\left( \frac{\Psi_\varepsilon\ast\delta_\tau T}{\Psi_\varepsilon\ast|T|_\tau+\varepsilon} \right)\in C^\infty(\mathbb{R}^n;\mathbb{R}^n)
	\end{equation*}
	and
	\begin{equation*}
		\tilde h_{\tau,\varepsilon}(T)=- \frac{\Psi_\varepsilon\ast\delta_\tau T}{\Psi_\varepsilon\ast|T|_\tau+\varepsilon} \in C^\infty(\mathbb{R}^n;\mathbb{R}^n).
	\end{equation*}
<!-- end defin-21 -->
<!-- lem-4: body_md -->
\label{hEstimates}
	Let $M\in\mathbb{R}_+^*$. Then there exists $\varepsilon_1=\varepsilon_1(M,n)\in(0,1)$ such that
	\begin{equation*}
		|\tilde h_{\tau,\varepsilon}(T)|\leq 2\varepsilon^{-2},\quad |h_{\tau,\varepsilon}(T)|\leq 2\varepsilon^{-2},\quad |\mathrm{D} h_{\tau,\varepsilon}(T)|_2\leq 4\varepsilon^{-4},\quad\textup{and}\quad|\mathrm{D}^2 h_{\tau,\varepsilon}(T)|_{2,2}\leq 4\varepsilon^{-6}
	\end{equation*}
	for all $\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_1)$ and $T\in\mathcal{R}_1(\mathbb{R}^n)$ with $|T|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)\leq M$.
<!-- end lem-4 -->
<!-- lem-5: body_md -->
\label{intBall}
	Let $T\in\mathcal{R}_1(\mathbb{R}^n)$ with $|T|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)<\infty$. Then
	\begin{equation*}
		\int |T|_\tau(\overline{B}_r(x))\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n(x)=\omega_nr^n|T|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)
	\end{equation*}
	for all $r\in\mathbb{R}_+^*$.
<!-- end lem-5 -->
<!-- proof-3: body_md -->
Using Fubini's theorem and $1_{\overline{B}_r(x)}(y)=1_{\overline{B}_r(y)}(x)$, we get
\begin{equation*}
\int |T|_\tau(\overline{B}_r(x))\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n(x)=\int\int 1_{\overline{B}_r(x)}(y)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n(x)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}|T|_\tau(y)=\omega_nr^n|T|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n).\qedhere
\end{equation*}
<!-- end proof-3 -->
<!-- prop-2: body_md -->
\label{Prop5.3}
	Let $M\in\mathbb{R}_+^*$. Then, for $\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}_+^*$ sufficiently small (depending on $M,n$), we have
	\begin{equation*}
		\left|\int h_{\tau,\varepsilon}(T)\cdot g\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}|T|_\tau+\int(\Psi_\varepsilon\ast\delta_\tau T)\cdot g\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n\right|\leq\varepsilon^{1/4}\left(\int\frac{|\Psi_\varepsilon\ast\delta_\tau T|^2}{\Psi_\varepsilon\ast|T|_\tau+\varepsilon}\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n\right)^{1/2}
	\end{equation*}
	for all $j\leq 2^{-1}\varepsilon^{-1/6},g\in\mathcal F_j^n$, and $T\in \mathcal{R}_1(\mathbb{R}^n)$ compactly supported with $|T|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)\leq M$.
<!-- end prop-2 -->
<!-- proof-4: body_md -->
Abbreviate $h=h_{\tau,\varepsilon}(T),\tilde h=\tilde h_{\tau,\varepsilon}(T)$, and 
	\begin{equation*}
		I=\int\frac{|\Psi_\varepsilon\ast\delta_\tau T|^2}{\Psi_\varepsilon\ast|T|_\tau+\varepsilon}\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n.
	\end{equation*}
	By a calculation similar to \cite[(5.10) \&\ (5.11)]{KT17} we get the analogue of \cite[(5.12)]{KT17},
	\begin{multline}
		\label[ineq]{in1:a}
		\left|\int h_{\tau,\varepsilon}\cdot g\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}|T|_\tau+\int(\Psi_\varepsilon\ast\delta_\tau T)\cdot g\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n\right|\\
		\leq \underbrace{\int|g||\tilde{h}|\varepsilon\mathcal{L}^n}_{I_1=}+\underbrace{\int|\tilde{h}(y)|\left|\int\Psi_\varepsilon(x-y)g(x)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}|T|_\tau(x)-(\Psi_\varepsilon\ast|T|_\tau)(y)g(y)\right|\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n(y)}_{I_2=}.
	\end{multline}
	We also get inequalities corresponding to \cite[(5.13) \&\ (5.14)]{KT17} by using the definition of $\mathcal F_j^n$,
	\begin{equation}
		\label[ineq]{in1:b}
		I_1\leq j\varepsilon|\tilde h|_{L^2}\leq j\varepsilon^{1/2}I^{1/2}.
	\end{equation}
	Instead of \cite[Lem.\ 4.7]{KT17}, we observe that $g$ is $j$-Lipschitz. Using this and the definition of $\Psi_\varepsilon$, we get the analogue of \cite[(5.15)]{KT17},
	\begin{equation}
		\label[ineq]{in1:B}
		\left|\int\Psi_\varepsilon(x-y)g(x)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}|T|_\tau(x)-(\Psi_\varepsilon\ast|T|_\tau)(y)g(y)\right|\leq j\int_{\overline{B}_1(y)}|x-y|\Psi_\varepsilon(x-y)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}|T|_\tau(x).
	\end{equation}
	In \cite[(5.16)]{KT17} we need to replace $n+1$ by $n$,
	\begin{equation}
		\label[ineq]{in1:B2}
		\sup_{\overline{B}_1(y)\setminus\overline{B}_{\sqrt \varepsilon}(y)}|x-y|\Psi_\varepsilon(x-y)\leq c(n)\varepsilon^{-n}e^{-1/(2\varepsilon)}=c_{\varepsilon,n}.
	\end{equation}
	Combining \cref{in1:B,in1:B2}, we obtain
	\begin{equation}
		\label[ineq]{in1:c}
		I_2\leq\underbrace{j\varepsilon^{1/2}\int|\tilde h|(\Psi_\varepsilon\ast|T|_\tau)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n}_{I_{2,1}=}+\underbrace{jc_{\varepsilon,n}\int|\tilde{h}(y)||T|_\tau(\overline{B}_1(y))\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n(y)}_{I_{2,2}=}.
	\end{equation}
	Note that $(\Psi_\varepsilon\ast|T|_\tau)(\mathbb{R}^n)=|T|_\tau(\Psi_\varepsilon\ast 1)=|T|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)$. Using this and Hölder's inequality, one obtains (see \cite[(5.18) \&\ (5.19)]{KT17})
	\begin{equation}
		\label[ineq]{in1:d}
		I_{2,1}\leq j\varepsilon^{1/2}\left( \int|\tilde{h}|^2(\Psi_\varepsilon\ast |T|_\tau)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n \right)^{1/2}(\Psi_\varepsilon\ast|T|_\tau)(\mathbb{R}^n)^{1/2}\leq j\varepsilon^{1/2}I^{1/2}M^{1/2}.
	\end{equation}
	Using Hölder's inequality again, we can estimate (see \cite[(5.20) \&\ (5.21)]{KT17})
	\begin{equation*}
		I_{2,2}\leq jc_{\varepsilon,n} I^{1/2}\varepsilon^{-1/2}M^{1/2}\left(\int|T|_\tau(\overline{B}_1(x))\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n(x)\right)^{1/2}.
	\end{equation*}
	Now recall that $\int|T|_\tau(\overline{B}_1(x))\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n(x)=\omega_n|T|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)\leq \omega_nM$ (\cref{intBall}), which yields
	\begin{equation}
		\label[ineq]{in1:e}
		I_{2,2}\leq jc_{\varepsilon,n} \varepsilon^{-1/2}M\omega_n^{1/2}I^{1/2}.
	\end{equation}
	Now combine \cref{in1:a,in1:b,in1:c,in1:d,in1:e} and use the assumption $j\leq 2^{-1}\varepsilon^{-1/6}$,
	\begin{multline*}
		\left|\int h_{\tau,\varepsilon}\cdot g\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}|T|_\tau+\int(\Psi_\varepsilon\ast\delta_\tau T)\cdot g\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n\right|\leq I_1+I_{2,1}+I_{2,2}\\
		\leq j\varepsilon^{1/2}I^{1/2}+j\varepsilon^{1/2}I^{1/2}M^{1/2}+jc_{\varepsilon,n}\varepsilon^{-1/2}M\omega_n^{1/2}I^{1/2}
		\leq 2^{-1}\varepsilon^{1/3}(1+M^{1/2}+c_{\varepsilon,n}\varepsilon^{-1}M\omega_n^{1/2})I^{1/2}\\
		\leq \varepsilon^{1/4}I^{1/2}
	\end{multline*}
	for $\varepsilon$ sufficiently small because $c_{\varepsilon,n}\varepsilon^{-1}\to 0$ for $\varepsilon\to 0$.
<!-- end proof-4 -->
<!-- prop-3: body_md -->
\label{Prop5.5}
	Let $M\in\mathbb{R}_+^*$. Then, for $\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}_+^*$ sufficiently small (depending on $M,n$), we have
	\begin{equation*}
		\left|\int h_{\tau,\varepsilon}(T)\cdot g\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}|T|_\tau+\partial_\tau T(g)\right|\leq\varepsilon^{1/4}+\varepsilon^{1/4}\left(\int\frac{|\Psi_\varepsilon\ast\delta_\tau T|^2}{\Psi_\varepsilon\ast|T|_\tau+\varepsilon}\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n\right)^{1/2}
	\end{equation*}
	for all $j\leq 2^{-1}\varepsilon^{-1/6},g\in\mathcal F_j^n$, and $T\in \mathcal{R}_1(\mathbb{R}^n)$ compactly supported with $|T|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)\leq M$. (We write $\delta_\tau T(g)=\int_S\tau(|\theta|)\textup{div}_S(g)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{H}^1$, which is well-defined because $|\mathrm{D} g|_2\leq j$.)
<!-- end prop-3 -->
<!-- proof-5: body_md -->
Instead of \cite[(5.49)]{KT17}, we have
	\begin{equation*}
		\left|\int (\Psi_\varepsilon\ast\delta_\tau T)\cdot g\mathcal{L}^n-\partial_\tau T(g)\right|\leq c(n) j\int\int\Psi_\varepsilon(x-y)|x-y|\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n(y)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}|T|_\tau(x)
	\end{equation*}
	using \cref{TauVariation,propertiesFn}. An estimate similar to \cref{in1:c} yields
	\begin{equation*}
		j\int\int\Psi_\varepsilon(x-y)|x-y|\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n(y)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}|T|_\tau(x)
		\leq jc_{\varepsilon,n}\int|T|_\tau(\overline{B}_1(y))\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n(y)+j\varepsilon^{1/2}\int(\Psi_\varepsilon\ast |T|_\tau)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n.
	\end{equation*}
	Note that $c_{\varepsilon,n}\leq\varepsilon^{1/2}$ for $\varepsilon$ sufficiently small, thus
	\begin{equation*}
		jc_{\varepsilon,n}\int|T|_\tau(\overline{B}_1(y))\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n(y)+j\varepsilon^{1/2}\int(\Psi_\varepsilon\ast |T|_\tau)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n\leq c(n) j\varepsilon^{1/2}|T|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n),
	\end{equation*}
	where we also used \cref{intBall} and $\int(\Psi_\varepsilon\ast |T|_\tau)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n=|T|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)$. We have shown
	\begin{equation*}
		\left|\int (\Psi_\varepsilon\ast\delta_\tau T)\cdot g\mathcal{L}^n-\partial_\tau T(g)\right|\leq c(n) j\varepsilon^{1/2}|T|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n).
	\end{equation*}
	Combining this with \cref{Prop5.3}, $j\leq 2^{-1}\varepsilon^{-1/6}$, and $|T|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)\leq M$ gives
	\begin{multline*}
		\left|\int h_{\tau,\varepsilon}(T)\cdot g\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}|T|_\tau+\partial_\tau T(g)\right|\\
		\leq \left|\int h_{\tau,\varepsilon}(T)\cdot g\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}|T|_\tau+\int (\Psi_\varepsilon\ast\delta_\tau T)\cdot g\mathcal{L}^n\right|+\left|-\int (\Psi_\varepsilon\ast\delta_\tau T)\cdot g\mathcal{L}^n+\partial_\tau T(g)\right|\\
		\leq c(n) \varepsilon^{1/4}\left(\int\frac{|\Psi_\varepsilon\ast\delta_\tau T|^2}{\Psi_\varepsilon\ast|T|_\tau+\varepsilon}\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n\right)^{1/2}+2^{-1}\varepsilon^{-1/6}\varepsilon^{1/2}M.
	\end{multline*}
	Now use that $2^{-1}\varepsilon^{-1/6}\varepsilon^{1/2}M=2^{-1}M\varepsilon^{1/3}\leq\varepsilon^{1/4}$ for $\varepsilon$ sufficiently small.
<!-- end proof-5 -->
<!-- lem-6: body_md -->
\label{FormulaPsi}
	We have 
	\begin{equation*}
		x\Psi_\varepsilon(x)+\varepsilon^2\nabla\Psi_\varepsilon(x)=\varepsilon^2c_\varepsilon\nabla\psi(x)\widehat{\Psi}_\varepsilon(x).
	\end{equation*}
<!-- end lem-6 -->
<!-- lem-7: body_md -->
\label{AuxEst}
	We have (see \cite[(5.6)]{KT17})
	\begin{equation*}
		|(\Psi_\varepsilon\ast\delta_\tau T)(x)|\leq\varepsilon^{-2}(\Psi_\varepsilon\ast|T|_\tau)(x)+ce^{-1/\varepsilon}|T|_\tau(\overline{B}_1(x)),
	\end{equation*}
	where $c$ is the constant from \cref{EstimatesNabla}. Moreover,
	\begin{equation*}
		(\Psi_\varepsilon\ast|T|_\tau)(x)\leq c(\varepsilon,n)|T|_\tau(\overline{B}_1(x)).
	\end{equation*}
<!-- end lem-7 -->
<!-- prop-4: body_md -->
\label{LongProof}
	Let $M\in\mathbb{R}_+$. Then, for $\varepsilon$ sufficiently small (depending on $M,n$), we have
	\begin{equation}
		\label[ineq]{in1:5.23}
		\left| \delta_\tau T(\phi h_{\tau,\varepsilon}(T))+\int\frac{\phi|\Psi_\varepsilon\ast\delta_\tau T|^2}{\Psi_\varepsilon\ast|T|_\tau+\varepsilon}\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n \right|\leq \varepsilon^{1/4}\left(\int\frac{\phi|\Psi_\varepsilon\ast\delta_\tau T|^2}{\Psi_\varepsilon\ast|T|_\tau+\varepsilon}\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n+1\right)
	\end{equation}
	and
	\begin{equation}
		\label[ineq]{in1:5.24}
		\int |h_{\tau,\varepsilon}(T)|^2\phi\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}|T|_\tau\leq \int\frac{\phi|\Psi_\varepsilon\ast\delta_\tau T|^2}{\Psi_\varepsilon\ast|T|_\tau+\varepsilon}(1+\varepsilon^{1/4})\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n+\varepsilon^{1/4}
	\end{equation}
	for all $j\leq 2^{-1}\varepsilon^{-1/6}$, $\phi\in\mathcal F_j^+$, and $T\in\mathcal{R}_1(\mathbb{R}^n)$ compactly supported with $\mathcal{E}_\tau(T)\leq M$.
<!-- end prop-4 -->
<!-- proof-6: body_md -->
Fix $T=\theta\mathcal{H}^1\mathbin{\vrule height 1.3ex depth 0pt width 0.13ex\vrule height 0.13ex depth 0pt width 1ex} S\in\mathcal{R}_1(\mathbb{R}^n)$ compactly supported with $|T|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)\leq M$. For simplicity, we write $h_{\tau,\varepsilon}=h_{\tau,\varepsilon}(T)$ and $\tilde h_{\tau,\varepsilon}=\tilde h_{\tau,\varepsilon}(T)$. 
	Define $V=v(\tau(|\theta|),S)$ induced by $T$ as in \cref{inducedV}. Note that it also satisfies $\delta V(\phi h_{\varepsilon})=\delta_\tau T(\phi h_{\tau,\varepsilon})$ ($h_\varepsilon$ defined as in \cite[(5.1)]{KT17}). Hence, the two inequalities can be written as in \cite[Prop.\ 5.4]{KT17}. Therefore, it only needs to be checked whether the proof of \cite[Prop.\ 5.4]{KT17} is independent of the codimension.
	As in the beginning of the proof of \cite[Prop.\ 5.4]{KT17} (the calculations are algebraically the same), we can write 
	\begin{equation*}
		\delta_\tau T(\phi h_{\tau,\varepsilon})+\int\frac{\phi|\Psi_\varepsilon\ast\delta_\tau T|^2}{\Psi_\varepsilon\ast|T|_\tau+\varepsilon}\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n=I_1+I_2+I_3,
	\end{equation*}
	where 
	\begin{align*}
		I_1&=\int\int(\phi(x)-\phi(y)-\nabla\phi(y)\cdot(x-y))T_xS\nabla\Psi_\varepsilon(x-y)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}|T|_\tau(x)\cdot \tilde h_{\tau,\varepsilon}(y)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n(y),\\
		I_2&=\int\int\Psi_\varepsilon(x-y)T_xS(\nabla\phi(x)-\nabla\phi(y))\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}|T|_\tau(x)\cdot \tilde h_{\tau,\varepsilon}(y)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n(y),\\
		I_3&=\int\int\nabla\phi(y)\cdot(x-y)T_xS\nabla\Psi_\varepsilon(x-y)+\Psi_\varepsilon(x-y)T_xS\nabla\phi(y)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}|T|_\tau(x)\cdot\tilde{h}_{\tau,\varepsilon}(y)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n(y).
	\end{align*}
	Further, we also define
	\begin{equation*}
		I_4=-\varepsilon^2\int\int T_xS\nabla_x(\nabla\phi(y)\cdot\nabla\Psi_\varepsilon(x-y))\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}|T|_\tau(x)\cdot\tilde h_{\tau,\varepsilon}(y)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n(y).
	\end{equation*}
	\underline{Estimation of $I_1$}: We can estimate as in \cite[(5.30)]{KT17}. However, since the dimension equals $n$, we obtain a term $\varepsilon^{-n-2}$ instead of $\varepsilon^{-n-3}$. More precisely, by \cref{propertiesF} and \cref{EstimatesNabla} we have (recall that $\phi\in[0,1]$)
	\begin{multline*}
		|I_1|\leq \int\int|\phi(x)-\phi(y)-\nabla\phi(y)\cdot(x-y)||T_xS|_2|\nabla\Psi_\varepsilon(x-y)|\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}|T|_\tau(x)| \tilde h_{\tau,\varepsilon}(y)|\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n(y)\\
		                                                                                                                         
		\leq j\int| \tilde h_{\tau,\varepsilon}(y)|\int |x-y|^2e^{j|x-y|}\frac{|x-y|}{\varepsilon^2}\Psi_\varepsilon(x-y)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}|T|_\tau(x)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n(y)\\
		+j\int| \tilde h_{\tau,\varepsilon}(y)|\int |x-y|^2e^{j|x-y|}c 1_{\overline B_1\setminus\overline B_{1/2}}(x-y)e^{-1/\varepsilon}\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}|T|_\tau(x)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n(y),
	\end{multline*}
	where we use the notation $\overline {B}_r=\overline {B}_r(0)$. We obtain 
	\begin{multline*}
		j\int| \tilde h_{\tau,\varepsilon}(y)|\int |x-y|^2e^{j|x-y|}\frac{|x-y|}{\varepsilon^2}\Psi_\varepsilon(x-y)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}|T|_\tau(x)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n(y)\\
		= j\int| \tilde h_{\tau,\varepsilon}(y)|\int_{S\cap\overline{B}_{\varepsilon^{5/6}}(y)} |x-y|^2e^{j|x-y|}\frac{|x-y|}{\varepsilon^2}\Psi_\varepsilon(x-y)\tau(|\theta(x)|)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{H}^1(x)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n(y)\\
		+ j\int| \tilde h_{\tau,\varepsilon}(y)|\int_{S\setminus\overline{B}_{\varepsilon^{5/6}}(y)} |x-y|^2e^{j|x-y|}\frac{|x-y|}{\varepsilon^2}\Psi_\varepsilon(x-y)\tau(|\theta(x)|)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{H}^1(x)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n(y).
	\end{multline*}
	We have (using $\frac{|x-y|^3}{\varepsilon^2}\leq \sqrt{\varepsilon}$ in $\overline{B}_{\varepsilon^{5/6}}(y)$)
	\begin{multline*}
		j\int| \tilde h_{\tau,\varepsilon}(y)|\int_{S\cap\overline{B}_{\varepsilon^{5/6}}(y)} |x-y|^2e^{j|x-y|}\frac{|x-y|}{\varepsilon^2}\Psi_\varepsilon(x-y)\tau(|\theta(x)|)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{H}^1(x)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n(y)\\
		\leq j\sqrt{\varepsilon}e^{j\varepsilon^{5/6}}\int| \tilde h_{\tau,\varepsilon}(y)|\int_{S\cap\overline{B}_{\varepsilon^{5/6}}(y)} \Psi_\varepsilon(x-y)\tau(|\theta(x)|)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{H}^1(x)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n(y)\\
		\leq  j\sqrt{\varepsilon}e^{j\varepsilon^{5/6}}\int| \tilde h_{\tau,\varepsilon}(y)|\int\Psi_\varepsilon(x-y)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}|T|_\tau(x)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n(y).
	\end{multline*}
	For the second summand, we get (using $j\leq 2^{-1}\varepsilon^{-1/6}$ and $\Psi_\varepsilon=c_\varepsilon\psi\widehat\Psi_\varepsilon$ with $c_\varepsilon\leq c(n)$, see \cite[p.\ 63]{KT17})
	\begin{multline*}
		j\int| \tilde h_{\tau,\varepsilon}(y)|\int_{S\setminus\overline{B}_{\varepsilon^{5/6}}(y)} |x-y|^2e^{j|x-y|}\frac{|x-y|}{\varepsilon^2}\Psi_\varepsilon(x-y)\tau(|\theta(x)|)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{H}^1(x)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n(y)\\
		=j\int| \tilde h_{\tau,\varepsilon}(y)|\int_{S\setminus\overline{B}_{\varepsilon^{5/6}}(y)} |x-y|^2e^{j|x-y|}\frac{|x-y|}{\varepsilon^2}c_\varepsilon\psi(x-y)\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{n/2}\varepsilon^n}e^{-\frac{|x-y|^2}{2\varepsilon^2}}\tau(|\theta(x)|)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{H}^1(x)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n(y)\\
		\leq j\varepsilon^{-n-2}c(n)\int| \tilde h_{\tau,\varepsilon}(y)|\int_{S\setminus\overline{B}_{\varepsilon^{5/6}}(y)} |x-y|^3e^{j|x-y|}1_{\overline{B}_1(y)}(x)e^{-\frac{|x-y|^2}{2\varepsilon^2}}\tau(|\theta(x)|)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{H}^1(x)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n(y)\\
		\leq j\varepsilon^{-n-2}c(n)\int| \tilde h_{\tau,\varepsilon}(y)|\int_{S\setminus\overline{B}_{\varepsilon^{5/6}}(y)} 1^3e^{j\cdot 1}1_{\overline{B}_1(y)}(x)e^{-\frac{(\varepsilon^{5/6})^2}{2\varepsilon^2}}\tau(|\theta(x)|)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{H}^1(x)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n(y)\\
		\leq j\varepsilon^{-n-2}c(n)e^je^{-\frac{\varepsilon^{1/3}}{2}}\int | \tilde h_{\tau,\varepsilon}(y)||T|_\tau(\overline{B}_1(y))\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n(y).
	\end{multline*}
	(Note that this estimate also works when $|x-y|^3$ is replaced by $|x-y|$, which is used below in the argument related to $|I_2|$.)
	Further, we have
	\begin{multline*}
		j\int| \tilde h_{\tau,\varepsilon}(y)|\int |x-y|^2e^{j|x-y|}c 1_{\overline B_1\setminus\overline B_{1/2}}(x-y)e^{-1/\varepsilon}\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}|T|_\tau(x)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n(y)\\
		\leq  je^{j}ce^{-1/\varepsilon}\int| \tilde h_{\tau,\varepsilon}(y)|\int_S 1_{\overline B_1\setminus\overline B_{1/2}}(x-y) \tau(|\theta(x)|)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{H}^1(x)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n(y)\\
		\leq je^{j}ce^{-1/\varepsilon}\int| \tilde h_{\tau,\varepsilon}(y)||T|_\tau(\overline{B}_1(y))\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n(y).
	\end{multline*}
	In summary, we obtain
	\begin{multline*}
		|I_1|\leq j\sqrt{\varepsilon}e^{j\varepsilon^{5/6}}\int| \tilde h_{\tau,\varepsilon}(y)|\int \Psi_\varepsilon(x-y)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}|T|_\tau(x)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n(y)\\
		+j\varepsilon^{-n-2}c(n)e^je^{-\frac{\varepsilon^{1/3}}{2}}\int| \tilde h_{\tau,\varepsilon}(y)||T|_\tau(\overline{B}_1(y))\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n(y)\\
		+je^{j}ce^{-1/\varepsilon}\int| \tilde h_{\tau,\varepsilon}(y)||T|_\tau(\overline{B}_1(y))\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n(y).
	\end{multline*}
	Now we can estimate as in \cite[(5.31) \&\ (5.32)]{KT17} using also \cref{intBall} (note that it does not matter that above we have $\varepsilon^{-n-2}$ instead of $\varepsilon^{-n-3}$ as in \cite[(5.30)]{KT17}). This yields
	\begin{equation}
		\label[ineq]{in1:I1Estimate}
		                   
		|I_1|\leq j\sqrt\varepsilon\int\frac{(\Psi_\varepsilon\ast\partial_\tau T)^2}{\Psi_\varepsilon\ast|T|_\tau+\varepsilon}\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n+j  c(M,n)\sqrt\varepsilon +j c(M,n)e^{-\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon^{-1/6}}. 
	\end{equation}
	for $\varepsilon$ sufficiently small.
	
	\underline{Estimation of $I_2$}: By \cite[p.\ 73]{KT17} we have $|\nabla\phi(x)-\nabla\phi(y)|\leq j|x-y|\phi(x)e^{j|x-y|}$. This can be used to obtain
	\begin{multline*}
		|I_2|\leq \int\int\Psi_\varepsilon(x-y)|\nabla\phi(x)-\nabla\phi(y)|\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}|T|_\tau(x)|\tilde h_{\tau,\varepsilon}(y)|\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n(y)\\
		\leq j\int\int\Psi_\varepsilon(x-y)|x-y|\phi(x)e^{j|x-y|}\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}|T|_\tau(x)|\tilde h_{\tau,\varepsilon}(y)|\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n(y).
	\end{multline*}
	This looks like the first summand in the first estimate for $|I_1|$ with $|x-y|^3\varepsilon^{-2}$ replaced by $|x-y|$. We may write $|x-y|\varepsilon^{-2}$ instead of $|x-y|$. Then it also holds $|x-y|\varepsilon^{-2}\leq\sqrt{\varepsilon}$ for $x\in \overline{B}_{\varepsilon^{5/6}}(y)$. Thus, splitting the integration as in the estimate for $|I_1|$ yields that \cref{in1:I1Estimate} also holds for $|I_1|$ replaced by $|I_2|$ (in principal, one could use a slightly better estimate because there is no integral involving $1_{\overline{B}_1\setminus\overline{B}_{1/2}}$).
	
	In the remainder, we only sketch the proof since the arguments are as in the proof of \cite[Prop.\ 5.4]{KT17}.
	\underline{Estimation of $I_3-I_4$}: Note that \cite[(5.33)]{KT17} also holds in our setting (product rule),
	\begin{equation*}
		\nabla\phi(y)\cdot(x-y)T_xS\nabla\Psi_\varepsilon(x-y)+\Psi_\varepsilon(x-y)T_xS\nabla\phi(y)=T_xS\nabla_x((x-y)\cdot\nabla\phi(y)\Psi_\varepsilon(x-y)).
	\end{equation*}
	Using this and \cref{FormulaPsi}, we obtain \cite[(5.34)]{KT17},
	\begin{equation*}
		I_3-I_4=\int\int T_xS\nabla_x\left(\nabla\phi(y)\cdot\varepsilon^2c_\varepsilon\nabla\psi(x-y)\widehat{\Psi}_\varepsilon(x-y)\right)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}|T|_\tau(x)\cdot\tilde{h}_{\tau,\varepsilon}(y)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n(y).
	\end{equation*}
	Now, using similar estimates as for $|I_1|$, we obtain
	\begin{equation}
		\label[ineq]{in1:I34Estimate}
		|I_3-I_4|\leq c(M,n) je^{-1/\varepsilon}.
	\end{equation}
	\underline{Estimation of $I_4$}:
	Taking $V=v(\tau(|\theta|),S)$ and applying \cite[(5.37)]{KT17} we get
	\begin{equation*}
		I_4=-\frac{\varepsilon^2}{2}\int\frac{\nabla\phi\cdot\nabla(|\Psi_\varepsilon\ast\delta_\tau T|^2)}{\Psi_\varepsilon\ast|T|_\tau+\varepsilon}\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n.
	\end{equation*}
	\cite[(5.38)]{KT17} can also be transferred (using $\psi_r(x)=\psi(x/r)$),
	\begin{multline*}
		\int\frac{\nabla\phi\cdot\nabla(|\Psi_\varepsilon\ast\delta_\tau T|^2)}{\Psi_\varepsilon\ast|T|_\tau+\varepsilon}\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n\\
		=-\int\nabla\cdot\left(\frac{\nabla\phi}{\Psi_\varepsilon\ast|T|_\tau+\varepsilon}\right)|\Psi_\varepsilon\ast\delta_\tau T|^2\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n-\lim_{r\to\infty}\int\frac{(\nabla\psi_r\cdot\nabla\phi)|\Psi_\varepsilon\ast\delta_\tau T|^2}{\Psi_\varepsilon\ast|T|_\tau+\varepsilon}\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n.
	\end{multline*}
	\cite[(5.39)]{KT17} also holds (using \cref{hEstimates}),
	\begin{equation*}
		\int\frac{(\nabla\psi_r\cdot\nabla\phi)|\Psi_\varepsilon\ast\delta_\tau T|^2}{\Psi_\varepsilon\ast|T|_\tau+\varepsilon}\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n\leq 2j\varepsilon^{-2}\int|\nabla\psi_r||\Psi_\varepsilon\ast\delta_\tau T|\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n.
	\end{equation*}
	Now we can use the first two estimates in \cref{AuxEst}, which yields (see \cite[(5.40)]{KT17})
	\begin{equation*}
		\int|\nabla\psi_r||\Psi_\varepsilon\ast\delta_\tau T|\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n\leq c(\varepsilon,n)r^{-1}\int_{\overline{B}_r\setminus\overline{B}_{r/2}}|T|_\tau(\overline{B}_1(x))\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n(x).
	\end{equation*}
	It can also be checked that \cite[(5.41)]{KT17} holds in our setting,
	\begin{equation*}
		|I_4|\leq\frac{\varepsilon^2}{2}\int\left(\frac{nj\phi}{\Psi_\varepsilon\ast|T|_\tau+\varepsilon}+\frac{j\phi|\nabla\Psi_\varepsilon\ast|T|_\tau|}{(\Psi_\varepsilon\ast|T|_\tau+\varepsilon)^2}\right)|\Psi_\varepsilon\ast\delta_\tau T|^2\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n.
	\end{equation*}
	The last estimate in \cite[(5.42)]{KT17} can also be copied because it does not depend on the dimension,
	\begin{equation*}
		|(\nabla\Psi_\varepsilon\ast|T|_\tau)(y)|\leq\varepsilon^{-3/2}(\Psi_\varepsilon\ast|T|_\tau)(y)+ce^{-\varepsilon^{-1/2}}|T|_\tau(\overline{B}_1(y)).
	\end{equation*}
	We also have (see \cite[(5.7)]{KT17})
	\begin{equation*}
		|\tilde{h}_{\tau,\varepsilon}|\leq\varepsilon^{-2}+c(n)M\varepsilon^{-1}e^{-1/\varepsilon}.
	\end{equation*}
	\cite[(5.43)]{KT17} can also be obtained,
	\begin{equation}
		\label[ineq]{in1:I4Estimate}
		|I_4|\leq c(n) j\varepsilon^{1/2}\int\frac{\phi|\Psi_\varepsilon\ast\delta_\tau T|^2}{\Psi_\varepsilon\ast|T|_\tau+\varepsilon}\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n+je^{-\varepsilon^{-1/6}}.
	\end{equation}
	Using \cref{in1:I1Estimate,in1:I34Estimate,in1:I4Estimate} (recall that \cref{in1:I1Estimate} also holds for $|I_2|$), we get \cref{in1:5.23}.
	Finally, let us show \cref{in1:5.24}.
	Calculations \cite[(5.44)\&\ (5.45)]{KT17} can be transferred, which gives
	\begin{equation*}
		\int |h_{\tau,\varepsilon}|^2\phi\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}|T|_\tau\leq\int|\tilde{h}_{\tau,\varepsilon}(y)|^2\left(\phi(y)(\Psi_\varepsilon\ast|T|_\tau)(y)+j\phi(y)\int e^{j|x-y|}|x-y|\Psi_\varepsilon(x-y)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}|T|_\tau(x)   \right)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n(y).
	\end{equation*}
	We also obtain an inequality similar to \cite[(5.46)]{KT17},
	\begin{equation*}
		\int e^{j|x-y|}|x-y|\Psi_\varepsilon(x-y)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}|T|_\tau(x)\leq\varepsilon^{1/2}e^{j\varepsilon^{1/2}}(\Psi_\varepsilon\ast|T|_\tau)(y)+c(n)e^{j-\varepsilon^{-1/2}}|T|_\tau(\overline{B}_1(y)).
	\end{equation*}
	The analogue of \cite[(5.47)]{KT17} is
	\begin{equation*}
		\int |h_{\tau,\varepsilon}|^2\phi\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}|T|_\tau\leq\int|\tilde{h}_{\tau,\varepsilon}(y)|^2\left((\Psi_\varepsilon\ast|T|_\tau)(y)\phi(y)(1+j\varepsilon^{1/2}e^{j\varepsilon^{1/2}})+jc(n)e^{-\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon^{-1/2}}|T|_\tau(\overline{B}_1(y))  \right)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n(y).
	\end{equation*}
	The final argument in the proof of \cite[Prop.\ 5.4]{KT17} then yields \cref{in1:5.24}.
<!-- end proof-6 -->
<!-- prop-5: body_md -->
\label{Prop5.7}
	Fix some $j\in\mathbb N$ and $M\in\mathbb{R}_+^*$.
	Define
	\begin{equation*}
		c_1=3n+20.
	\end{equation*}
	Set (implicitly depending on $\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}_+^*,\Delta t\in\mathbb{R}_+$, and $T=\theta\mathcal{H}^1\mathbin{\vrule height 1.3ex depth 0pt width 0.13ex\vrule height 0.13ex depth 0pt width 1ex} S\in \mathcal{R}_1(\mathbb{R}^n)$ with $|T|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)<\infty$)
	\begin{equation*}
		f=\mathrm{id}+\Delta t\, h_{\tau,\varepsilon}(T). 
	\end{equation*}
	Then, for $\varepsilon$ sufficiently small (depending on $M,n$), we have
	\begin{equation}
		\label[ineq]{in1:Q1}
		\left| \frac{|f_\#T|_\tau(\phi)-|T|_\tau(\phi)}{\Delta t}-\delta_\tau(T,\phi)(h_{\tau,\varepsilon}(T)) \right|\leq\varepsilon^{c_1-10}  
	\end{equation}
	and
	\begin{equation}
		\label[ineq]{in1:Q2}
		\frac{|f_\#T|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)-|T|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)}{\Delta t}+\frac{1}{4}\int\frac{|\Psi_\varepsilon\ast\delta_\tau T|^2}{\Psi_\varepsilon\ast|T|_\tau+\varepsilon}\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n\leq 3\varepsilon^{1/4}  
	\end{equation}
	for all $\Delta t\in(2^{-1}\varepsilon^{c_1},\varepsilon^{c_1}),\phi\in\mathcal F_j^+$, and $T=\theta\mathcal{H}^1\mathbin{\vrule height 1.3ex depth 0pt width 0.13ex\vrule height 0.13ex depth 0pt width 1ex} S\in \mathcal{R}_1(\mathbb{R}^n)$ compactly supported with $\mathcal{H}^1(S)<\infty$ and $|T|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)\leq M$.
	Further, for $\varepsilon$ sufficiently small, we get
	\begin{equation}
		\label[ineq]{in1:Q3}
		\left| \partial_\tau(T,\phi)(h_{\tau,\varepsilon}(T))-\partial_\tau(f_\# T,\phi)(h_{\tau,\varepsilon}(f_\# T)) \right|\leq \varepsilon^{c_1-2n-17}
	\end{equation}
	and
	\begin{equation}
		\label[ineq]{in1:Q4}
		\left| \int\frac{|\Psi_\varepsilon\ast\delta_\tau T|^2}{\Psi_\varepsilon\ast|T|_\tau+\varepsilon}\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n -\int\frac{|\Psi_\varepsilon\ast\delta_\tau (f_\# T)|^2}{\Psi_\varepsilon\ast| f_\# T|_\tau+\varepsilon}\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n\right|\leq\varepsilon^{c_1-3n-15}
	\end{equation}
	for all $\Delta t\in(2^{-1}\varepsilon^{c_1},\varepsilon^{c_1}),\phi\in\mathcal F_j^+$, and $T=\theta\mathcal{H}^1\mathbin{\vrule height 1.3ex depth 0pt width 0.13ex\vrule height 0.13ex depth 0pt width 1ex} S\in \mathcal{R}_1(\mathbb{R}^n)$ compactly supported with $\mathcal{H}^1(S)<\infty$, $|T|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)\leq M$, and $|f_\# T|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)\leq M$.
<!-- end prop-5 -->
<!-- proof-7: body_md -->
\underline{\Cref{in1:Q1}:}
	Always assume that $\Delta t\in(2^{-1}\varepsilon^{c_1},\varepsilon^{c_1})$.
	Write 
	\begin{equation*}
		F=f-\mathrm{id}.
	\end{equation*}
	We have (\cref{hEstimates})
	\begin{equation}
		\label[ineq]{in1:5.59}
		|F|=|\Delta t\, h_{\tau,\varepsilon}(T)|\leq \Delta t2\varepsilon^{-2}\leq 2\varepsilon^{c_1-2}\qquad\textup{and}\qquad|\mathrm{D} F|_2=|\Delta t\, \mathrm{D} h_{\tau,\varepsilon}(T)|_2\leq \Delta t4\varepsilon^{-4}\leq 4\varepsilon^{c_1-4}.
	\end{equation}
	By \cref{propertiesF} we have (recall that $\phi\in[0,1]$)
	\begin{equation}
		\label[ineq]{in1:I}
		|\phi(f)-\phi|\leq j|f-\textup{id}|\phi e^{j|f-\textup{id}|}\leq j2\varepsilon^{c_1-2}e^{j2\varepsilon^{c_1-2}}\leq\varepsilon^{c_1-3}
	\end{equation}
	for $\varepsilon$ sufficiently small. For $\mathcal{H}^1$-a.e.\ $x\in S$ we have $\mathrm{J} f(x)=|\mathrm{D} f(x)u_x |$, where $u_x\in T_xS$ with $|u_x|=1$. This yields
	\begin{equation}
		\label[ineq]{in1:II}
		|\mathrm{J} f(x)-1|=||\mathrm{D} f(x)u_x |-|u_x||\leq |\mathrm{D} f(x)u_x -u_x|=|\mathrm{D} F(x)u_x|\leq 4\varepsilon^{c_1-4}\leq 2^{-1}\varepsilon^{c_1-5}\leq\Delta t\varepsilon^{-5}
	\end{equation}
	for $\mathcal{H}^1$-a.e.\ $x\in S$ and $\varepsilon$ sufficiently small (independent of $x$).
	Using \cref{propertiesF} again, we obtain
	\begin{equation}
		\label[ineq]{in1:III}
		|\phi(f)-\phi-\nabla\phi\cdot F|\leq j|F|^2\phi e^{j|F|}\leq j4\varepsilon^{2c_1-4}e^{j2\varepsilon^{c_1-2}}\leq 2^{-1}\varepsilon^{2c_1-5}\leq\Delta t\varepsilon^{c_1-5}
	\end{equation}
	for $\varepsilon$ sufficiently small.
	Next, we estimate $|\mathrm{J} f-1-\mathrm{D} F:T_\cdot S|$. To this end, define
	\begin{equation*}
		g_x(\Delta t)=|(\mathbb 1 +\Delta t\mathrm{D} h_{\tau,\varepsilon}(T)(x))u_x|,
	\end{equation*}
	where $u_x$ is as above, for $\mathcal{H}^1$-a.e.\ $x\in S$. We get
	\begin{equation*}
		g_x^\prime(\Delta t)=\mathrm{D} h_{\tau,\varepsilon}(T)(x)u_x\cdot\frac{(\mathbb 1 +\Delta t\mathrm{D} h_{\tau,\varepsilon}(T)(x))u_x}{|(\mathbb 1 +\Delta t\mathrm{D} h_{\tau,\varepsilon}(T)(x))u_x|}.
	\end{equation*}
	Further, we have (with $v=(\mathbb 1 +\Delta t\mathrm{D} h_{\tau,\varepsilon}(T)(x))u_x$)
	\begin{multline*}
		\left|g_x^{\prime\prime}(\Delta t)\right|=\left||v|^{-1}(\mathrm{D} h_{\tau,\varepsilon}(T)(x)u_x)^\intercal\left(\mathbb 1-|v|^{-2}v\otimes v  \right)\mathrm{D} h_{\tau,\varepsilon}(T)(x)u_x\right|\\
		=\left||v|^{-1}|\mathrm{D} h_{\tau,\varepsilon}(T)(x)u_x|^2-|v|^{-3}|v^\intercal \mathrm{D} h_{\tau,\varepsilon}(T)(x)u_x|^2\right|\\
		=|v|^{-1}|\mathrm{D} h_{\tau,\varepsilon}(T)(x)u_x|^2-|v|^{-3}|v^\intercal \mathrm{D} h_{\tau,\varepsilon}(T)(x)u_x|^2\leq |v|^{-1}|\mathrm{D} h_{\tau,\varepsilon}(T)(x)u_x|^2\leq(1-4\varepsilon^{c_1-4})^{-1}(\Delta t)^{-2}|\mathrm{D} F|_2^2\\
		\leq (1-4\varepsilon^{c_1-4})^{-1}(\Delta t)^{-2}16\varepsilon^{2c_1-8}\leq (\Delta t)^{-2}\varepsilon^{2c_1-9}\leq (\Delta t)^{-1}2\varepsilon^{c_1-9}
	\end{multline*}
	for $\varepsilon$ sufficiently small. 
	Using this and Taylor's theorem, we obtain
	\begin{equation}
		\label[ineq]{in1:IV}
		|\mathrm{J} f(x)-1-\mathrm{D} F(x):T_x S|=|g_x(\Delta t)-g_x(0)-g_x^\prime(0)\Delta t|\leq\sup_{\xi\in(-2\Delta t,2\Delta t)}|2^{-1}g_x^{\prime\prime}(\xi)(\Delta t)^2|\leq \Delta t\varepsilon^{c_1-9}.
	\end{equation}
	Applying \cite[Cor.\ 3.2.20]{F} (note that $f$ is a diffeomorphism for $\varepsilon$ sufficiently small by the Hadamard\textendash Cacciopoli theorem) we get (writing $f_\# T=\eta\mathcal{H}^1\mathbin{\vrule height 1.3ex depth 0pt width 0.13ex\vrule height 0.13ex depth 0pt width 1ex} f(S)$ as in \cref{FedererPushforward})
	\begin{multline*}
		|f_\#T|_\tau(\phi)-|T|_\tau(\phi)-\Delta t\delta_\tau(T,\phi)(h_{\tau,\varepsilon}(T))\\
		=\int_{f(S)}\phi\tau(|\eta|)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{H}^1-\int_S\phi\tau(|\theta|)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{H}^1-\Delta t \int_S(\phi\textup{div}_S(h_{\tau,\varepsilon}(T))+\nabla\phi\cdot h_{\tau,\varepsilon}(T))\tau(|\theta|)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{H}^1\\
		=\int_S\phi(f)\tau(|\eta(f)|)\mathrm{J} f-\phi\tau(|\theta|)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{H}^1-\int_S(\phi\mathrm{D} F:T_\cdot S+\nabla\phi\cdot F)\tau(|\theta|)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{H}^1\\
		=\int_S\left((\phi(f)-\phi)(\mathrm{J} f-1)+\phi(f)-\phi-\nabla\phi\cdot F+(\mathrm{J} f-1-\mathrm{D} F:T_\cdot S)\phi\right)\tau(|\theta|)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{H}^1
	\end{multline*}
	for all $\varepsilon$ sufficiently small, $\Delta t\in(2^{-1}\varepsilon^{c_1},\varepsilon^{c_1}),\phi\in\mathcal F_j^+$, and $T=\theta\mathcal{H}^1\mathbin{\vrule height 1.3ex depth 0pt width 0.13ex\vrule height 0.13ex depth 0pt width 1ex} S\in \mathcal{R}_1(\mathbb{R}^n)$ compactly supported with $\mathcal{H}^1(S)<\infty$ and $|T|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)\leq M$. Finally, application of \cref{in1:I,in1:II,in1:III,in1:IV} yields (note that $\varepsilon$ needs to satisfy $3M\leq\varepsilon^{-1}$)
	\begin{multline*}
		||f_\#T|_\tau(\phi)-|T|_\tau(\phi)-\Delta t\delta_\tau(T,\phi)(h_{\tau,\varepsilon}(T))|\\
		\leq \int_S\left(|\phi(f)-\phi||\mathrm{J} f-1|+|\phi(f)-\phi-\nabla\phi\cdot F|+|\mathrm{J} f-1-\mathrm{D} F:T_\cdot S||\phi|\right)\tau(|\theta|)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{H}^1\\
		\leq  \int_S\left(\varepsilon^{c_1-3}\Delta t\varepsilon^{-5}+\Delta t\varepsilon^{c_1-5}+\Delta t\varepsilon^{c_1-9}\right)\tau(|\theta|)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{H}^1\leq \Delta t 3\varepsilon^{c_1-9}M\leq \Delta t \varepsilon^{c_1-10}
	\end{multline*}
	for all $\varepsilon$ sufficiently small, $\Delta t\in(2^{-1}\varepsilon^{c_1},\varepsilon^{c_1}),\phi\in\mathcal F_j^+$, and $T=\theta\mathcal{H}^1\mathbin{\vrule height 1.3ex depth 0pt width 0.13ex\vrule height 0.13ex depth 0pt width 1ex} S\in \mathcal{R}_1(\mathbb{R}^n)$ compactly supported with $\mathcal{H}^1(S)<\infty$ and $|T|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)\leq M$. This shows \cref{in1:Q1}.
	
	\underline{\Cref{in1:Q2}:} Note that $\phi\equiv 1\in\mathcal F_j^+$ for all $j\in\mathbb N$. Thus, taking $\varepsilon$ sufficiently small and applying \cref{in1:5.23}, we get (cf.\ \cite[(5.66)]{KT17})
	\begin{equation*}
		\delta_\tau(T,1)(h_{\tau,\varepsilon})\leq(\varepsilon^{1/4}-1)I+\varepsilon^{1/4}
	\end{equation*}
	using the abbreviation $I=\int\frac{|\Psi_\varepsilon\ast\delta_\tau T|^2}{\Psi_\varepsilon\ast|T|_\tau+\varepsilon}\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n$. Applying this and \cref{in1:Q1}, we obtain
	\begin{multline*}
		\frac{|f_\#T|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)-|T|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)}{\Delta t}+\frac{1}{4}I=\frac{|f_\#T|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)-|T|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)}{\Delta t}-\delta_\tau(T,1)(h_{\tau,\varepsilon})+\delta_\tau(T,1)(h_{\tau,\varepsilon})+\frac{1}{4}I\\
		\leq\varepsilon^{c_1-10}+(\varepsilon^{1/4}-3/4)I+\varepsilon^{1/4}\leq 3\varepsilon^{1/4},
	\end{multline*}
	which yields \cref{in1:Q2}.
	
	\underline{\Cref{in1:Q3,in1:Q4}:} Write $f_\# T=\eta\mathcal{H}^1\mathbin{\vrule height 1.3ex depth 0pt width 0.13ex\vrule height 0.13ex depth 0pt width 1ex} f(S)$ as in \cref{FedererPushforward} (recall that $f$ is a diffeomorphism for $\varepsilon$ sufficiently small).
	Considering the varifolds $v(\tau(|\theta|),S)$ and $v(\tau(|\eta|),f(S))$ and applying the area formula \cite[Cor.\ 3.2.20]{F} and \cref{FedererPushforward} we get the analogue of \cite[(5.67)]{KT17},
	\begin{multline}
		\label[ineq]{in1:V}
		|(\Psi_\varepsilon\ast|f_\# T|_\tau)(x)-(\Psi_\varepsilon\ast|T|_\tau)(x)|\\
		\leq\int|\Psi_\varepsilon(f(y)-x)-\Psi_\varepsilon(y-x)|\mathrm{J} f(y)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}|T|_\tau(y)+\int\Psi_\varepsilon(y-x)|\mathrm{J} f(y)-1|\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}|T|_\tau(y).
	\end{multline}
	Using $|F|\leq 2\varepsilon^{c_1-2}$ (beginning of proof) and \cref{EstimatesNabla}, we get (see \cite[(5.68)]{KT17})
	\begin{equation}
		\label[ineq]{in1:VI}
		|\Psi_\varepsilon(f(y)-x)-\Psi_\varepsilon(y-x)|\leq c(n) \varepsilon^{c_1-n-4}1_{\overline{B}_1}(\hat y)\leq c(n) \varepsilon^{c_1-n-4}1_{B_2(x)}( y)
	\end{equation}
	for some $\hat y\in[y-x,f(y)-x]$, where the last inequality follows from
	\begin{equation*}
		1\geq|\hat y|=|y-x|+\lambda|f(y)-y|\geq|y-x|-2\varepsilon^{c_1-2}
	\end{equation*}
	for some $\lambda\in[-1,1]$. 
	By \cref{in1:II} and $\Delta t<\varepsilon^{c_1}$ we get an inequality similar to \cite[(5.69)]{KT17},
	\begin{equation}
		\label[ineq]{in1:VII}
		\Psi_\varepsilon(x-y)|\mathrm{J} f(y)-1|\leq c(n)\varepsilon^{c_1-n-5}1_{\overline{B}_1(x)}(y).
	\end{equation}
	Using \cref{in1:V,in1:VI,in1:VII} and $\mathrm{J} f(y)\leq 1+\varepsilon^{c_1-5}$ by \cref{in1:II}, we obtain
	\begin{equation}
		\label[ineq]{in1:VIII}
		|(\Psi_\varepsilon\ast|f_\# T|_\tau)(x)-(\Psi_\varepsilon\ast|T|_\tau)(x)|\leq\varepsilon^{c_1-n-6}|T|_\tau(B_2(x))
	\end{equation}
	for $\varepsilon$ sufficiently small, see \cite[(5.70)]{KT17}.
	A calculation similar to \cite[(5.71)]{KT17} yields (using \cite[Cor.\ 3.2.20]{F}, \cref{FedererPushforward}, and \cref{FedererPushforward})
	\begin{equation*}
		|(\Psi_\varepsilon\ast\delta_\tau(f_\# T))(x)-(\Psi_\varepsilon\ast\delta_\tau T)(x)|=\left| \int\left(\nabla f(y)T_yS\nabla\Psi_\varepsilon(f(y)-x)\mathrm{J} f(y)-T_yS\nabla\Psi_\varepsilon(y-x)\right)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}} |T|_\tau(y) \right|.
	\end{equation*}
	An argument as in the passage before \cite[(5.72)]{KT17} gives 
	\begin{equation}
		\label[ineq]{in1:VIIII}
		|(\Psi_\varepsilon\ast\delta_\tau(f_\# T))(x)-(\Psi_\varepsilon\ast\delta_\tau T)(x)|\leq \varepsilon^{c_1-n-8}|T|_\tau(B_2(x))
	\end{equation}
	for $\varepsilon$ sufficiently small. 
	The rough estimates in \cite[(5.73)]{KT17} can also be replaced,
	\begin{equation}
		\label[ineq]{in1:X}
		|(\Psi_\varepsilon\ast\delta_\tau T)(x)|, |(\Psi_\varepsilon\ast\delta_\tau (f_\# T))(x)|\leq\varepsilon^{-n-3}|T|_\tau(B_2(x)).
	\end{equation}
	Now use \cref{in1:VIII,in1:VIIII,in1:X},
	\begin{multline}
		\label[ineq]{in1:XI}
		\left| \frac{\Psi_\varepsilon\ast\delta_\tau(f_\# T)}{\Psi_\varepsilon\ast|f_\# T|_\tau+\varepsilon}-\frac{\Psi_\varepsilon\ast\delta_\tau T}{\Psi_\varepsilon\ast|T|_\tau+\varepsilon}  \right|\\
		\leq \left| \frac{\Psi_\varepsilon\ast\delta_\tau(f_\# T)-\Psi_\varepsilon\ast\delta_\tau T}{\Psi_\varepsilon\ast|f_\# T|_\tau+\varepsilon}\right|+\left|\frac{\Psi_\varepsilon\ast\delta_\tau T}{\Psi_\varepsilon\ast|f_\# T|_\tau+\varepsilon}-\frac{\Psi_\varepsilon\ast\delta_\tau T}{\Psi_\varepsilon\ast|T|_\tau+\varepsilon}  \right|\\
		\leq\varepsilon^{c_1-n-9}|T|_\tau(B_2(\cdot))+\varepsilon^{c_1-2n-11}|T|_\tau(B_2(\cdot))^2,
	\end{multline}
	see \cite[(5.74)]{KT17}.
	Applying \cref{in1:VIII,in1:VIIII,in1:X} again,
	\begin{equation}
		\label[ineq]{in1:XII}
		\left| \frac{(\Psi_\varepsilon\ast\delta_\tau(f_\# T))^2}{\Psi_\varepsilon\ast|f_\# T|_\tau+\varepsilon}-\frac{(\Psi_\varepsilon\ast\delta_\tau T)^2}{\Psi_\varepsilon\ast|T|_\tau+\varepsilon}  \right|
		\leq 2\varepsilon^{c_1-2n-12}|T|_\tau(B_2(\cdot))^2+\varepsilon^{c_1-3n-14}|T|_\tau(B_2(\cdot))^3,
	\end{equation}
	see \cite[(5.75)]{KT17}.
	Using \cref{in1:XII} and $\int|T|_\tau(\overline{B}_2(x))^k\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n(x)\leq M^{k-1}\omega_n2^nM=M^k\omega_n2^n$ (\cref{intBall}), we get \cref{in1:Q4}.
	Calculating $\nabla\Psi_\varepsilon$ and $\nabla^2\Psi_\varepsilon$ and applying \cref{in1:XI} gives the analogue of \cite[(5.76)]{KT17},
	\begin{align}
		|h_{\tau,\varepsilon}(T)-h_{\tau,\varepsilon}(f_\# T)|&\leq\varepsilon^{c_1-2n-12}(M+M^2),\label[ineq]{in1:5.76a}\\
		|\mathrm{D} h_{\tau,\varepsilon}(T)-\mathrm{D} h_{\tau,\varepsilon}(f_\# T)|&\leq\varepsilon^{c_1-2n-14}(M+M^2),\textup{ and}\label[ineq]{in1:5.76b}\\
		|\mathrm{D}^2h_{\tau,\varepsilon}(T)-\mathrm{D}^2 h_{\tau,\varepsilon}(f_\# T)|_2&\leq\varepsilon^{c_1-2n-16}(M+M^2).\label[ineq]{in1:5.76c}
	\end{align}
	Further, \cite[(5.77)]{KT17} becomes (using the definition of $\phi$-weighted $\tau$-variation, \cite[Cor.\ 3.2.20]{F}, \cref{FedererPushforward}, and \cref{FedererPushforward})
	\begin{multline*}
		\delta_\tau(T,\phi)(h_{\tau,\varepsilon}(T))-\delta_\tau(f_\# T,\phi)(h_{\tau,\varepsilon}(f_\# T)) \\
		=  \int_S[\mathrm{D} h_{\tau,\varepsilon}(T):T_\cdot S\phi+h_{\tau,\varepsilon}(T)\cdot\nabla\phi\\
		-\left( \mathrm{D} h_{\tau,\varepsilon}(f_\# T)(f):(\mathrm{D} fT_\cdot S)\phi(f)+h_{\tau,\varepsilon}(f_\# T)(f)\cdot\nabla\phi(f) \right)\mathrm{J} f]\tau(|\theta|)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{H}^1.
	\end{multline*}
	The last argument in the proof of \cite[Prop.\ 5.7]{KT17} uses \cite[(5.59)--(5.62)]{KT17} and \cite[(5.76)]{KT17} which correspond to \cref{in1:5.59,in1:I,in1:II,in1:5.76a,in1:5.76b,in1:5.76c}.
	In comparison, the exponent of $\varepsilon$ in \cref{in1:5.59,in1:I,in1:II,in1:5.76a,in1:5.76b,in1:5.76c} can be obtained by replacing $n$ in the proof of \cite[Prop.\ 5.7]{KT17} by $n-1$.
	Hence, applying the same argument as in the last two lines in the proof of \cite[Prop.\ 5.7]{KT17} gives \cref{in1:Q3}.
<!-- end proof-7 -->
<!-- lem-8: body_md -->
\label{ProjCost}
Let $\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^n$ be compact and convex. Write $p:\mathbb{R}^n\to\Omega$ for the orthogonal projection onto $\Omega$. Then we have
\begin{equation*}
|p_\# T|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)\leq |T|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)
\end{equation*}
for all $T\in\mathcal{T}_1(\mathbb{R}^n)$.
<!-- end lem-8 -->
<!-- proof-8: body_md -->
First, let us verify the inequality for the polyhedral $1$-chains $\mathbb P_1(\mathbb{R}^n)$. Each $P\in\mathbb P_1(\mathbb{R}^n)$ can be written
\begin{equation*}
	P=\sum_em_e\vec{e}\mathcal{H}^1\mathbin{\vrule height 1.3ex depth 0pt width 0.13ex\vrule height 0.13ex depth 0pt width 1ex} e,
\end{equation*}
where the sum if over finitely many non-overlapping edges $e=e_-+[0,1](e_+-e_-)\subset\mathbb{R}^n$ with orientation $(e_+-e_-)/|e_+-e_-|\in\mathcal S^{n-1}$ and mass $m_e\in\mathbb{R}_+$. 
Now label the edges $e_1,\ldots,e_N$ and write $p_\# P=\theta\mathcal{H}^1\mathbin{\vrule height 1.3ex depth 0pt width 0.13ex\vrule height 0.13ex depth 0pt width 1ex} S$. 
We can write (possibly neglecting some $\mathcal{H}^1$-zero set) $S$ as a disjoint union $S=\bigcup_I S_I$, where $I\subset\{ 1,\ldots,N \}$ and $S_I=\bigcap_{i\in I}p(e_i)\setminus\bigcup_{j\in\{ 1,\ldots, N \}\setminus I} p(e_j)$.
Using this decomposition (in the second equation), we obtain
\begin{multline*}
|P|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)=\sum_e\tau(m_e)\mathcal{H}^1(e)\geq\sum_e\tau(m_e)\mathcal{H}^1(p(e))=\sum_{I\subset\{ 1,\ldots,N \}}\sum_{i\in I}\tau(m_{e_i})\mathcal{H}^1(S_I)=\int_{S}\sum_{x\in p(e)}\tau(m_e)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{H}^1(x)\\
\geq \int_{S}\tau\bigg(\sum_{x\in p(e)} m_e\bigg)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{H}^1(x)\geq \int_{S}\tau(|\theta|)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{H}^1=|p_\# P|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n),
\end{multline*}
where we also used the $1$-Lipschitz continuity of $p$ in the first inequality, the subadditivity of $\tau$ in the second inequality, and \cref{FedererPushforward} in the last inequality.
Now let $T\in\mathcal{T}_1(\mathbb{R}^n)$ and $\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}_+^*$ be arbitrary. Take any compact and convex $K\subset\mathbb{R}^n$ with $\textup{supp}(T)\subset K$. By \cite[Prop.\ 2.32]{BW} and \cite[Thm.\ 5]{MW19} we have the following formula:
\begin{equation*}
|T|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)=\inf_{(P_i)}\,\liminf_i|P_i|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n),
\end{equation*}
where the infimum is over sequences $(P_i)\subset\mathbb P_1(\mathbb{R}^n)$ with $\textup{supp}(P_i)\subset K$ and $|P_i-T|^\flat\to 0$. 
Fix any such sequence $(P_i)$ with $\lim_i|P_i|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)\leq |T|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)+\varepsilon/2$.
Write $T_i=p_\# P_i$. 
By \cite[p.\ 371]{F} and \cite[Memo 2.5]{LSW25} we obtain
\begin{equation*}
|T_i-p_\# T|_\Omega^\flat\leq |P_i-T|_K^\flat=|P_i-T|^\flat\to 0.
\end{equation*}
Using the formula from above, for each $i\in\mathbb N$, we can pick a sequence $(Q_i^j)\subset\mathbb P_1(\mathbb{R}^n)$ with $\lim_j|T_i-Q_i^j|^\flat= 0$ and $|Q_i^j|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)\leq |T_i|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)+\varepsilon/2$ for all $j$. Further, we can let $j=j(i)$ depend on $i$ such that $|Q_i^{j(i)}-p_\# T|^\flat\to 0$ for $i\to\infty$ (triangle inequality). This gives
\begin{equation*}
|p_\# T|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)\leq\liminf_i|Q_i^{j(i)}|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)\leq\liminf_i|T_i|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\leq \liminf_i|P_i|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\leq |T|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)+\varepsilon
\end{equation*}
where the formula from above was used in the first inequality and $T_i=p_\# P_i$ with $P_i\in\mathbb P_1(\mathbb{R}^n)$ in the third inequality (beginning of proof). Since $\varepsilon$ was arbitrary, this proves the claim.
<!-- end proof-8 -->
<!-- prop-6: body_md -->
\label{Prop6.1}
	Let $T_0\in\mathcal{T}_1(\mathbb{R}^n)$ with $|T_0|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)<\infty$. 
	Write $\Omega=\textup{conv}(\textup{supp}(T_0))$.
	Define $T^{j,0}=T_0$ for all $j\in\mathbb N$. 
	For all $j\in\mathbb N$ there exist $\varepsilon_j\in(0,j^{-6}),p_j\in\mathbb N$, and $T^{j,1},\ldots,T^{j,j2^{p_j}}\in\mathcal{T}_1(\mathbb{R}^n)$ with $\textup{supp}(T^{j,\ell})\subset\{ \textup{dist}(\cdot,\Omega)\leq 2\varepsilon_j^{c_1-2} \}$ and
	\begin{align}
		|T^{j,\ell}|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)&\leq |T_0|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)+\varepsilon_j^{1/8}\ell\Delta t_j,\label[ineq]{in1:6.3} \\
		\frac{|T^{j,\ell}|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)-|T^{j,\ell-1}|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)}{\Delta t_j}&+\frac{1}{4}\int\frac{| \Psi_{\varepsilon_j}\ast\delta_\tau T^{j,\ell} |^2}{\Psi_{\varepsilon_j}\ast|T^{j,\ell}|_\tau+\varepsilon_j  }\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n-\frac{1-j^{-5}}{\Delta t_j}\Delta_j|T^{j,\ell-1}|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n) \leq\varepsilon_j^{1/8},\qquad\textup{and} \label[ineq]{in1:6.4}\\
		\frac{|T^{j,\ell}|_\tau(\phi)-|T^{j,\ell-1}|_\tau(\phi)}{\Delta t_j} &\leq \delta_\tau(T^{j,\ell},\phi)(h_{\tau,\varepsilon_j}(T^{j,\ell}))+\varepsilon_j^{1/8}\label[ineq]{in1:6.5}
	\end{align}
	for all $\ell=1,\ldots,j2^{p_j}$ and $\phi\in\mathcal F_j^+$, where  $\Delta t_j=2^{-p_j}$. Further, we can choose $p_j$ such that $j\mapsto p_j$ is increasing.
<!-- end prop-6 -->
<!-- proof-9: body_md -->
Set $M=|T_0|(\mathbb{R}^n)+1$, see \cite[(6.6)]{KT17}. For each $j\in\mathbb N$ take $\varepsilon_j\in(0,1)$ such that $j\leq 2^{-1}\varepsilon_j^{-1/6}$, the conclusions in \cref{hEstimates,Prop5.3,LongProof,Prop5.5,Prop5.7} hold, 
	\begin{equation}
		\label[ineq]{in1:6.7}
		\varepsilon_j^{1/8}j<1,\qquad\textup{and}\qquad 3\varepsilon_j^{1/4}+\varepsilon_j^{c_1-3n-15}<\varepsilon_j^{1/8},
	\end{equation}
	in particular $\varepsilon_j<j^{-6}$, cf.\ \cite[(6.7)]{KT17}.
	Choose $p_j\in \mathbb N$ such that $\Delta t_j=2^{-p_j}\in(\varepsilon_j^{c_1}/2,\varepsilon_j^{c_1}]$ \cite[(6.8)]{KT17}.
	If necessary, decrease $\varepsilon_j$ to ensure that $j\mapsto p_j$ is increasing.
	No keep $j$ fixed.
	Assume that $T^{j,k}$ satisfies \cref{in1:6.3,in1:6.4,in1:6.5} for all $k\in\{ 0,1,\ldots,\ell \}$. 
	We show that \cref{in1:6.3,in1:6.4,in1:6.5} are true for all $k\in\{ 0,1,\ldots,\ell+1 \}$. 
	(If $k=0$, then \cref{in1:6.3} is trivially satisfied, and for $k=1$ it will be sufficient to use this inequality to obtain \cref{in1:6.3,in1:6.4,in1:6.5}.) 
	Take $f_1\in\mathcal{A}_j(T^{j,\ell})$ such that (see \cite[(6.9)]{KT17} or \cite[Sec.\ 4.13]{B78})
	\begin{equation}
		\label[ineq]{in1:6.9}
		|(f_1)_\# T^{j,\ell}|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)-|T^{j,\ell}|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)\leq(1-j^{-5})\Delta_j|T^{j,\ell}|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n).
	\end{equation}
	As in \cite[(6.10)]{KT17} define 
	\begin{equation}
		\label{6.10}
		\widetilde{T}^{j,\ell+1}=(f_1)_\# T^{j,\ell}.
	\end{equation}
	This is a transportation network by \cref{FedererPushforward}. The admissibility of $f_1$, induction hypothesis, and the first inequality in \labelcref{in1:6.7} yield (cf.\ \cite[(6.11)]{KT17})
	\begin{equation}
		\label[ineq]{in1:6.11}
		|\widetilde T^{j,\ell+1}|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)\leq|T^{j,\ell}|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)\leq|T_0|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)+\varepsilon_j^{1/8}\ell\Delta t_j\leq |T_0|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)+\varepsilon_j^{1/8}j2^{p_j}2^{-p_j}<M.
	\end{equation}
	Let $f_{1.5}:\mathbb{R}^n\to\Omega $ be the orthogonal projection onto $\Omega$. Define
	\begin{equation*}
		\widehat{T}^{j,\ell+1}=(f_{1.5})_\#\widetilde{T}^{j,\ell+1},
	\end{equation*}
	which is still a transportation network (by Lipschitz continuity of $f_{1.5}$ and \cref{FedererPushforward}). Note that
	\begin{equation}
		\label[ineq]{in1:6.111}
		|\widehat T^{j,\ell+1}|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)\leq |\widetilde T^{j,\ell+1}|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)
	\end{equation}
	by \cref{ProjCost}.
	Next, set (see \cite[(6.12)]{KT17} or \cite[Sec.\ 4.13]{B78})
	\begin{equation*}
		f_2=\textup{id}+\Delta t_j h_{\tau,\varepsilon_j}(\widehat{T}^{j,\ell+1})\in C_c^\infty(\mathbb{R}^n;\mathbb{R}^n).
	\end{equation*}
	\Cref{hEstimates} implies the analogue of \cite[(6.13)]{KT17},
	\begin{equation}
		\label[ineq]{in1:6.13}
		|\Delta t_jh_{\tau,\varepsilon_j}(\widehat T^{j,\ell+1})|\leq 2\varepsilon_j^{c_1-2}\qquad\textup{and}\qquad |\mathrm{D}(\Delta t_jh_{\tau,\varepsilon_j}(\widehat T^{j,\ell+1}))|\leq 4\varepsilon_j^{c_1-4}.
	\end{equation}
	A simple application of Hadamard's global inverse function theorem yields that $f_2$ is a diffeomorphism.
	As in \cite[(6.14)]{KT17}, we can define another transportation network by
	\begin{equation}
		\label{6.14}
		T^{j,\ell+1}=(f_2)_\# \widehat T^{j,\ell+1}.
	\end{equation}
	By construction and \cref{hEstimates} we obtain $\textup{supp}(T^{j,\ell+1})\subset\{ \textup{dist}(\cdot,\Omega)\leq 2\varepsilon_{j}^{c_1-2} \}$ as desired.
	\Cref{in1:Q2,in1:6.111,in1:6.7} (in the first inequality) and the second inequality in \labelcref{in1:6.11} (in the second inequality) yield the analogue of \cite[(6.16)]{KT17},
	\begin{multline*}
		|T^{j,\ell+1}|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)=|(f_2)_\#\widehat T^{j,\ell+1}|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)\leq |T^{j,\ell}|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)+\Delta t_j\varepsilon_j^{1/8}\leq |T_0|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)+\varepsilon_j^{1/8}\ell\Delta t_j+\Delta t_j\varepsilon_j^{1/8}\\
		=|T_0|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)+\varepsilon_j^{1/8}(\ell+1)\Delta t_j,
	\end{multline*}
	which gives \cref{in1:6.3}. In particular, we have $|T^{j,\ell+1}|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)\leq M$ using the first inequality in \labelcref{in1:6.7}. Thus, \cref{in1:Q1,in1:Q2,in1:Q3,in1:Q4} are applicable to $\widehat{T}^{j,\ell+1}$. Using \cref{in1:6.9} (in the first inequality), \cref{6.14,6.10,in1:6.111} (in the second inequality), \cref{in1:Q4,in1:Q2} (in the third inequality), and last inequality in \labelcref{in1:6.7} (in the last estimate) we obtain
	\begin{multline*}
		\frac{|T^{j,\ell+1}|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)-|T^{j,\ell}|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)}{\Delta t_j}+\frac{1}{4}\int\frac{| \Psi_{\varepsilon_j}\ast\delta_\tau T^{j,\ell+1} |^2}{\Psi_{\varepsilon_j}\ast|T^{j,\ell+1}|_\tau+\varepsilon_j  }\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n-\frac{1-j^{-5}}{\Delta t_j}\Delta_j|T^{j,\ell}|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)\\
		\leq \frac{|T^{j,\ell+1}|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)-|T^{j,\ell}|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)}{\Delta t_j}+\frac{1}{4}\int\frac{| \Psi_{\varepsilon_j}\ast\delta_\tau T^{j,\ell+1} |^2}{\Psi_{\varepsilon_j}\ast|T^{j,\ell+1}|_\tau+\varepsilon_j  }\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n+\frac{1}{\Delta t_j}\left( |T^{j,\ell}|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)-|(f_1)_\# T^{j,\ell}|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n) \right)\\
		\leq\frac{|(f_2)_\# \widehat T^{j,\ell+1}|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)-|\widehat T^{j,\ell+1}|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)}{\Delta t_j}+\frac{1}{4}\int\frac{| \Psi_{\varepsilon_j}\ast\delta_\tau ((f_2)_\# \widehat T^{j,\ell+1}) |^2}{\Psi_{\varepsilon_j}\ast|(f_2)_\# \widehat T^{j,\ell+1}|_\tau+\varepsilon_j  }\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n\leq 3\varepsilon_j^{1/4}+\frac{1}{4}\varepsilon_j^{c_1-3n-15}<\varepsilon_j^{1/8},
	\end{multline*}
	which yields \cref{in1:6.4}. Finally, application of \cref{6.14,in1:6.111,in1:6.11} (in the first inequality), \cref{in1:Q1} (in the second inequality), \cref{6.14,in1:Q3} (in the third inequality), and last inequality in \labelcref{in1:6.7} (in the last estimate) gives
	\begin{multline*}
