		\frac{|T^{j,\ell+1}|_\tau(\phi)-|T^{j,\ell}|_\tau(\phi)}{\Delta t_j}\leq \frac{|(f_2)_\#\widehat T^{j,\ell+1}|_\tau(\phi)-|\widehat T^{j,\ell+1}|_\tau(\phi)}{\Delta t_j}\leq\varepsilon_j^{c_1-10}+\delta_\tau(\widehat T^{j,\ell+1},\phi)(h_{\tau,\varepsilon_j}(\widehat T^{j,\ell+1}))\\
		\leq \varepsilon_j^{c_1-10}+\varepsilon_j^{c_1-2n-17}+\delta_\tau( T^{j,\ell+1},\phi)(h_{\tau,\varepsilon_j}( T^{j,\ell+1}))\leq 3\varepsilon_j^{1/4}+\varepsilon_j^{c_1-3n-15}+\delta_\tau( T^{j,\ell+1},\phi)(h_{\tau,\varepsilon_j}( T^{j,\ell+1}))\\
		<\varepsilon_j^{1/8}+\delta_\tau( T^{j,\ell+1},\phi)(h_{\tau,\varepsilon_j}( T^{j,\ell+1})).
	\end{multline*}
<!-- end proof-9 -->
<!-- prop-7: body_md -->
\label{Prop6.4}
	Let the assumptions be as in \cref{Prop6.1}. For $j\in\mathbb N$ and $t\in[0,j]$ define
	\begin{equation*}
		T_t^j=T^{j,\lceil t/\Delta t_j\rceil}.
	\end{equation*}
	Then there exists a subsequence $(j_k)$ and a family $(\mu_t)_{t\in\mathbb{R}_+}\subset\mathcal{M}_+(\mathbb{R}^n)$ such that $|T_t^{j_k}|_\tau\stackrel{*}{\rightharpoonup}\mu_t$ for $k\to\infty$ for all $t\in\mathbb{R}_+$,
	\begin{equation}
		\label[ineq]{in1:6.19}
		\limsup_k\int_{[0,R]}\left( \int\frac{|\Psi_{\varepsilon_{j_k}}\ast\delta_\tau T_t^{j_k}|^2}{\Psi_{\varepsilon_{j_k}}\ast|T_t^{j_k}|_\tau+\varepsilon_{j_k}}\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n-\frac{1}{\Delta t_{j_k}}\Delta_{j_k}|T_t^{j_k}|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n) \right)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}(t)<\infty
	\end{equation}
	for all $R\in\mathbb{R}_+$, and
	\begin{equation}
		\label[ineq]{in1:6.20}
		\lim_k j_k^{2n}\Delta_{j_k}|T_t^{j_k}|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)=0
	\end{equation}
	for a.e.\ $t\in\mathbb{R}_+$.
<!-- end prop-7 -->
<!-- proof-10: body_md -->
Abbreviate $D=\{ i2^{-j}\:|\: i,j\in\mathbb N_0 \}$. Note that $D$ is dense in $\mathbb{R}_+$. By \cref{in1:6.3} we have
	\begin{equation*}
		\limsup_{j}\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}_+}|T_t^j|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)\leq|T_0|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n).
	\end{equation*}
	As in \cite[(6.21)]{KT17}, application of the Banach\textendash Alaoglu theorem and a diagonal argument yields the existence of a subsequence $(j_k)$ and a family $(\mu_t)_{t\in D}\subset\mathcal{M}_+(\mathbb{R}^n)$ such that $|T_t^{j_k}|_\tau\stackrel{*}{\rightharpoonup}\mu_t$ for $k\to\infty$ for all $t\in D$. Note that $\mu_t(\mathbb{R}^n)\leq |T_0|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)$, which follows from the lower semicontinuity of the total variation with respect to weak-$*$ convergence. As in the proof of \cite[Prop.\ 6.4]{KT17}, we choose a sequence $(\phi_i)\subset C_c^2(\mathbb{R}^n;\mathbb{R}_+)$ such that $\{ \phi_i\:|\: i\in\mathbb N \}$ is dense in $C_c(\mathbb{R}^n;\mathbb{R}_+)$. We can actually assume $(\phi_i)\subset C_c^2(\mathbb{R}^n;\mathbb{R}_+^*)$ by adding countably many small numbers $\delta_m$ ($m\in\mathbb N$) with $\delta_m\to 0$ to each member of the sequence.
	For $i,J\in\mathbb N$ define (see \cite[(6.23)]{KT17})
	\begin{equation*}
		g_{i,J}(t)=\mu_t(\phi_i)-t||\nabla^2\phi_i|_2|_\infty |T_0|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)
	\end{equation*}
	for all $t\in[0,J]\cap D$. We claim that $g_{i,J}$ is monotone decreasing. Since $\phi_i$ attains a maximum, we can assume $\phi_i\in(0,1)$ by rescaling $g_{i,J}$. Following the calculation in \cite[(5.66)]{KT17}, we have (see \cite[(6.24)]{KT17})
	\begin{multline}
		\label[ineq]{in1:6.24}
		\delta_\tau(T_t^j,\phi)(h_{\tau,\varepsilon_j}(T_t^j))=\delta_\tau T_t^j(\phi h_{\tau,\varepsilon_j}(T_t^j))+\int_S h_{\tau,\varepsilon_j}(T_t^j)\cdot(\nabla\phi-T\cdot S\nabla\phi )\tau(|\theta|)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{H}^1\\
		\leq \delta_\tau T_t^j(\phi h_{\tau,\varepsilon_j}(T_t^j))+\frac{1}{2}\int\frac{|\nabla\phi|^2}{\phi}\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}|T_t^j|_\tau+\frac{1}{2}\int | h_{\tau,\varepsilon_j}(T_t^j) |^2\phi\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}|T_t^j|_\tau\\
		\leq 2\varepsilon_j^{1/4}+\left( \frac{3}{2}\varepsilon_j^{1/4}-\frac{1}{2} \right) I_\phi+\frac{1}{2}\int\frac{|\nabla\phi|^2}{\phi}\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}|T_t^j|_\tau\leq 2\varepsilon_j^{1/4}+\frac{1}{2}\int\frac{|\nabla\phi|^2}{\phi}\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}|T_t^j|_\tau
	\end{multline}
	for all $j\in\mathbb N$ and $\phi\in \mathcal F_j^+\setminus\{ 0 \}$, where $T_t^j=\theta\mathcal{H}^1\mathbin{\vrule height 1.3ex depth 0pt width 0.13ex\vrule height 0.13ex depth 0pt width 1ex} S$ and $I_\phi=\int\phi|\Psi_{\varepsilon_j}\ast\delta_\tau T_t^j|^2/(\Psi_{\varepsilon_j}\ast|T_t^j|_\tau+\varepsilon_j)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n$. Here we used $2ab\leq a^2+b^2$ and $|\nabla\phi-T_\cdot S\nabla\phi|\leq|\nabla\phi|$ as well as \cref{in1:5.23,in1:5.24}.
	Recall that we assumed $\phi_i<1$. Take $i_0,j_0\in\mathbb N$ such that $\phi_i+i_0^{-1}<1$ and $j_0\geq\max\{ J,|\nabla\phi_i|,|\nabla^2\phi_i|_2 \}$. Then
	\begin{equation*}
		\phi_i+i_0^{-1}\in\mathcal F_{j_0}^+\qquad\textup{and}\qquad j_0\geq J.
	\end{equation*}
	Now take any $t_1,t_2\in[0,J]\cap D$ with $t_1<t_2$. Write $t_1=i_12^{-j_1},t_2=i_22^{-j_2}$. We can choose larger $j_0$ such that $j_0\geq\max\{ j_1,j_2 \}$. Then
	\begin{equation*}
		t_1,t_2\in 2^{-j_0}\mathbb N_0.
	\end{equation*}
	Using \cref{in1:6.5,in1:6.24}, we get (writing $t_1=n_12^{-j_0},t_2=n_22^{-j_0}$)
	\begin{multline}
		\label[ineq]{in1:6.25}
		|T_{t_2}^{j_k}|_\tau(\phi_i+i_0^{-1})-|T_{t_1}^{j_k}|_\tau(\phi_i+i_0^{-1})=|T^{j_k,\lceil t_2/\Delta t_{j_k}\rceil }|_\tau(\phi_i+i_0^{-1})-|T^{j_k,\lceil t_1/\Delta t_{j_k}\rceil }|_\tau(\phi_i+i_0^{-1})\\
		=\sum_{\ell=\lceil t_1/\Delta t_{j_k}\rceil}^{\lceil t_2/\Delta t_{j_k}\rceil-1}\left( |T^{j_k,\ell+1}|_\tau(\phi_i+i_0^{-1})-|T^{j_k,\ell}|_\tau(\phi_i+i_0^{-1}) \right)\\
		\leq \Delta t_{j_k}\sum_{\ell=\lceil t_1/\Delta t_{j_k}\rceil}^{\lceil t_2/\Delta t_{j_k}\rceil-1}\left( \delta_\tau(T^{j_k,\ell+1},\phi_i+i_0^{-1})(h_{\tau,\varepsilon_{j_k}}(T^{j_k,\ell+1}))+\varepsilon_{j_k}^{1/8} \right)\\
		=\Delta t_{j_k}\sum_{\ell=\lceil t_1/\Delta t_{j_k}\rceil}^{\lceil t_2/\Delta t_{j_k}\rceil-1}\left( \delta_\tau(T_{(\ell+1)\Delta t_{j_k}}^{j_k},\phi_i+i_0^{-1})(h_{\tau,\varepsilon_{j_k}}(T_{(\ell+1)\Delta t_{j_k}}^{j_k}))+\varepsilon_{j_k}^{1/8} \right)\\
		\leq \Delta t_{j_k}\sum_{\ell=\lceil t_1/\Delta t_{j_k}\rceil}^{\lceil t_2/\Delta t_{j_k}\rceil-1}\left( 2\varepsilon_{j_k}^{1/4}+\frac{1}{2}\int\frac{|\nabla\phi_i|^2}{\phi+i_0^{-1}}\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}|T_{(\ell+1)\Delta t_{j_k}}^{j_k}|_\tau+\varepsilon_{j_k}^{1/8}  \right)\\
		=(t_2-t_1)(2\varepsilon_{j_k}^{1/4}+\varepsilon_{j_k}^{1/8})+\sum_{\ell=\lceil t_1/\Delta t_{j_k}\rceil}^{\lceil t_2/\Delta t_{j_k}\rceil-1}\Delta t_{j_k}\frac{1}{2}\int\frac{|\nabla\phi_i|^2}{\phi+i_0^{-1}}\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}|T_{(\ell+1)\Delta t_{j_k}}^{j_k}|_\tau\\
		=(t_2-t_1)(2\varepsilon_{j_k}^{1/4}+\varepsilon_{j_k}^{1/8})+\frac{1}{2}\int_{[t_1,t_2]}\int\frac{|\nabla\phi_i|^2}{\phi+i_0^{-1}}\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}|T_{t}^{j_k}|_\tau\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}(t)
	\end{multline}
	for all $j_k\geq j_0$\footnote{The last equality follows from $t_1,t_2\in\Delta t_{j_k}\mathbb N_0$ for $j_k\geq j_0$, which is true by $p_{j_k}\geq j_k$ (\cref{Prop6.1}). For example, we get $t_1=\Delta t_{j_k}n_12^{p_{j_k}-j_k}2^{j_k-j_0}\in\Delta t_{j_k}\mathbb N_0$.}, see \cite[6.25]{KT17}.
	Using $|T_t^{j_k}|_\tau\stackrel{*}{\rightharpoonup}\mu_t$ for $t\in D$, \cref{in1:6.3}, and $\varepsilon_{j_k}<j_k^{-6}$, we get the analogue of \cite[(6.26)]{KT17},
	\begin{equation}
		\label[ineq]{in1:6.26}
		\mu_{t_2}(\phi_i)-\mu_{t_1}(\phi_i)-i_0^{-1}|T_0|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)\leq \lim_k |T_{t_2}^{j_k}|_\tau(\phi_i+i_0^{-1})-|T_{t_1}^{j_k}|_\tau(\phi_i+i_0^{-1}).
	\end{equation}
	Now \cite[(6.27)]{KT17} simplifies to
	\begin{equation}
		\label[ineq]{in1:6.27}
		\frac{|\nabla\phi_i|^2}{\phi_i+i_0^{-1}}\leq \frac{|\nabla\phi_i|^2}{\phi_i}\leq 2||\nabla^2\phi_i|_2|_\infty,
	\end{equation}
	where we used the formula $|\nabla f|^2\leq 2f||\nabla^2f|_2|_\infty$ for all $f\in C_c^2(\mathbb{R}^n;\mathbb{R}_+^*)$ (it can be shown via Taylor's theorem).
	By \cref{in1:6.25,in1:6.26,in1:6.27,in1:6.3} we have
	\begin{equation*}
		\mu_{t_2}(\phi_i)-\mu_{t_1}(\phi_i)\leq (t_2-t_1)||\nabla^2\phi_i|_2|_\infty|T_0|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)+i_0^{-1}|T_0|(\mathbb{R}^n).
	\end{equation*}
	Letting $i_0\to\infty$ (cf.\ \cite[(6.28)]{KT17}),
	\begin{equation*}
		\mu_{t_2}(\phi_i)-\mu_{t_1}(\phi_i)\leq (t_2-t_1)||\nabla^2\phi_i|_2|_\infty|T_0|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n).
	\end{equation*}
	It follow that $g_{i,J}$ is monotone decreasing on $[0,J]\cap D$. Now define
	\begin{equation*}
		P=\bigcup_{J\in\mathbb N}\{  t\in(0,J)\:|\: g_{i,J}\textup{ not continuous in }t \}\subset\mathbb{R}_+.
	\end{equation*}
	Set $P$ is countable because a monotone $\mathbb{R}$-valued function defined on an interval has countably many discontinuities.
	First, we claim that $\lim_k|T_t^{j_k}|_\tau(\phi_i)=\mu_t(\phi_i)$ for all $t\in\mathbb{R}_+\setminus(P\cup D)$.
	Fix $t\in\mathbb{R}_+\setminus(P\cup D)$. Let $(t_k)\subset D$ such that $t_k\searrow t$ and $T_{t_k}^{j_k}=T_{t}^{j_k}$.
	Now take $s\in D$ with $t\leq t_k<s$ for $k$ sufficiently large.
	\Cref{in1:6.25} implies (see \cite[(6.31)]{KT17})
	\begin{equation*}
		|T_s^{j_k}|_\tau(\phi_i+i_0^{-1})\leq|T_{t_k}^{j_k}|_\tau(\phi_i+i_0^{-1})+ o(1)
	\end{equation*}
	as $s\searrow t$. Applying $\lim_{i_0}\liminf_k$ yields an inequality corresponding to \cite[(6.32)]{KT17},
	\begin{equation*}
		\mu_s(\phi_i)\leq\liminf_k|T_{t_k}^{j_k}|_\tau(\phi_i)+o(1)
	\end{equation*}
	as $s\searrow t$. Letting $s\searrow t$ gives (recall that $T_{t_k}^{j_k}=T_{t}^{j_k}$ and $t\mapsto \mu_t(\phi_i)$ is continuous because $t\notin P$)
	\begin{equation}
		\label[ineq]{in1:6.33}
		\mu_t(\phi_i)\leq\liminf_k|T_{t}^{j_k}|_\tau(\phi_i),
	\end{equation}
	compare with \cite[(6.33)]{KT17}.
	Using similar arguments one also obtains (see \cite[(6.36)]{KT17})
	\begin{equation}
		\label[ineq]{in1:6.36}
		\limsup_k|T_{t}^{j_k}|_\tau(\phi_i)\leq\mu_t(\phi_i).
	\end{equation}
	\Cref{in1:6.33,in1:6.36} show $\lim_k|T_t^{j_k}|_\tau(\phi_i)=\mu_t(\phi_i)$ for all $t\in\mathbb{R}_+\setminus(P\cup D)$. Since $\{ \phi_i\:|\:i\in\mathbb N \}$ is dense in $C_c(\mathbb{R}^n;\mathbb{R}_+)$, this relation is also true for $\phi_i$ replaced by any $\phi\in C_c(\mathbb{R}^n;\mathbb{R}_+)$. Thus, it also holds for $\phi\in C_c(\mathbb{R}^n)$ by decomposing into positive and negative part. Using the beginning of the proof, we get the validity for $t\in D$. Since $P$ is countable, using a diagonal argument again, there exist measures $\{ \mu_t\:|\: t\in P \}$ such that, up to a subsequence, $\lim_k|T_t^{j_k}|_\tau(\phi)=\mu_t(\phi)$ for all $\phi\in C_c(\mathbb{R}^n),t\in P$. In summary, this shows the first claim.\\
	Next, we show \cref{in1:6.19}.
	Multiplication of \cref{in1:6.4} with $\Delta t_{j_k}$ and summation over $\ell$ gives
	\begin{multline*}
		\sum_{\ell=1}^{j_k2^{p_{j_k}}}\left( |T^{j_k,\ell}|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)-|T^{j_k,\ell-1}|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)+\frac{\Delta t_{j_k}}{4}\int\frac{| \Psi_{\varepsilon_{j_k}}\ast\delta_\tau T^{j_k,\ell} |^2}{\Psi_{\varepsilon_{j_k}}\ast|T^{j_k,\ell}|_\tau+\varepsilon_{j_k}  }\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n-\Delta t_{j_k}\frac{1-{j_k}^{-5}}{\Delta t_{j_k}}\Delta_{j_k}|T^{j_k,\ell-1}|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)\right)\\ \leq\varepsilon_{j_k}^{1/8}\Delta t_{j_k}j_k2^{p_{j_k}}=\varepsilon_{j_k}^{1/8}j_k < 1,
	\end{multline*}
	where the inequality follows from the first inequality in \labelcref{in1:6.7}. This becomes
	\begin{multline*}
		|T^{j_k,j_k2^{p_{j_k}}}|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)-|T_0|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)+\frac{1}{4}\int_{[0,j_k]}\int\frac{| \Psi_{\varepsilon_{j_k}}\ast\delta_\tau T_t^{j_k} |^2}{\Psi_{\varepsilon_{j_k}}\ast|T_t^{j_k}|_\tau+\varepsilon_{j_k}  }\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}(t)\\
		-\int_{[0,j_k-\Delta t_{j_k}]}\frac{1-{j_k}^{-5}}{\Delta t_{j_k}}\Delta_{j_k}|T_t^{j_k}|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}(t) -(1-{j_k}^{-5})|T_0|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n) < 1.
	\end{multline*}
	In particular, we have (using $1/4<1-j_k^{-5}$ for $j_k>1$ and $\Delta_{j_k}|T_t^{j_k}|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)\in\mathbb{R}_-$)
	\begin{multline*}
		\int_{[0,j_k-\Delta t_{j_k}]}\left(\int\frac{| \Psi_{\varepsilon_{j_k}}\ast\delta_\tau T_t^{j_k} |^2}{\Psi_{\varepsilon_{j_k}}\ast|T_t^{j_k}|_\tau+\varepsilon_{j_k}  }\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n-\frac{1}{\Delta t_{j_k}}\Delta_{j_k}|T_t^{j_k}|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)\right)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}(t)\\
		\leq 4\left( \int_{[0,j_k-\Delta t_{j_k}]}\left(\frac{1}{4}\int\frac{| \Psi_{\varepsilon_{j_k}}\ast\delta_\tau T_t^{j_k} |^2}{\Psi_{\varepsilon_{j_k}}\ast|T_t^{j_k}|_\tau+\varepsilon_{j_k}  }\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n-\frac{1-{j_k}^{-5}}{\Delta t_{j_k}}\Delta_{j_k}|T_t^{j_k}|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)\right)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}(t) \right)\\
		\leq 4\left( 1+|T_0|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)+(1-{j_k}^{-5})|T_0|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n) \right).
	\end{multline*}
	Hence, for every $R\in\mathbb{R}_+$ we get (eventually $R<j_k-\Delta t_{j_k}$)
	\begin{multline*}
		\limsup_k\int_{[0,R]}\left(\int\frac{| \Psi_{\varepsilon_{j_k}}\ast\delta_\tau T_t^{j_k} |^2}{\Psi_{\varepsilon_{j_k}}\ast|T_t^{j_k}|_\tau+\varepsilon_{j_k}  }\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n-\frac{1}{\Delta t_{j_k}}\Delta_{j_k}|T_t^{j_k}|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)\right)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}(t)\\
		\leq\limsup_k 4\left( 1+|T_0|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)+(1-{j_k}^{-5})|T_0|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n) \right)=4(1+2|T_0|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)).
	\end{multline*}
	Recall that $\Delta t_{j_k}\leq\varepsilon_{j_k}^{c_1}<(j_k^{-6})^{c_1}\ll j_k^{-2n}$.
	Since, by the above, we have that $\lim_k \int_{[0,R]}\frac{1}{\Delta t_{j_k}}\Delta_{j_k}|T_t^{j_k}|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}(t)$ exists, we obtain
	\begin{equation*}
		\lim_k \int_{[0,R]}\frac{-1}{j_k^{-2n}}\Delta_{j_k}|T_t^{j_k}|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}(t)=0
	\end{equation*}
	for all $R\in\mathbb{R}_+$. Therefore, the integrand (whose values lie in $\mathbb{R}_+$) converges to $0$ for a.e.\ $t\in\mathbb{R}_+$, which yields \cref{in1:6.20}.
<!-- end proof-10 -->
<!-- proof-11: body_md -->
\underline{STEP 1 (Application of Banach\textendash Alaoglu theorem):} Note that \cref{GrowthCondition} implies $\tau'(0)\geq C$.
	Hence, we have $|T|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)\geq C|T|(\mathbb{R}^n)$ for all $T\in\mathcal R_1(\mathbb{R}^n)$.
	                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                          
	In particular, we have $|T_t^j|(\mathbb{R}^n)\leq C^{-1}|T_t^j|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)\leq C^{-1}(|T_0|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)+1)$ by \cref{in1:6.3,in1:6.7}. Thus, the Banach\textendash Alaoglu theorem and a diagonal argument imply the existence of a subsequence $(j_k)$ and vector-valued measures $(T_t)_{t\in D}\subset \mathcal{M}(\mathbb{R}^n;\mathbb{R}^n)$ such that $T_t^{j_k}\stackrel{*}{\rightharpoonup} T_t$ for $k\to\infty$ for all $t\in D$ ($D$ defined as in the beginning of the proof of \cref{Prop6.4}). Note that this directly yields $\partial T_t=0$ for all $t\in D$ using also \cref{ZeroBoundary}.
	We can assume that the subsequence $(j_k)$ also satisfies \cref{in1:6.19}.
	To simplify the notation, we restrict $j$ to the indices $j_k$.
	Using $\textup{supp}(T_t^j)\subset\{ \textup{dist}(\cdot,\Omega)<2\varepsilon_j^{c_1-2} \}$ (\cref{Prop6.1}), it is not difficult to see that $\textup{supp}(T_t)\subset\Omega$ for all $t\in D$.
	
	\underline{STEP 2 (Continuity property with respect to flat norm):}
	Let $t_1,t_2\in D$ with $t_1<t_2$. As in the proof of \cref{Prop6.4}, we can always take $j$ sufficiently large such that $t_1,t_2\in\Delta t_j\mathbb N_0$, in particular $t_2=t_1+N_j\Delta t_j$ for $N_j\in\mathbb N$. 
	Now fix $j$ sufficiently large with this property.
	For $\ell=0,\ldots,N_j-1$ we have
	\begin{equation*}
		T_{t_1+(\ell+1)\Delta t_j}^j=(f_2^\ell)_\#(f_{1.5}^\ell)_\#(f_1^\ell)_\# T_{t_1+\ell\Delta t_j}^j,\quad\widetilde{T}_{t_1+\ell\Delta t_j}^j=(f_1^\ell)_\# T_{t_1+\ell\Delta t_j}^j,\quad\text{and}\quad\widehat{T}_{t_1+\ell\Delta t_j}^j=(f_{1.5}^\ell)\widetilde{T}_{t_1+\ell\Delta t_j}^j.
	\end{equation*}
	Application of the triangle inequality gives
	\begin{equation}
		\label[ineq]{in1:smartIneq}
		|T_{t_1}^j-T_{t_2}^j|_K^\flat\leq |T_{t_1+0\cdot\Delta t_j}^j-\widehat{T}_{t_1-1\cdot\Delta t_j}^j|_K^\flat+\sum_{\ell=0}^{N_j-1}|\widehat{T}_{t_1+(\ell-1)\Delta t_j}^j-\widehat{T}_{t_1+\ell\Delta t_j}^j|_K^\flat+|\widehat{T}_{t_1+(N_j-1)\Delta t_j}^j-T_{t_1+N_j\Delta t_j}^j|_K^\flat.
	\end{equation}
	(If $t_1=0$, then replace first two summands by $|T_0^j-\widehat{T}_0^j|_K^\flat$.)
	Here, we have chosen compact and convex $K\subset\mathbb{R}^n$ such that the supports of all the involved currents are contained in $K$. This is possible because (formally) deformation of type $f_{1.5}$ always follows deformations of type $f_2$ and $f_1$ (in the first step, only $f_1$) and $|\Delta t_jh_{\tau,\varepsilon_j}(\widehat{T})|\leq 2\varepsilon_j^{c_1-2}$ respectively $|f_1-\textup{id}|_\infty\leq j^{-2}$ (see \cref{AdmDeform}, also recall the definition of the homotopy $h_{f_1}$). Further, we can assume that always $\textup{supp}((h_{f_1})_\#(\llbracket 0,1\rrbracket\times T))\subset K$ (which we will use below) because
	\begin{equation*}
		\textup{supp}((h_{f_1})_\#(\llbracket 0,1\rrbracket\times T))\subset h_{f_1}(\textup{supp}(\llbracket 0,1\rrbracket\times T))=h_{f_1}(\textup{supp}(\llbracket 0,1\rrbracket)\times \textup{supp}(T)),
	\end{equation*}
	see \cite[pp.\ 359--360]{F}, thus (by the above) $\textup{supp}((h_{f_1})_\#(\llbracket 0,1\rrbracket\times T))\subset\{\textup{dist}(\cdot,\Omega) \leq  2\varepsilon_j^{c_1-2}+j^{-2} \}$.
	First, we claim that 
	\begin{equation}
		\label[ineq]{in1:Claim}
		|\widehat{T}_{t_1+(\ell-1)\Delta t_j}^j-\widehat{T}_{t_1+\ell\Delta t_j}^j|_K^\flat\leq |\widehat{T}_{t_1+(\ell-1)\Delta t_j}^j-T_{t_1+\ell\Delta t_j}^j|_K^\flat+|T_{t_1+\ell\Delta t_j}^j-(f_1^\ell)_\# T_{t_1+\ell\Delta t_j}^j|_K^\flat
	\end{equation}
	for all $\ell=0,\ldots,N_j-1$. For simplicity, write \cref{in1:Claim} formally as ($p$ for ``previous iteration'')
	\begin{equation}
		\label[ineq]{in1:PrevCur}
		|\widehat T_p-\widehat T|_K^\flat\leq 	|\widehat T_p- T|_K^\flat+	| T-(f_1)_\# T|_K^\flat,
	\end{equation}
	see \cref{fig4}.
	\begin{figure}
		\centering
		\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=2, transform shape=false]
			       
			\filldraw[draw=lightgray, fill=lightgray] (-2,0) -- (2,0) -- (2,-1) -- (-2,-1);
			\node at (0,-0.5) {$\textcolor{gray}{\Omega}$};
			\draw (-2,0) -- (2,0);
			
			            
			\node[fill,circle,minimum size=2mm,inner sep=0pt] at (-1,0) {};
			\node[fill,circle,minimum size=2mm,inner sep=0pt] at (-0.5,1) {};
			\node[fill,circle,minimum size=2mm,inner sep=0pt] at (1,2) {};
			\node[fill,circle,minimum size=2mm,inner sep=0pt] at (1,0) {};
			
			            
			\draw (-1,0) -- (-0.5,1);
			\draw (-0.5,1) -- (1,2) ;
			                               
			 
			         
			\node at (-1.2,0.2) {$\widehat T_p$};
			\node at (-1.2,1) {$T=(f_2^p)_\#\widehat T_p$};
			\node at (1.6,2) {$\widetilde T=(f_1)_\# T$};
			\node at (1.6,0.2) {$\widehat T=(f_{1.5})_\#\widetilde T$};
			
			\node at (-0.5,0.5) {$Q_1$};
			\node at (0.63,1.5) {$Q_2$};
		\end{tikzpicture}
		\caption{Formal sketch of the currents from \cref{in1:PrevCur}. Each $k$-current is indicated as a $(k-1)$-dimensional set.}
		\label{fig4}
	\end{figure}
	Now recall that \cite[pp.\ 367]{F} (a minimizer always exists by \cite[pp.\ 368]{F})
	\begin{equation*}
		|\overline{T}|_K^\flat=\min\{ \mathbb M(\overline{T}-\partial Q)+\mathbb M(Q)\:|\: Q\in\mathcal{D}_2(\mathbb{R}^n),\textup{supp}(Q)\subset K \}\in[0,\infty]
	\end{equation*} 
	for all $\overline{T}\in\mathcal{D}_1(\mathbb{R}^n)$ with $\textup{supp}(\overline{T})\subset K$.
	Let $Q_1\in\mathcal N_2(\mathbb{R}^n)$ be a minimizer of $|\widehat T_p- T|_K^\flat$ and $Q_2\in\mathcal N_2(\mathbb{R}^n)$ be a minimizer of $| T-(f_1)_\# T|_K^\flat$. 
	We obtain (using also that the support of $\widehat T_p$ is contained in $\Omega$)
	\begin{multline*}
		|\widehat T_p-\widehat T|_K^\flat\leq \mathbb M((f_{1.5})_\# (Q_1+Q_2))+\mathbb M(\partial ((f_{1.5})_\# (Q_1+Q_2))-(\widehat T_p-\widehat T))\\
		=\mathbb M((f_{1.5})_\# (Q_1+Q_2))+\mathbb M((f_{1.5})_\#(\partial (Q_1+Q_2)-(\widehat T_p-\widetilde T)))\\
		\leq \mathbb M(Q_1+Q_2)+\mathbb M(\partial (Q_1+Q_2)-(\widehat T_p-\widetilde T))=\mathbb M(Q_1+Q_2)+\mathbb M(\partial (Q_1+Q_2)-(\widehat T_p-T+T-\widetilde T))\\
		\leq \mathbb M(Q_1)+\mathbb M(\partial Q_1-(\widehat T_p-T))+\mathbb M(Q_2)+\mathbb M(\partial Q_2-(T-\widetilde T))=|\widehat T_p- T|_K^\flat+	| T-(f_1)_\# T|_K^\flat.
	\end{multline*}
	This shows \cref{in1:Claim}. 
	(If $t_1=0$, then $|T_0^j-\widehat T_0^j|_K^\flat\leq |T_0^j-(f_1^0)_\# T_0^j|_K^\flat$ using a similar argument.)
	Thus, \cref{in1:smartIneq} can be written (if $t_1=0$, then replace first three summands by $|T_0^j-(f_1^0)_\# T_0^j|_K^\flat$)
	\begin{multline}
		\label[ineq]{in1:smartIneq2}
		|T_{t_1}^j-T_{t_2}^j|_K^\flat\leq |T_{t_1+0\cdot\Delta t_j}^j-\widehat{T}_{t_1-1\cdot\Delta t_j}^j|_K^\flat+\sum_{\ell=0}^{N_j-1}\left(|\widehat{T}_{t_1+(\ell-1)\Delta t_j}^j-T_{t_1+\ell\Delta t_j}^j|_K^\flat+|T_{t_1+\ell\Delta t_j}^j-(f_1^\ell)_\# T_{t_1+\ell\Delta t_j}^j|_K^\flat\right)\\
		+|\widehat{T}_{t_1+(N_j-1)\Delta t_j}^j-T_{t_1+N_j\Delta t_j}^j|_K^\flat.
	\end{multline}
	Next, we estimate ($f_2^{-1}$ corresponds to the deformation prior to $f_1^0$)
	\begin{itemize}
		\item $|T_{t_1+\ell\Delta t_j}^j-(f_1^\ell)_\# T_{t_1+\ell\Delta t_j}^j|_K^\flat$ for $\ell=0,1,\ldots,N_j-1$ and
		\item $|\widehat{T}_{t_1+(\ell-1)\Delta t_j}^j-T_{t_1+\ell\Delta t_j}^j|_K^\flat=|\widehat{T}_{t_1+(\ell-1)\Delta t_j}^j-(f_2^{\ell-1})_\#\widehat{T}_{t_1+(\ell-1)\Delta t_j}^j|_K^\flat$ for $\ell=0,1,\ldots,N_j$.
	\end{itemize}
	(If $t_1=0$, then $\ell$ starts from $1$ in second bullet point.) Let us start by considering the terms $|T_{t_1+\ell\Delta t_j}^j-(f_1^\ell)_\# T_{t_1+\ell\Delta t_j}^j|_K^\flat$ for $\ell=0,1,\ldots,N_j-1$. To simplify the notation, we write them (formally) as $|T-(f_1)_\# T|_K^\flat$. The homotopy formula for currents is applicable to $T-(f_1)_\# T$ by \cite[p.\ 371]{F} (essentially because $T\in \mathbb F_1(\mathbb{R}^n)$). It is stated on \cite[p.\ 363]{F},
	\begin{equation}
		\label{homotopy2}
		(f_1)_\# T-T=\partial h_\#(\llbracket 0,1\rrbracket\times T)+ h_\#(\llbracket 0,1\rrbracket\times \partial T),
	\end{equation}
	where $h=h_{f_1}$. Now we use that $\partial T=0$ by \cref{ZeroBoundary}. The current $\llbracket 0,1\rrbracket\times 0\in\mathcal{D}_1(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^n)$ is uniquely characterized by the conditions on \cite[p.\ 360]{F}. Using these, it is not difficult to show that $\llbracket 0,1\rrbracket\times \partial T=0$, thus the last term in \cref{homotopy2} vanishes. Now recall that always $|\cdot|_K^\flat\circ\partial\leq|\cdot|_K^\flat$ \cite[p.\ 367]{F}. 
	We obtain (using $Q=0$ in the formula from above)
	\begin{multline*}
		|(f_1)_\# T-T|_{K}^\flat=|\partial h_\#(\llbracket 0,1\rrbracket\times T)|_{K}^\flat \leq | h_\#(\llbracket 0,1\rrbracket\times T)|_{K}^\flat \leq\mathbb M(h_\#(\llbracket 0,1\rrbracket\times T))\\
		\leq j^{-1}(|T|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)-|(f_1)_\# T|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)),
	\end{multline*}
	where the last inequality follows from \cref{AdmDeform}.
	Using this and \cref{in1:6.4,in1:6.3}, we get                                                        
	\begin{multline}
		\sum_{\ell=0}^{N_j-1}|T_{t_1+\ell\Delta t_j}^j-(f_1^\ell)_\# T_{t_1+\ell\Delta t_j}^j|_K^\flat\leq j^{-1}\sum_{\ell=0}^{N_j-1}(|T_{t_1+\ell\Delta t_j}^j|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)-|(f_1^\ell)_\# T_{t_1+\ell\Delta t_j}^j|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n))\\
		\leq j^{-1}\sum_{\ell=0}^{N_j-1}|\Delta_j |T^{j,t_1/\Delta t_j+\ell}|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)|\\
		\leq (1-j^{-5})^{-1} j^{-1}\sum_{\ell=0}^{N_j-1}(|T^{j,t_1/\Delta t_j+\ell}|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)-|T^{j,t_1/\Delta t_j+\ell+1}|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n))+(1-j^{-5})^{-1}j^{-1}\sum_{\ell=0}^{N_j-1}\Delta t_j\varepsilon_j^{1/8}\\
		\leq 2j^{-1}|T^{j,t_1/\Delta t_j}|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)+2j^{-1}\varepsilon_j^{1/8}(t_2-t_1)\leq 2j^{-1}(|T_0|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)+\varepsilon_j^{1/8}t_1)+2j^{-1}\varepsilon_j^{1/8}(t_2-t_1).\label[ineq]{in1:FirstSum2}
	\end{multline}
	Next, we estimate the terms $|\widehat{T}_{t_1+(\ell-1)\Delta t_j}^j-(f_2^{\ell-1})_\#\widehat{T}_{t_1+(\ell-1)\Delta t_j}^j|_K^\flat$ for $\ell=0,1,\ldots,N_j$.
	As before, we (formally) write $|\widehat{T}_{t_1+(\ell-1)\Delta t_j}^j-(f_2^{\ell-1})_\#\widehat{T}_{t_1+(\ell-1)\Delta t_j}^j|_K^\flat$ as $|\widehat{T}-(f_2)_\#\widehat{T}|_K^\flat$.
	We apply \cite[Rmk.\ 4.1.13]{F} with $g=f_2,f=\textup{id},$ and
	\begin{equation*}
		A=\{ (1-t)x+tf_2(x)\:|\:x\in\textup{supp}(\widehat{T}),t\in[0,1] \}\subset K.
	\end{equation*}
	Using \cite[p.\ 359]{F}, we obtain
	\begin{equation*}
		\textup{supp}((f_2)_\#\widehat{T}-\widehat{T})\subset\textup{supp}((f_2)_\#\widehat{T})\cup\textup{supp}(\widehat{T})\subset f_2(\textup{supp}(\widehat{T}))\cup\textup{supp}(\widehat{T})\subset A.
	\end{equation*}
	Note that (using \cite[p.\ 367]{F}) $|(f_2)_\#\widehat{T}-\widehat{T}|_{K}^\flat\leq |(f_2)_\#\widehat{T}-\widehat{T}|_A^\flat$. Application of \cite[Rmk.\ 4.1.13]{F} gives
	\begin{multline*}
		|(f_2)_\#\widehat{T}-\widehat{T}|_{K}^\flat\leq |(f_2)_\#\widehat{T}-\widehat{T}|_A^\flat\leq \Delta t_j\int |h_{\tau,\varepsilon_j}(\widehat{T})|\max\{ 1,|\mathbb 1+\Delta t_j\mathrm{D} h_{\tau,\varepsilon_j}(\widehat{T})|_2 \}\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}|\widehat{T} |\\
		\leq \Delta t_j(1+\Delta t_j4\varepsilon_j^{-4})\int |h_{\tau,\varepsilon_j}(\widehat{T})|\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}|\widehat{T} |,
	\end{multline*}
	where we also used \cref{hEstimates} (which is applicable by the beginning of the proof of \cref{Prop6.1}). 
	This gives (using $\Delta t_j\varepsilon_j^{-4}\leq\varepsilon_j^{c_1-4}\leq 1$)
	\begin{multline*}
		|T_{t_1+0\cdot\Delta t_j}^j-\widehat{T}_{t_1-1\cdot\Delta t_j}^j|_K^\flat+\sum_{\ell=0}^{N_j}|\widehat{T}_{t_1+(\ell-1)\Delta t_j}^j-T_{t_1+\ell\Delta t_j}^j|_K^\flat\\
		=|(f_2^{-1})_\#\widehat{T}_{t_1-1\cdot\Delta t_j}^j-\widehat{T}_{t_1-1\cdot\Delta t_j}^j|_K^\flat+\sum_{\ell=0}^{N_j}|\widehat{T}_{t_1+(\ell-1)\Delta t_j}^j-(f_2^{\ell-1})_\#\widehat{T}_{t_1+(\ell-1)\Delta t_j}^j|_K^\flat\\
		\leq 10\sum_{\ell=0}^{N_j}\Delta t_j\int |h_{\tau,\varepsilon_j}(\widehat{T}_{t_1+(\ell-1)\Delta t_j}^j)|\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}|\widehat{T}_{t_1+(\ell-1)\Delta t_j}^j|=10\int_{[t_1-2\Delta t_j,t_2-\Delta t_j]}\int |h_{\tau,\varepsilon_j}(\widehat{T}_t^j)|\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}|\widehat{T}_t^j |\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}(t).
	\end{multline*}
	By \cref{GrowthCondition}, $f_1^\ell\in\mathcal{A}_j(T_{t_1+\ell\Delta t_j}^j)$, and \cref{in1:6.3}, we have
	\begin{multline*}
		|\widehat{T}_t^j|(\mathbb{R}^n)=|(f_{1.5})_\# \widetilde{T}_t^j|(\mathbb{R}^n)\leq | \widetilde{T}_t^j|(\mathbb{R}^n)=| (f_1^\ell)_\# T_{t_1+\ell\Delta t_j}^j|(\mathbb{R}^n)\leq C^{-1} |(f_1^\ell)_\# T_{t_1+\ell\Delta t_j}^j|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)\leq\\
		C^{-1}|T_{t_1+\ell\Delta t_j}^j|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)\leq C^{-1}(|T_0|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)+\varepsilon_j^{1/8}(t_1+\ell\Delta t_j))
	\end{multline*}
	if $t\in( t_1+(\ell-1)\Delta t_j,t_1+\ell\Delta t_j]$. 
	In particular, we obtain (using the first inequality in \labelcref{in1:6.7})
	\begin{equation*}
		|\widehat{T}_t^j|(\mathbb{R}^n)\leq  C^{-1}(|T_0|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)+\varepsilon_j^{1/8}j)<  C^{-1}M 
	\end{equation*}
	for all $t\in [t_1-2\Delta t_j,t_2-\Delta t_j]$ (see integral above), where $M=|T_0|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)+1$.
	Using the above estimate and the Cauchy\textendash Schwarz inequality, we have 
	\begin{multline*}
		10\int_{[t_1-2\Delta t_j,t_2-\Delta t_j]}\int |h_{\tau,\varepsilon_j}(\widehat{T}_t^j)|\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}|\widehat{T}_t^j |\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}(t)\\
		\leq 10C^{-1/2}M^{1/2} \int_{[t_1-2\Delta t_j,t_2-\Delta t_j]}  \left(\int |h_{\tau,\varepsilon_j}(\widehat{T}_t^j)|^2\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}|\widehat{T}_t^j |\right)^{1/2}\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}(t).
	\end{multline*}
	Note that also $|\widehat T_t^j |(\phi)\leq C^{-1}|\widehat T_t^j |_\tau(\phi)$ for all $\phi\in C_c(\mathbb{R}^n)$ by \cref{GrowthCondition} (used in first inequality below).
	Further, we can use \cref{in1:5.24} (in second inequality below) since 
	\begin{equation*}
		|\widehat{T}_t^j|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)\leq|(f_1^\ell)_\# T_{t_1+\ell\Delta t_j}^j|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)\leq |T_{t_1+\ell\Delta t_j}^j|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)\leq M
	\end{equation*}
	if $t\in( t_1+(\ell-1)\Delta t_j,t_1+\ell\Delta t_j]$ (see \cref{in1:6.11,in1:6.111}). This yields 
	\begin{multline*}
		10C^{-1/2}M^{1/2} \int_{[t_1-2\Delta t_j,t_2-\Delta t_j]}  \left(\int |h_{\tau,\varepsilon_j}(\widehat{T}_t^j)|^2\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}|\widehat{T}_t^j |\right)^{1/2}\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}(t)\\
		\leq 10C^{-1}M^{1/2} \int_{[t_1-2\Delta t_j,t_2-\Delta t_j]}  \left(\int |h_{\tau,\varepsilon_j}(\widehat{T}_t^j)|^2\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}|\widehat{T}_t^j |_\tau\right)^{1/2}\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}(t)\\
		\leq 10C^{-1}M^{1/2}\int_{[t_1-2\Delta t_j,t_2-\Delta t_j]} \left(    (1+\varepsilon_j^{1/4})\int\frac{|\Psi_{\varepsilon_j}\ast\delta_\tau \widehat{T}_t^j|^2}{\Psi_{\varepsilon_j}\ast|\widehat{T}_t^j|_\tau+\varepsilon_j}\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n+\varepsilon_j^{1/4}    \right)^{1/2}\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}(t)\\
		\leq 10C^{-1}M^{1/2}
		\int_{[t_1-2\Delta t_j,t_2-\Delta t_j]}  \left(    (1+\varepsilon_j^{1/4})\left( \int\frac{|\Psi_{\varepsilon_j}\ast\delta_\tau (f_2^{\ell(t)})_\#\widehat{T}_t^j|^2}{\Psi_{\varepsilon_j}\ast|(f_2^{\ell(t)})_\#\widehat{T}_t^j|_\tau+\varepsilon_j}\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n +\varepsilon_j^{c_1-3n-15} \right)+\varepsilon_j^{1/4}    \right)^{1/2}\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}(t)\\
		\leq 10C^{-1}M^{1/2}
		\int_{[t_1-2\Delta t_j,t_2-\Delta t_j]}  (1+\varepsilon_j^{1/8})\left(\left( \int\frac{|\Psi_{\varepsilon_j}\ast\delta_\tau (f_2^{\ell})_\#\widehat{T}_t^j|^2}{\Psi_{\varepsilon_j}\ast|(f_2^{\ell})_\#\widehat{T}_t^j|_\tau+\varepsilon_j}\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n\right)^{1/2} +\varepsilon_j^{(c_1-3n-15)/2} \right)+\varepsilon_j^{1/8}\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L},
	\end{multline*}
	where we also applied \cref{in1:Q4} (in the third inequality) and the subadditivity of the square root (last inequality). 
	Here $\ell=\ell(t)$ is such that $t\in( t_1+(\ell(t)-1)\Delta t_j,t_1+\ell(t)\Delta t_j]$.
	Application of the Cauchy\textendash Schwarz inequality gives
	\begin{multline*}
		\int_{[t_1-2\Delta t_j,t_2-\Delta t_j]}   \left( \int\frac{|\Psi_{\varepsilon_j}\ast\delta_\tau (f_2^{\ell(t)})_\#\widehat{T}_t^j|^2}{\Psi_{\varepsilon_j}\ast|(f_2^{\ell(t)})_\#\widehat{T}_t^j|_\tau+\varepsilon_j}\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n\right)^{1/2} \textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}(t)\\
		\leq (t_2-t_1+\Delta t_j)^{1/2}\left(\int_{[t_1-2\Delta t_j,t_2-\Delta t_j]}  \int\frac{|\Psi_{\varepsilon_j}\ast\delta_\tau (f_2^{\ell(t)})_\#\widehat{T}_t^j|^2}{\Psi_{\varepsilon_j}\ast|(f_2^{\ell(t)})_\#\widehat{T}_t^j|_\tau+\varepsilon_j}\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n \textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}(t)\right)^{1/2}.
	\end{multline*}
	The latter integral is bounded by some $C_*\in\mathbb{R}_+$ (\cref{in1:6.19}). In summary, using \cref{in1:smartIneq2,in1:FirstSum2} plus the above, we have
	\begin{multline}
		|T_{t_1}^j-T_{t_2}^j|_K^\flat\leq                                                                                                                                                                                                                                      
		                                                               
		\sum_{\ell=0}^{N_j-1}|T_{t_1+\ell\Delta t_j}^j-(f_1^\ell)_\# T_{t_1+\ell\Delta t_j}^j|_K^\flat
		+|T_{t_1+0\cdot\Delta t_j}^j-\widehat{T}_{t_1-1\cdot\Delta t_j}^j|_K^\flat+\sum_{\ell=0}^{N_j}|\widehat{T}_{t_1+(\ell-1)\Delta t_j}^j-T_{t_1+\ell\Delta t_j}^j|_K^\flat\\
		\leq 2j^{-1}(|T_0|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)+\varepsilon_j^{1/8}t_1)+2j^{-1}\varepsilon_j^{1/8}(t_2-t_1)+|T_{t_1+0\cdot\Delta t_j}^j-\widehat{T}_{t_1-1\cdot\Delta t_j}^j|_K^\flat+\sum_{\ell=0}^{N_j}|\widehat{T}_{t_1+(\ell-1)\Delta t_j}^j-T_{t_1+\ell\Delta t_j}^j|_K^\flat\\
		\leq 2j^{-1}(|T_0|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)+\varepsilon_j^{1/8}t_1)+2j^{-1}\varepsilon_j^{1/8}(t_2-t_1)+10C^{-1}M^{1/2}\cdot\\
		\left( (1+\varepsilon_j^{1/8})(t_2-t_1+\Delta t_j)^{1/2}C_*+(1+\varepsilon_j^{1/8})\varepsilon_j^{(c_1-3n-15)/2}(t_2-t_1+\Delta t_j)+\varepsilon_j^{1/8}(t_2-t_1+\Delta t_j)  \right)\\
		\leq 2j^{-1}M+2\varepsilon_j^{1/8}+10C^{-1}M^{1/2}
		\left( 2(t_2-t_1)^{1/2}C_*+2\varepsilon_j^{5/2}(t_2-t_1)+\varepsilon_j^{1/8}(t_2-t_1)  \right).\label[ineq]{in1:lastIn}
	\end{multline}
	This gives the following continuity property: If $\varepsilon>0$ and $t_1\in D$, then there exists $\delta>0$ such that for each $t_2\in D$ with $|t_1-t_2|<\delta$ there is some $j_0\in\mathbb N$ such that $|T_{t_1}^j-T_{t_2}^j|_K^\flat<\varepsilon$ for all $j\geq j_0$.
	
	\underline{STEP 3 (Continuation to $t\in\mathbb{R}_+$):} Let $s\in\mathbb{R}_+\setminus D$. Pick arbitrary sequences $(s_i),(\bar s_j)\subset D$ with $s_i\to s$ and $\bar s_j\to s$. Recall that (see STEP 1 and beginning of STEP 2) $\mathbb M(T)+\mathbb M(\partial T)\leq C^{-1}(|T_0|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)+1)$ and $\textup{supp}(T)\subset K$ for $T=T_{s_i}$ or $T=T_{\bar s_j}$. Using Federer's compactness theorem \cite[Thm.\ 4.2.17]{F}, we obtain
	\begin{equation*}
		|T_{s_{i_\ell}}-T|_K^\flat+|T_{\bar s_{j_\ell}}-\overline T|_K^\flat\to 0
	\end{equation*}
	for $\ell\to\infty$ for certain $T,\overline{T}\in\mathcal N_1(\mathbb{R}^n)$ with $\partial T=\partial \overline{T}=0$ and subsequences $(s_{i_\ell})\subset (s_{i})$ and $(\tilde s_{j_\ell})\subset (\tilde s_{j})$.
	We have
	\begin{equation*}
		|T-\overline{T}|_K^\flat\leq |T-T_{s_{i_\ell}}|_K^\flat+|T_{s_{i_\ell}}-T_{s_{i_\ell}}^j|_K^\flat+|T_{s_{i_\ell}}^j-T_{\bar s_{j_\ell}}^j|_K^\flat+|T_{\bar s_{j_\ell}}^j-T_{\bar s_{j_\ell}}|_K^\flat+|T_{\bar s_{j_\ell}}-\overline{T}|_K^\flat.
	\end{equation*}
	Now the right-hand side can be made arbitrarily small by choosing $\ell, j$ sufficiently large (invoking also STEP 2). Recall that the weak-$*$ convergence of the sequences $(T_t^j)$ for $j\to\infty$ ($t\in D$) implies the convergence with respect to $|\cdot|_K^\flat$ because (as above) $\mathbb M(T_t^j)+\mathbb M(\partial T_t^j)\leq C^{-1}(|T_0|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)+1), \textup{supp}(T_t^j)\subset K$, and $T_t^j\in\mathcal N_1(\mathbb{R}^n)$ (see, for example, the summary in \cite[Rmk.\ 3]{MW19}). We set $T_s=T=\overline{T}$. Finally, it is not difficult to check that $\textup{supp}(T_s)\subset\Omega$ (because this is true for the $T_{s_i}$ by STEP 1).
	
	\underline{STEP 4 (Hölder continuity of $t\mapsto T_t$):} Let $s,t\in\mathbb{R}_+$ and $(s_i),(t_i)\subset D$ with $s_i\to s$ and $t_i\to t$. Fix $\varepsilon>0$. Using the triangle inequality and \cref{in1:lastIn}, we get
	\begin{equation*}
		|T_s-T_t|_K^\flat\leq |T_{s_i}-T_{t_i}|_K^\flat+\varepsilon\leq C_{i,j}+C_0|s_i-t_i|^{1/2}+\varepsilon
	\end{equation*}
	for $i,j$ sufficiently large, where $C_0\in\mathbb{R}_+$ is a constant and $(C_{i,j})\subset\mathbb{R}_+^*$ with $C_{i,j}\to 0$ for $j\to \infty$. Letting $j\to \infty$ and then $i\to \infty$ yields (since $\varepsilon>0$ was arbitrary)
	\begin{equation*}
		|T_s-T_t|_K^\flat\leq C_0|s-t|^{1/2}.
	\end{equation*}
	\underline{STEP 5 ($T_s^j\stackrel{*}{\rightharpoonup} T_s$ for all $s\in\mathbb{R}_+\setminus D$):} Fix $s\in\mathbb{R}_+\setminus D$. Note that $s\in\mathbb{R}_+^*$. For every $j\in\mathbb N$ with $s\in[0,j]$ we define $s_j=(\ell_j+1)\Delta t_j$, where $\ell_j\in\mathbb N_0$ is chosen such that $s\in(\ell_j\Delta t_j,(\ell_j+1)\Delta t_j]$. By construction we get $s_j\to s$ and $T_{s_j}^j=T_s^j$. 
	We have (triangle inequality) 
	\begin{equation*}
		|T_s-T_s^j|_K^\flat\leq |T_s-T_{s_j}|_K^\flat+|T_{s_j}-T_{\tilde s}|_K^\flat+|T_{\tilde s}-T_{\tilde s}^j|_K^\flat+|T_{\tilde s}^j-T_{s_j}^j|_K^\flat+|T_{s_j}^j-T_s^j|_K^\flat
	\end{equation*}
	for all $\tilde s\in\mathbb{R}_+$.
	The first summand on the right-hand side goes to zero by $s_j\to s$. The second term can be made arbitrarily small for $\tilde s\in D$ sufficiently close to $s_j$ and $j$ sufficiently large (STEP 4). The third term converges to zero by $\tilde s\in D$. For the fourth term we can use \cref{in1:lastIn} again\footnote{If $\tilde s=i2^{-k}$, then $\tilde s\in2^{-j}\mathbb N_0\subset 2^{-p_j}\mathbb N_0=\Delta t_j\mathbb N_0$ for $j\geq k$ due to $p_j\geq j$ (\cref{Prop6.1}).}. The last term is equal to zero by $T_{s_j}^j=T_s^j$. This shows $|T_s-T_s^j|_K^\flat\to 0$ implying the weak-$*$ convergence (since all the involved currents are normal $1$-currents with uniformly bounded mass and zero boundary and supports lying in $K$).
	\underline{$T_0\in\mathcal I_1(\mathbb{R}^n)\implies T_t\in\mathcal I_1(\mathbb{R}^n)$ for all $t\in\mathbb{R}_+$:} The property of being a transportation network is stable with respect to deformations of type $f_1,f_{1.5}$, and $f_2$ (recall the proof of \cref{Prop6.1}). Hence, all the currents $T_t^j=T^{j,\lceil t/\Delta t_j\rceil}$ ($t\in[0,j]$) from \cref{Prop6.4} are transportation networks. By the representation of the multiplicity in \cref{FedererPushforward}, we also have $T_t^j\in \mathcal{I}_1(\mathbb{R}^n)$ for all $t\in[0,j]$ if $T_0\in\mathcal I_1(\mathbb{R}^n)$. Finally, STEPS 1 and 3 together with the closure theorem for integral currents \cite[Thm.\ 8.12]{FF60} imply the claim.
<!-- end proof-11 -->
<!-- lem-9: body_md -->
\label{GrowthSBall}
	Let $S\subset\mathbb{R}^n$ be countably $1$-rectifiable\footnote{There exist countably many Lipschitz functions $f_i:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}^n$ such that $\mathcal H^1(S\setminus\bigcup_if_i(\mathbb{R}))=0$.} and $x\in\mathbb{R}^n$. Define
	\begin{equation*}
		f(r)=\mathcal H^1(\overline{B}_r(x)\cap S)\in[0,\infty]
	\end{equation*}
	for $r\in\mathbb{R}_+$. Then we have $f'(r)=0$ or $f'(r)\geq 1$ for a.e.\ $r\in\textup{dom}(f)$. In particular, if $f(R)<\lambda R$ for some $\lambda\in(0,1]$ and $R\in\mathbb{R}_+$, then $\mathcal{L}([0,R]\cap \{ f'=0 \})> (1-\lambda)R$.
	Further, if $T=\theta\mathcal{H}^1\mathbin{\vrule height 1.3ex depth 0pt width 0.13ex\vrule height 0.13ex depth 0pt width 1ex} S\in\mathcal I_1(\mathbb{R}^n)$ and $\kappa:\mathbb{R}_+\to\mathbb{R}_+$ a transportation cost, then
	\begin{equation*}
		g(r)=\int_{\overline{B}_r(x)\cap S}\kappa(|\theta|)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{H}^1
	\end{equation*}
	satisfies $g'(r)=0$ or $g'(r)\geq \kappa(1)$ for a.e.\ $r\in\textup{dom}(g)$. In particular, if $g(R)<\lambda R$ for some $\lambda\in(0,\tau(1)]$ and $R\in\mathbb{R}_+$, then $\mathcal{L}([0,R]\cap \{ g'=0 \})> (1-\lambda/\kappa(1))R$.
<!-- end lem-9 -->
<!-- proof-12: body_md -->
Let $\lambda\in(0,1]$ and $R\in\mathbb{R}_+$ such that $f(R)<\lambda R$. 
	We show $\mathcal{L}([0,R]\cap \{ f'=0 \})> (1-\lambda)R$ assuming that we already know that $f'(r)=0$ or $f'(r)\geq 1$ for a.e.\ $r\in\textup{dom}(f)$. 
	Write $B=[0,R]\cap \{ f'=0 \}$. Note that $f$ is non-decreasing. Thus 
	\begin{equation*}
		\lambda R>f(R)\geq\int_{[0,R]}f'(r)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}(r)=\int_{B}f'(r)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}(r)+\int_{[0,R]\setminus B}f'(r)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}(r)\geq \int_{[0,R]\setminus B}1\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}(r)=R-\mathcal{L}(B) ,
	\end{equation*}
	This gives $\mathcal{L}([0,R]\cap \{ f'=0 \})> (1-\lambda)R$.\\ 
	To show $f'(r)=0$ or $f'(r)\geq 1$ for a.e.\ $r\in\textup{dom}(f)$, we apply \cite[Lem.\ 3.2.18]{F} (note that $S$ is countably $(\mathcal H^1,1)$ rectifiable in the sense of \cite[Def.\ 3.2.14]{F}). Up to some $\mathcal H^1$-zero set (which we can neglect by definition of $f$), we obtain $S=\bigcup_j\psi_j(K_j)$, where $K_j\subset\mathbb{R}$ compact, $\psi_j:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}^n$ Lipschitz, and $\psi_j(K_j)$ mutually disjoint ($j\in\mathbb N$). Now define $S_j=\psi_j(K_j)$ and $f_j(r)=\mathcal H^1(\overline{B}_r(x)\cap S_j)$. This gives (because $f=\sum_{j }f_j$ with non-decreasing $f_j$, cf. \cite[Ex.\ 222Y (a)]{Frem03})
	\begin{equation*}
		f'(r)=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}f_j'(r)
	\end{equation*}
	for a.e.\ $r\in\textup{dom}(f)$. Hence, for each $j\in\mathbb N$, we only need to show that $f_j'(r)=0$ or $f_j'(r)\geq 1$ for a.e.\ $r\in\textup{dom}(f)$. By \cite[Thm.\ 1.8, Ch.\ 2, \S 1]{LS} (with $A=(\overline{B}_{r+h}(x)\setminus\overline{B}_r(x))\cap S_j$ and $|\cdot|:A\to(r,r+h]$) we get (for $h\in\mathbb{R}_+^*$)
	\begin{equation*}
		\frac{f_j(r+h)-f_j(r)}{h}=\frac{1}{h}\mathcal H^1((\overline{B}_{r+h}(x)\setminus\overline{B}_r(x))\cap S_j)\geq\frac{1}{h}\int_{(r,r+h]}\mathcal H^0(\partial B_s(x)\cap S_j)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{H}^1(s).
	\end{equation*}
	By Lebesgue's differentiation theorem the right-hand side converges to $\mathcal H^0(\partial B_r(x)\cap S_j)$ for a.e.\ $r\in\textup{dom}(f)$. Hence, we only need to show that $f_j'(r)>0$ implies $\mathcal H^0(\partial B_r(x)\cap S_j)>0$ for a.e.\ $r\in\textup{dom}(f_j)$ (namely, those $r$ for which the conclusion of Lebesgue's differentiation theorem holds and $f_j'(r)$ exists). If we had $f_j'(r)>0$ and $\mathcal H^0(\partial B_r(x)\cap S_j)=0$ for some $r\in \textup{dom}(f)$, then $\textup{dist}(\partial B_r(x),S_j)>0$ (using the closedness of $S_j$). This would yield a contradiction because then $f_j'(r)=0$.\\
	To show the last part, we define $g_j(r)=\int_{\overline{B}_r(x)\cap S_j}\kappa(|\theta|)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{H}^1$. Now we can use exactly the same arguments as above together with the estimate (recall that $|\theta|\in\mathbb N$ $\mathcal{H}^1\mathbin{\vrule height 1.3ex depth 0pt width 0.13ex\vrule height 0.13ex depth 0pt width 1ex} S$-a.e.)
	\begin{equation*}
		\frac{g_j(r+h)-g_j(r)}{h}=\frac{1}{h}\int_{(\overline{B}_{r+h}(x)\setminus\overline{B}_r(x))\cap S_j}\kappa(|\theta|)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{H}^1 \geq\frac{\kappa(1)}{h}\int_{(r,r+h]}\mathcal H^0(\partial B_s(x)\cap S_j)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{H}^1(s).\qedhere
	\end{equation*}
	\begin{comment}
                               
                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                          
                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                      
                                                                                                               
                   
                                   
                 
                                                                                                                                         
                                                                                                                                                                                                           
                                                        
                   
                                                                                                                                                                                              
                              
                 
                                 
                 
                                                                                                                                                                               
                                                                
                                                                           
                                    
                    
                                                                                             
                  
                                                                                                                                                                                                                                               
                                                               
                    
                                                                                                                    
                  
                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                  
               
 \end{comment}
<!-- end proof-12 -->
<!-- examp-2: body_md -->
\label{Casek2}
	In general, the statement is not true for $k>1$, countably $k$-rectifiable $S\subset\mathbb{R}^n$, and $\mathcal{H}^1$ replaced by $\mathcal H^k$, i.e.\ $f(r)=\mathcal H^k(\overline{B}_r(x)\cap S)$ for $r\in\mathbb{R}_+$.
	For example, take $k=2,\ x=0$, and $S=\mathbb{R}\times[-\ell,\ell]\times\{ 0 \}$ for some $\ell\in(0,1/5)$, see \cref{fig5}.
	Then $4\ell<f'(r)<5\ell \in(0,1)$ for $r\in(\ell,\infty)$ sufficiently large because the mean curvature of $\partial B_r(0)$ is decreasing.
	The assumption $k=1$ was used in the step before Lebesgue's differentiation theorem was applied (for $\mathcal H^k$ in place of $\mathcal{H}^1=\mathcal{L}$ on $(r,r+h]$, the right-hand side of the inequality would be equal to zero).
<!-- end examp-2 -->
<!-- coro-2: body_md -->
\label{Besicovitch}
	Let $A\in\mathcal B(\mathbb{R}^n)$ and $\mu\in\mathcal{M}_+(\mathbb{R}^n)$. Assume that $\mathcal F\subset \mathcal B(\mathbb{R}^n)$ is a Besicovitch cover of $A$. Then there exists a sequence $(B_i)\subset\mathcal F$ of mutually disjoint balls $B_i$ such that 
	\begin{equation*}
		\mu(A)\leq\mathbb B_n\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\mu(B_i),
	\end{equation*}
	where $\mathbb B_n$ denotes the Besicovitch constant.
<!-- end coro-2 -->
<!-- prop-8: body_md -->
\label{Lemma1}
	Let the setup be as in \cref{Prop6.1,Prop6.4}. Fix $t\in\mathbb{R}_+$ and take $k$ sufficiently large such that $t\in[0,j_k]$. Define $r_k=j_k^{-5/2}$ and (write $T_t^{j_k}=\theta_t^{j_k}\mathcal{H}^1\mathbin{\vrule height 1.3ex depth 0pt width 0.13ex\vrule height 0.13ex depth 0pt width 1ex} S_t^{j_k}$)
	\begin{equation*}
		X_t^{k}=\left\{ x\in S_t^{k}\:\middle|\: |T_t^{j_k}|_\tau(\overline{B}_r(x))\geq \lambda r\textup{ for all }r\in(0,r_k) \right\}.
	\end{equation*} 
	Then $X_t^{k}$ is closed. Now let \cref{IntegralityT0,GrowthCondition} be satisfied. Increase $k$ such that $j_k^{-3/2}\leq C/2$. Then we have
	\begin{equation*}
		|T_t^{j_k}|_\tau(S_t^k\setminus X_t^k)\leq-\frac{\hat c}{r_k^n}\Delta_{j_k}|T_t^{j_k}|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n),
	\end{equation*}
	where $\hat c>0$ only depends on $\Omega$ (implicitly on the dimension $n$).
<!-- end prop-8 -->
<!-- proof-13: body_md -->
Assume that $X_t^{k}$ is not closed. 
	Then there exists $x\in\mathbb{R}^n\setminus X_t^k$ with $|T_t^{j_k}|_\tau(\overline{B}_r(x))< \lambda r$ for some $r\in(0,r_k)$ and a sequence $(x_i)\subset X_t^{k}$ with $x_i\to x$.
	Define $r_i =\textup{dist}(x_i,\partial B_r(x))<r$ for $i$ sufficiently large. 
	Since $x_i\in X_t^k$, we obtain $|T_t^{j_k}|_\tau(\overline{B}_{r_i}(x_i))\geq \lambda r_i$.
	Note that $r_i\to r$ by $x_i\to x$.
	Hence, we get (using also $\overline{B}_{r_i}(x_i)\subset \overline{B}_{r}(x)$)
	\begin{equation*}
		\lambda r>|T_t^{j_k}|_\tau(\overline{B}_r(x))\geq\liminf_i|T_t^{j_k}|_\tau(\overline{B}_{r_i}(x_i))\geq \liminf_i\lambda r_i=\lambda r,
	\end{equation*}
	which yields a contradiction. Thus, set $X_t^{k}$ is closed.\\
	Next, we prove the second claim. To this end, we cover $\{ \textup{dist}(\cdot,\Omega)\leq 2 \}$ with closed balls whose radii are equal to $r_k$ and whose number does not exceed $c(\Omega)r_k^{-n}$.
	Let $\overline{B}_{r_k}(\hat x)$ be any of these balls.
	Define $R_t^k=\overline{B}_{r_k}(\hat x)\cap(S_t^k\setminus X_t^k)$.
	
	\underline{STEP 1 (covering of $R_t^k$):} For each $x\in R_t^k$ there exists some $r_x\in(0,r_k)$ such that $|T_t^{j_k}|_\tau(\overline{B}_{r_x}(x))< \lambda r_x$.
	Let $f_x(r)=|T_t^{j_k}|_\tau(\overline{B}_r(x))$ for $x\in R_t^k$ and $r\in\mathbb{R}_+$.
	By \cref{GrowthSBall} we have $\mathcal{L}([0,r_x]\cap \{ f_x'=0 \})>(1-\lambda/\tau(1))r_x$.
	Further, for each $x\in R_t^k$ there exists $\tilde r_{x}\in((1-\lambda/\tau(1))r_{x},r_{x})$ such that $f_{x}'(\tilde r_{x})=0$ (otherwise $\mathcal{L}([0,r_x]\cap \{ f_x'=0 \})\leq (1-\lambda/\tau(1))r_x$).
	Note that $\{ \overline{B}_{\tilde r_x}(x)\:|\: x\in R_t^k \}$ is a Besicovitch cover of $R_t^k$ because $\sup_{x\in R_t^k}\tilde r_x\leq r_k<\infty$.
	By \cref{Besicovitch} there exists a sequence $(x_\ell)\subset R_t^k$ such that the balls $\overline{B}_{\tilde r_{x_\ell}}(x_\ell)$ are mutually disjoint and 
	\begin{equation*}
		|T_t^{j_k}|_\tau(R_t^k)\leq\mathbb B_n\sum_{\ell=1}^{\infty}|T_t^{j_k}|_\tau(\overline{B}_{\tilde r_{x_\ell}}(x_\ell))\leq  2\mathbb B_n\sum_{\ell=1}^{\ell_0}|T_t^{j_k}|_\tau(\overline{B}_{\tilde r_{x_\ell}}(x_\ell)),
	\end{equation*}
	where $\ell_0\in\mathbb N$ is chosen sufficiently large. We may assume that $|T_t^{j_k}|_\tau(\overline{B}_{\tilde r_{x_\ell}}(x_\ell))>0$ for all $\ell=1,\ldots,\ell_0$.
	
	\underline{STEP 2 (admissible deformation $f_1^h\in\mathcal{A}_{j_k}(T_t^{j_k})$):}
\begin{figure}
	\centering
	\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=0.86, transform shape=false]
		\draw[line width=1,->] (0,0) -- (6.9,0);
		\draw[line width=1,->] (0,0) -- (0,6.9);
		
		\draw[color=lightgray,line width=2] (3.5,0) -- (5.5,0);
		
		\draw[line width=2] (0,0) -- (3.5,0) -- (5.5,5.5) -- (6.9,6.9);
		
		\draw[color=lightgray,line width=2] (0,0) -- (0,5.5);
		
		\node[lightgray,fill,circle,minimum size=2mm,inner sep=0pt] at (0,0) {};
		
		\node at (0,-0.4) {$x_\ell$};
		\node at (3.5,-0.4) {$\partial B_{\tilde r_{x_\ell}}(x_\ell)$};
		\node at (5.6,-0.4) {$\partial B_{\tilde r_{x_\ell}+h}(x_\ell)$};
		
		\node at (4.5,0.4) {$\textcolor{lightgray}{A}$};
		\node at (-0.6,2.75) {$\textcolor{lightgray}{f(A)}$};
		
		\node at (7.8,0) {$\partial B_r(x_\ell)$};
		\node at (0,7.3) {$f(\partial B_r(x_\ell))$};
		
		\draw[lightgray,line width=1]  (3.5,-0.15) -- (3.5,0.15);
		\draw[line width=1]  (5.5,-0.15) -- (5.5,0.15);
		\draw[lightgray,line width=1]  (-0.15,3.5) -- (0.15,3.5);
		\draw [line width=1] (-0.15,5.5) -- (0.15,5.5);
		
		\node at (2.5,4) {$\textcolor{gray}{\textup{Lip}(f|_{B_{\tilde r_{x_\ell}+h}(x_\ell)})=\frac{\tilde r_{x_\ell}+h}{h}}$};
	\end{tikzpicture}
	\caption{Admissible deformation $f_1^h$ near $x_\ell$.}
	\label{fig3}
\end{figure}
	Take $h\in\mathbb{R}_+^*$ such that the balls $\overline{B}_{\tilde r_{x_\ell}+h}(x_\ell)$ are mutually disjoint ($\ell=1,\ldots,\ell_0$).
	Define $f_1^h:\mathbb{R}^n\to\mathbb{R}^n$ by (see \cref{fig3})
	\begin{equation*}
		f_1^h(x)=\begin{cases*}
			x&if $x\in\mathbb{R}^n\setminus \bigcup_{\ell=1}^{\ell_0}B_{\tilde r_{x_\ell}+h}(x_\ell )$,\\
			x_\ell&if $x\in \overline{B}_{\tilde r_{x_\ell}}(x_\ell)$ for some $\ell=1,\ldots,\ell_0$,\\
			x_\ell+ \frac{\tilde{r}_{x_\ell}+h}{h}(|x-x_\ell|-\tilde{r}_{x_\ell}) \frac{x-x_\ell}{|x-x_\ell|}&if $x\in B_{\tilde r_{x_\ell}+h}(x_\ell)\setminus \overline{B}_{\tilde r_{x_\ell}}(x_\ell)$ for some $\ell=1,\ldots,\ell_0$.
		\end{cases*}
	\end{equation*}
	Note that $f_1^h$ is Lipschitz with $\textup{Lip}(f_1^h)=(\max_{\ell=1,\ldots,\ell_0}\tilde r_{x_\ell}+h)/h$. 
	We get
	\begin{equation*}
		|f_1^h-\textup{id}|_\infty\leq \tilde{r}_{x_\ell}<r_{x_\ell}<r_k\leq j_k^{-2} ,
	\end{equation*}
	which shows that the property in the first bullet point in \cref{AdmDeform} is satisfied.
	Next, we use the proposition on \cite[pp.\ 371--372]{F} with $U=\mathbb{R}^n,T=T_t^{j_k},f=\textup{id}$, and $g=f_1^h$. Let $\sigma\in C(\mathbb{R}^n)$ with 
	\begin{equation*}
		\textup{max}(|\mathrm{D} f(x)|_2,|\mathrm{D} g(x)|_2)\leq\sigma(x)
	\end{equation*}
	for $\mathcal{L}^n$-a.e.\ $x\in\mathbb{R}^n$. One can take any continuous $\sigma$ with $\sigma\geq 1$ and $\sigma =(\tilde r_{x_\ell}+h)/h$ on $B_{\tilde r_{x_\ell}+h}(x_\ell)\setminus \overline{B}_{\tilde r_{x_\ell}}(x_\ell)$ for all $\ell=1,\ldots,\ell_0$. Then the proposition implies (recall \cref{AdmDeform})
	\begin{equation}
		\label[ineq]{in1:SigmaEst}
		\mathbb \mathbb{M}\left((h_{f_1^h})_\#(\llbracket 0,1\rrbracket\times T_t^{j_k})\right)\leq |T_t^{j_k}|(|f_1^h-\textup{id}|\sigma).
	\end{equation}
	To simplify the notation, we abbreviate $\overline{B}_\ell=\overline{B}_{\tilde r_{x_\ell}}(x_\ell),A_\ell=B_{\tilde r_{x_\ell}+h}(x_\ell)\setminus \overline{B}_{\tilde r_{x_\ell}}(x_\ell), T=\theta\mathcal{H}^1\mathbin{\vrule height 1.3ex depth 0pt width 0.13ex\vrule height 0.13ex depth 0pt width 1ex} S=T_t^{j_k}$, and $(f_1^h)_\# T=\eta\mathcal{H}^1\mathbin{\vrule height 1.3ex depth 0pt width 0.13ex\vrule height 0.13ex depth 0pt width 1ex} f_1^h(S)$, cf.\ \cref{FedererPushforward}.
	We get (\cref{FedererPushforward} and area formula \cite[Cor.\ 3.2.20]{F})
	\begin{multline*}
		|(f_1^h)_\# T|_\tau(\overline{B}_\ell\cup A_\ell)=\int_{f_1^h(A_\ell\cap S)}\tau(|\eta|)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{H}^1=\int\sum_{x\in (f_1^h)^{-1}(y)}1_{A_\ell\cap S}(x)\tau(|\theta(x)|)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{H}^1(y)\\
		=\int_{A_\ell\cap S}\tau(|\theta|)\mathrm{ap\,J}(f|_{A_\ell\cap S})\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{H}^1\leq\frac{\tilde{r}_{x_\ell}+h}{h}|T|_\tau(A_\ell)\to (\tilde{r}_{x_\ell}+h)\cdot 0=0
	\end{multline*}
	for $h\to 0$ for all $\ell=1,\ldots,\ell_0$.
	Thus, we can choose $h$ sufficiently small such that 
	\begin{equation}
		\label[ineq]{in1:auxh}
		-\frac{1}{2}|T|_\tau(\overline{B}_\ell)+\frac{1}{2h}|T|_\tau(A_\ell)\leq -|(f_1^h)_\# T|_\tau(\overline{B}_\ell\cup A_\ell)
	\end{equation}
	for all $\ell=1,\ldots,\ell_0$.
	Further, in \cref{in1:SigmaEst} we can just take $\sigma$ with $\sigma = 1$ on $\overline{B}_\ell$, $\sigma= (\tilde r_{x_\ell}+h)/h$ on $A_\ell$, and $\sigma=\max_{\ell=1,\ldots,\ell_0}(\tilde r_{x_\ell}+h)/h$ else. 
	                                                                                       
	We get (using $j_k^{-3/2}\leq C/2$, \cref{GrowthCondition}, and \cref{in1:auxh})
	\begin{multline*}
		\mathbb \mathbb{M}\left((h_{f_1^h})_\#(\llbracket 0,1\rrbracket\times T)\right)\leq |T|(|f_1^h-\textup{id}|\sigma)=\sum_{\ell=1}^{\ell_0}\left(|T|(|f_1^h-\textup{id}||_{\overline{B}_\ell})+|T|(|f_1^h-\textup{id}||_{A_\ell}\sigma)\right)\\
		\leq \sum_{\ell=1}^{\ell_0}\left(\tilde r_{x_\ell}|T|(\overline{B}_\ell)+\tilde{r}_{x_\ell}\frac{\tilde{r}_{x_\ell}+h}{h}|T|(A_\ell)\right)\leq r_k\sum_{\ell=1}^{\ell_0}\left(|T|(\overline{B}_\ell)+\frac{1}{h}|T|(A_\ell)\right)\leq j_k^{-1}\frac{C}{2}\sum_{\ell=1}^{\ell_0}\left(|T|(\overline{B}_\ell)+\frac{1}{h}|T|(A_\ell)\right)\\
		                                                                                                                                                
		\leq j_k^{-1}\sum_{\ell=1}^{\ell_0}\left( |T|_\tau(\overline{B}_\ell) -|(f_1^h)_\# T|_\tau(\overline{B}_\ell\cup A_\ell)\right)
		\leq j_k^{-1}(|T|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)-|(f_1^h)_\# T|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)).
	\end{multline*}
	This shows that the first condition in the second bullet point in \cref{AdmDeform} is satisfied. The second condition in the second bullet point in \cref{AdmDeform} is also satisfied:
	By the proof of the lemma in \cite[Sec.\ 4.10]{B78} (cf.\ \cite[Lem.\ 4.12]{KT17}) it is enough to verify that $|(f_1^h)_\# T|_\tau(\overline{B}_\ell\cup A_\ell)\leq e^{-j_k(\tilde r_{x_\ell} +h)}|T|_\tau(\overline{B}_\ell\cup A_\ell)$ for all $\ell=1,\ldots,\ell_0$ and $h$ sufficiently small because then (using \cref{propertiesF}) 
	\begin{multline*}
		|(f_1^h)_\# T|(\phi)-|T|_\tau(\phi)=\sum_{\ell=1}^{\ell_0}\left( |(f_1^h)_\# T|(\phi1_{\overline{B}_\ell\cup A_\ell})-|T|_\tau(\phi 1_{\overline{B}_\ell\cup A_\ell}) \right)\\
		\leq\sum_{\ell=1}^{\ell_0}\left( \max_{\overline{B}_\ell\cup\overline{A}_\ell}\phi|(f_1^h)_\# T|(\overline{B}_\ell\cup A_\ell)-\min_{\overline{B}_\ell\cup\overline{A}_\ell}\phi|T|_\tau(\overline{B}_\ell\cup A_\ell) \right)\\
		\leq \sum_{\ell=1}^{\ell_0}\left( \min_{\overline{B}_\ell\cup\overline{A}_\ell}\phi e^{j_k(\tilde r_{x_\ell}+h)}|(f_1^h)_\# T|(\overline{B}_\ell\cup A_\ell)-\min_{\overline{B}_\ell\cup\overline{A}_\ell}\phi |T|_\tau(\overline{B}_\ell\cup A_\ell) \right)\leq 0
	\end{multline*}
	for all $\phi\in\mathcal{F}_{j_k}^+$. But we already argued that $|(f_1^h)_\# T|_\tau(\overline{B}_\ell\cup A_\ell)\to 0$ for $h\to 0$ and $|T|_\tau(\overline{B}_\ell)>0$ for all $\ell=1,\ldots,\ell_0$. Thus, the desired inequality is satisfied for all $\ell=1,\ldots,\ell_0$. This finally shows $f_1^h\in\mathcal A_{j_k}(T_j^{j_k})$.
	
	\underline{STEP 3 (final estimates):} Let $\delta\in(0,1)$. We can decrease $h$ such that (the left-hand side goes to zero, cf.\ STEPS 1 and 2, and the right-hand side is assumed to be positive)
	\begin{equation*}
		|(f_1^h)_\# T|_\tau(\overline{B}_\ell\cup A_\ell)-|T|_\tau(A_\ell)\leq\delta|T|_\tau(\overline{B}_\ell)
	\end{equation*}
	for all $\ell=1,\ldots,\ell_0$. Hence, we obtain (recall STEP 1)
	\begin{multline*}
		\Delta_{j_k}|T|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)\leq|(f_1^h)_\# T|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)-|T|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)=\sum_{\ell=1}^{\ell_0}\left(|(f_1^h)_\# T|_\tau(\overline{B}_\ell\cup A_\ell)-|T|_\tau(\overline{B}_\ell\cup A_\ell)\right)\\
		\leq (\delta-1)\sum_{\ell=1}^{\ell_0}|T|_\tau(\overline{B}_\ell)
		\leq \frac{\delta-1}{2\mathbb B_n}|T|_\tau(R_t^k).
	\end{multline*}
	By the beginning of the proof this gives
	\begin{equation*}
		|T|_\tau(S\setminus X_t^k)\leq\frac{2\mathbb B_nc(\Omega)}{(\delta-1)r_k^n}\Delta_{j_k}|T|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n).
	\end{equation*}
	Note that this inequality does not depend on $h$ anymore. Hence, we can let $\delta\to 0$ and take $\hat c=2\mathbb B_nc(\Omega)$.
<!-- end proof-13 -->
<!-- thm-4: body_md -->
\label{Allard}
	Let $U\subset\mathbb{R}^n$ open and $V\in\mathcal V_k(U)$. Assume that $\|\delta V \|$ is a Radon measure. Then 
	\begin{multline*}
		s^{-k}\| V\|(\overline{B}_s(x))e^{ \int_{[r,s]} \delta V(f(V,x;u))\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}(u)}-r^{-k}\| V\|(\overline{B}_r(x))\\
		=\int_{\{ (y,S)\:|\: r<|x-y|\leq s \}}|x-y|^{-k-2}|P^\perp(x-y)|^2e^{\int_{[r,|x-y|]}\delta V(f(V,x;u))\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}(u)}\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}} V(y,P)
	\end{multline*}
	for all $\textup{dist}(x,\textup{supp}(\| V\|))<r<s<\textup{dist}(x,\mathbb{R}^n\setminus U)$, where
	\begin{equation*}
		f(V,x;u)(y)=\begin{cases*}
			(u\| V\|(\overline{B}_u(x)))^{-1}(y-x)&if $y\in\overline{B}_u(x)$,\\
			0&else.
		\end{cases*}
	\end{equation*}
<!-- end thm-4 -->
<!-- lem-10: body_md -->
\label{auxAllard}
	Let $U\subset\mathbb{R}^n$ open, $V\in\mathcal V_k(U)$, and $\varphi\in C_c^1(U)$. Assume that $\|V\|(U)<\infty$. Then 
	\begin{equation*}
		|\delta (\varphi \ast V)(g)|\leq c(n,\varphi,V)|g|_\infty
	\end{equation*}
	for all $g\in C_c^1(U;\mathbb{R}^n)$, in particular $\delta (\varphi \ast V)\in \mathcal M(U;\mathbb{R}^n)$ respectively $\|\delta (\varphi \ast V)\| \in\mathcal{M}_+(U)$.
<!-- end lem-10 -->
<!-- proof-14: body_md -->
We have
	\begin{multline*}
		|\delta (\varphi \ast V)(g)|=\left|\int_{\mathcal G_k(U)}\int_{U}P:\mathrm{D} g(x-y)\varphi(y)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n(y)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}} V(x,P)\right|\\
		=\left|\int_{\mathcal G_k(U)}\int_{U}P:(g(x-y)\otimes\nabla\varphi(y))\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n(y)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}} V(x,P)\right|\leq \sqrt k|g|_\infty|\nabla\varphi|_\infty\mathcal{L}^n(\textup{supp}(\varphi))V(\mathcal G_k(U)).
	\end{multline*}
	Thus, we can take $c(n,\varphi,V)=\sqrt k|\nabla\varphi|_\infty\mathcal{L}^n(\textup{supp}(\varphi))V(\mathcal G_k(U))$. The last conclusion follows directly,
	\begin{equation*}
		\|\delta (\varphi \ast V)\|(U)=	\sup\{ \delta (\varphi \ast V)(g)\:|\: g\in C_c^\infty(U;\mathbb{R}^n),|g|_\infty\leq 1 \}\leq c(n,\varphi,V).\qedhere
	\end{equation*}
<!-- end proof-14 -->
<!-- lem-11: body_md -->
\label{aux2}
	Let $\mu\in\mathcal{M}_+(\mathbb{R}^n), \gamma,\delta\in(0,1)$, and $\overline{B}_r(x)\subset\mathbb{R}^n$ some closed ball. Then
	\begin{equation*}
		(\Psi_\varepsilon\ast\mu)(\overline{B}_r(x))=\mu(\Psi_\varepsilon\ast 1_{\overline{B}_r(x)}).
	\end{equation*}
	Moreover, we have
	\begin{equation*}
		\Psi_\varepsilon\ast 1_{\overline{B}_r(x)}\geq (1-\gamma)1_{\overline{B}_{(1-\delta)r}(x)}
	\end{equation*}
	in $\mathbb{R}^n$ for $\varepsilon$ sufficiently small.
<!-- end lem-11 -->
<!-- proof-15: body_md -->
By the outer regularity of $\Psi_\varepsilon\ast\mu$ there exist bounded and open $U_i\subset\mathbb{R}^n$ with $\overline{B}_r(x)\subset U_i$ and a sequence $(\delta_i)\subset(0,1)$ with $\delta_i\to 0$ such that $(\Psi_\varepsilon\ast\mu)(U_i)\leq(\Psi_\varepsilon\ast\mu)(\overline{B}_r(x))+\delta_i$. Now pick a sequence $(\phi_i)\subset C_c(\mathbb{R}^n)$ with $\textup{supp}(\phi_i)\subset U_i,\phi_i\equiv 1$ on $\overline{B}_r(x),\phi_i\in[0,1]$, and $\phi_i\to 1_{\overline{B}_r(x)}$ pointwise. Then $(\Psi_\varepsilon\ast\mu)(\overline{B}_r(x))\leq(\Psi_\varepsilon\ast\mu)(\phi_i)\leq(\Psi_\varepsilon\ast\mu)(U_i)\leq(\Psi_\varepsilon\ast\mu)(\overline{B}_r(x))+\delta_i$ implying (Lebesgue's dominated convergence theorem)
	\begin{equation*}
		(\Psi_\varepsilon\ast\mu)(\overline{B}_r(x))=\lim_i(\Psi_\varepsilon\ast\mu)(\phi_i)=\lim_i\mu(\Psi_\varepsilon\ast\phi_i)=\lim_i\int\int \Psi_\varepsilon(z-y)\phi_i(y)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n(y)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mu(z).
	\end{equation*}
	This gives (again by Lebesgue's dominated convergence theorem)
	\begin{equation*}
		|(\Psi_\varepsilon\ast\mu)(\overline{B}_r(x))-\mu(\Psi_\varepsilon\ast 1_{\overline{B}_r(x)})|\leq\max\Psi_\varepsilon\lim_i\int\int|\phi_i(y)-1_{\overline{B}_r(x)}(y)|\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n(y)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mu=0,
	\end{equation*}
	which proves the first claim. By definition of $\Psi_\varepsilon$ the function $\Psi_\varepsilon\ast 1_{\overline{B}_r(x)}$ is bounded from below by $1-\gamma$ on $\overline{B}_{(1-\delta)r}(x)$ for $\varepsilon$ sufficiently small, which directly yields the second statement.
<!-- end proof-15 -->
<!-- prop-9: body_md -->
\label{LowerBound}
	Let the setup be as in \cref{Prop6.1,Prop6.4}. Recall the definition of $X_t^{j_k}$ from \cref{Lemma1}. For a.e.\ $t\in\mathbb{R}_+$ there exists $c_2=c_2(t)\in(0,1)$ and a subsequence $\ell \mapsto j_{k_\ell}$ (depending on $t$) such that 
	\begin{equation*}
		|T_t^{j_{k_\ell}}|_\tau(\overline{B}_r(x))\geq\frac{\lambda r}{11}
	\end{equation*}
	for all $x\in X_t^{j_{k_\ell}},r\in (0,c_2]$.
<!-- end prop-9 -->
<!-- proof-16: body_md -->
We follow the proof of \cite[Lem.\ 5.4]{KT20}. By definition of $X_t^{j_k}$ it is enough to find (for a.e.\ $t\in\mathbb{R}_+$) some $c_2\in(0,1)$ and validate $|T_t^{j_{k_\ell}}|_\tau(\overline{B}_r(x))\geq\frac{\lambda r}{11}$ for $x\in X_t^{j_{k_\ell}},r\in [r_{k_\ell},c_2]$ for a suitable subsequence $\ell\mapsto j_{k_\ell}$. 
	\Cref{in1:6.19} implies
	\begin{equation*}
		\limsup_k\int_{[0,R]}   I_k(t) \textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}(t)<\infty
	\end{equation*}
	for all $R\in\mathbb{R}_+$, where we use the abbreviation $I_k(t)=\int|\Psi_{\varepsilon_{j_k}}\ast\delta_\tau T_t^{j_k}|^2/(\Psi_{\varepsilon_{j_k}}\ast|T_t^{j_k}|_\tau+\varepsilon_{j_k})\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n$.
	In particular, Fatou's lemma yields
	\begin{equation*}
		\int_{[0,R]}\liminf_kI_k(t)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}(t)<\infty
	\end{equation*}
	for all $R\in\mathbb{R}_+$. Thus, for a.e.\ $t\in\mathbb{R}_+$ there exists a subsequence $\ell\mapsto j_{k_\ell}$ with
	\begin{equation*}
		\liminf_kI_k(t)=\lim_{\ell} I_{k_{\ell}}(t)\in\mathbb{R}_+.
	\end{equation*}
	Now fix any such $t$ and a corresponding subsequence $\ell\mapsto j_{k_\ell}$.
	Define
	\begin{equation*}
		\check c=\check c(t)=\sup_{\ell} I_{k_{\ell}}(t).
	\end{equation*}
	In the remainder of the proof, we abbreviate $T=\theta\mathcal{H}^1\mathbin{\vrule height 1.3ex depth 0pt width 0.13ex\vrule height 0.13ex depth 0pt width 1ex} S=T_t^{j_{k_\ell}},X=X_t^{j_{k_\ell}}$, and $\varepsilon=\varepsilon_{j_{k_\ell}}$. Let $V=v(\tau(|\theta|),S)\in\mathcal V_1(\mathbb{R}^n)$ (recall \cref{inducedV}). By \cref{auxAllard} we can apply \cref{Allard} to $V_\varepsilon=\Psi_{\varepsilon}\ast V$. In particular, we obtain
	\begin{equation}
		\label[ineq]{in1:AllardIn}
		s^{-1}\| V_\varepsilon \|(\overline{B}_s(x))e^{ \int_{[r,s]} \delta V_{\varepsilon}(f(V_{\varepsilon},x;u))\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}(u)}\geq r^{-1}\| V_{\varepsilon }\|(\overline{B}_r(x))
	\end{equation}
	for all $\textup{dist}(x,\textup{supp}(\| V_{\varepsilon}\|))<r<s<\infty$.
	Note that $X\subset \textup{supp}(\| V_{\varepsilon}\|)$. 
	Take $x\in X$, $a\in(0,1)$, and $u\in [ar_{k_\ell},1]$.
	Depending on the factor $a\in(0,1)$, we can choose $\ell$ sufficiently large such that the forthcoming estimates are valid. 
	Later, we take $a=1/2$.
	By the same conversion as in \cite[(5.13)]{KT20} we have (recall \cref{Smoothed})
	\begin{equation}
		\label[ineq]{in1:Y}
		|\delta V_{\varepsilon}(f(V_{\varepsilon},x;u))|\leq \| V_{\varepsilon}\|(\overline{B}_u(x))^{-1}\int_{\overline{B}_u(x)}|\Psi_{\varepsilon}\ast\delta V|\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n.
	\end{equation}
	Further, using the Cauchy\textendash Schwarz inequality, we get
	\begin{equation*}
		\int_{\overline{B}_u(x)}|\Psi_{\varepsilon}\ast\delta V|\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n\leq I_{k_\ell}(t)^{1/2}\left( \int_{\overline{B}_u(x)}(\Psi_{\varepsilon}\ast\| V\|+\varepsilon)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n \right)^{1/2},
	\end{equation*}
	where we used (cf.\ text passage below \cref{hEstimates})
	\begin{equation*}
		I_{k_\ell}(t)=\int\frac{|\Psi_{\varepsilon}\ast\delta_\tau T|^2}{\Psi_{\varepsilon}\ast|T|_\tau+\varepsilon } \textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n=\int\frac{ |\Psi_{\varepsilon}\ast\delta V|^2}{\Psi_{\varepsilon}\ast\| V\|+\varepsilon}\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n.
	\end{equation*}
	This gives
	\begin{equation}
		\label[ineq]{in1:Z}
		\int_{\overline{B}_u(x)}|\Psi_{\varepsilon}\ast\delta V|\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n\leq \check c^{1/2}\left( \int_{\overline{B}_u(x)}\Psi_{\varepsilon}\ast |T|_\tau\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n+\varepsilon\mathcal{L}^n(\overline{B}_u(x)) \right)^{1/2}.
	\end{equation}
	Now it is not difficult to see that $\int_{\overline{B}_u(x)}\Psi_{\varepsilon}\ast |T|_\tau\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n\leq (\Psi_{\varepsilon}\ast|T|_\tau)(\overline{B}_u(x))$\footnote{Formally, abbreviate $\int_{\overline{B}_u(x)}\Psi_{\varepsilon}\ast |T|_\tau\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n\leq (\Psi_{\varepsilon}\ast|T|_\tau)(\overline{B}_u(x))$ as $I\leq \mu(\overline{B})$. By the outer regularity of $\mu$ we have $\mu(\overline{B})=\inf\{ \mu(U)\:|\: U\supset\overline{B}\textup{ open} \}$. In particular, for every $\delta>0$ there is some open and bounded set $U\supset \overline{B}$ with $\mu(U)\leq\mu(\overline{B})+\delta$. Now take $\phi\in C_c(\mathbb{R}^n)$ with $\textup{supp}(\phi)\subset U,\phi\equiv 1$ on $\overline{B}$, and $\phi\in[0,1]$. Then $I\leq \mu(\phi)\leq\mu(U)\leq \mu(\overline{B})+\delta$.}.
	Define $\xi(u)=(\Psi_{\varepsilon}\ast|T|_\tau)(\overline{B}_u(x))$.
	Applying \cref{aux2} we have
	\begin{equation*}
		\xi(u)=|T|_\tau(\Psi_{\varepsilon}\ast1_{\overline{B}_u(x)})\geq\frac{1}{2}|T|_\tau(1_{ \overline{B}_{u/2}(x) })
	\end{equation*}
	(In the remainder, we always assume that $\ell$ is taken such that $\varepsilon_{j_{k_{\ell}}}$ is sufficiently small.)
	By $ar_{k_\ell}\leq u$ and $x\in X$ we have
	\begin{equation*}
		\frac{1}{2}|T|_\tau(1_{ \overline{B}_{u/2}(x) })\geq \frac{1}{2}|T|_\tau(1_{ \overline{B}_{ar_{k_\ell}/2}(x) })\geq \lambda\frac{ar_{k_\ell}}{4}.
	\end{equation*}
	Further, recalling $\varepsilon<j_{k_\ell}^{-6}<aj_{k_\ell}^{-5/2}=ar_{k_\ell}\leq u\leq 1$ (see \cref{Prop6.1}), we get
	\begin{equation*}
		\varepsilon\mathcal{L}^n(\overline{B}_u(x))\leq \varepsilon\mathcal{L}^n(\overline{B}_1(x))\leq\lambda\frac{ar_{k_\ell}}{4}\leq \xi(u).
	\end{equation*}
	Using the above together with \cref{in1:Y,in1:Z} gives
	\begin{multline*}
		|\delta V_{\varepsilon}(f(V_{\varepsilon},x;u))|\leq \| V_{\varepsilon}\|(\overline{B}_u(x))^{-1}\check c^{1/2}\left( \int_{\overline{B}_u(x)}\Psi_{\varepsilon}\ast |T|_\tau\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n+\varepsilon\mathcal{L}^n(\overline{B}_u(x)) \right)^{1/2}\\
		\leq \xi(u)^{-1}\check c^{1/2}\left( \xi(u)+\xi(u) \right)^{1/2}=(2\check c)^{1/2}\xi(u)^{-1/2}.
	\end{multline*}
	Using this in \cref{in1:AllardIn} yields
	\begin{equation}
		\label[ineq]{in1:inAllard2}
		s^{-1}\xi(s)e^{ \tilde c\int_{[r,s]} \xi(u)^{-1/2}\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}(u)}\geq r^{-1}\xi(r)
	\end{equation}
	for all $ar_{k_\ell}\leq r<s\leq 1$, where $\tilde c=(2\check c)^{1/2}$. Note that $\xi$ is non-decreasing on $[ar_{k_\ell},1]$, thus it is differentiable a.e.\ in $[ar_{k_\ell},1]$. It is also continuous: for $\phi\in C_c(\mathbb{R}^n)$ one may identify $(\Psi_\varepsilon\ast |T|_\tau) (\phi)$ as the $L^2$-pairing of $\phi$ with the $C_c^\infty$-function $y\mapsto \int\Psi_\varepsilon(z-y)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}|T|_\tau(z)$. Hence, the fundamental theorem of calculus implies that $\xi$ admits an antiderivative on $[ar_{k_\ell},1]$, in particular this is true for $\xi^{-1/2}$ (this function is continuous and non-increasing on $[ar_{k_\ell},1]$). Let $\Xi$ be the antiderivative of $\xi^{-1/2}$ given by $\Xi(r)=\int_{[ar_{k_\ell},r]}\xi(u)^{-1/2}\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}(u)$ for $r\in [ar_{k_\ell},1]$. \Cref{in1:inAllard2} directly implies that 
	\begin{equation*}
		r\mapsto r^{-1}\xi(r)e^{\tilde c\Xi(r)}
	\end{equation*}
	is non-decreasing on $[ar_{k_\ell},1]$. Thus, we have $\partial_r(r^{-1}\xi(r)e^{\tilde c\Xi(r)})\geq 0$ for a.e.\ $r\in [ar_{k_\ell},1]$. A straightforward calculation shows that this is equivalent to 
	\begin{equation*}
		\xi'(r)\geq-\tilde c\xi(r)^{1/2}+\frac{\xi(r)}{r}
	\end{equation*}
	for a.e.\ $r\in [ar_{k_\ell},1]$. Using this, it is easy to obtain
	\begin{equation*}
		r\frac{\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}}{\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}} r}\left( \frac{\xi(r)^{1/2}}{r^{1/2}}+\tilde cr^{1/2} \right)\geq 0
	\end{equation*}
	for a.e.\ $r\in [ar_{k_\ell},1]$, which shows that $r\mapsto \xi(r)^{1/2}/r^{1/2}+\tilde cr^{1/2} $ is non-decreasing on $ [ar_{k_\ell},1]$. Thus (using also the previous estimate $\xi(u)\geq \lambda ar_{k_\ell}/4$ for $u\in[ar_{k_\ell},1]$)
	\begin{equation*}
		\frac{\xi(r)^{1/2}}{r^{1/2}}+\tilde cr^{1/2}\geq \frac{\xi(ar_{k_\ell})^{1/2}}{(ar_{k_\ell})^{1/2}}+\tilde cr_{k_\ell}^{1/2}\geq  \frac{\lambda^{1/2}}{2}+\tilde cr_{k_\ell}^{1/2}.
	\end{equation*}
	Hence, we can choose $\bar c\in(0,1/2)$ such that 
	\begin{equation*}
		\frac{\xi(r)}{r}\geq \left(  \frac{\lambda^{1/2}}{2}-\tilde cr^{1/2}  \right)^2\geq \frac{\lambda}{5}
	\end{equation*}
	for all $r\in [ar_{k_\ell},1]\cap [0,\bar c]$.
	Next, we estimate $|T|_\tau(\overline{B}_{2r}(x))/(2r)$. If $y\in\mathbb{R}^n\setminus \overline{B}_{2r}(x)$, then
	\begin{multline*}
		\Psi_\varepsilon\ast 1_{\overline{B}_r(x)}(y)=\int_{\overline{B}_r(x)}\Psi_\varepsilon(y-z)\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n(z)=\frac{c_\varepsilon}{(2\pi\varepsilon^2)^{n/2}}\int_{\overline{B}_r(x)}\psi(y-z)e^{-|y-z|^2/(2\varepsilon^2)}\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n(z)\\
		\leq \frac{c_\varepsilon}{(2\pi\varepsilon^2)^{n/2}}e^{-(ar_{k_\ell})^2/(2\varepsilon^2)}\mathcal{L}^n(\overline{B}_r(x))\leq c_\varepsilon\varepsilon^{-n}e^{-1/(2\varepsilon)}\mathcal{L}^n(\overline{B}_r(x)),
	\end{multline*}
	where we also used $\varepsilon<(ar_{k_\ell})^2$ in the last inequality. Note that $1_{\overline{B}_{2r}(x)}+(\Psi_\varepsilon\ast 1_{\overline{B}_r(x)})|_{\mathbb{R}^n\setminus \overline{B}_{2r}(x)}\geq \Psi_\varepsilon\ast 1_{\overline{B}_r(x)}$. This gives (\cref{aux2})
	\begin{multline*}
		|T|_\tau(\overline{B}_{2r}(x))\geq |T|_\tau( \Psi_\varepsilon\ast 1_{\overline{B}_r(x)})- |T|_\tau((\Psi_\varepsilon\ast 1_{\overline{B}_r(x)})|_{\mathbb{R}^n\setminus \overline{B}_{2r}(x)})\\
		\geq  ( \Psi_\varepsilon\ast |T|_\tau)(\overline{B}_r(x))-  c_\varepsilon\varepsilon^{-n}e^{-1/(2\varepsilon)}\mathcal{L}^n(\overline{B}_r(x))|T|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)
	\end{multline*}
	implying (using also \cref{in1:6.3})
	\begin{equation*}
		\frac{|T|_\tau(\overline{B}_{2r}(x))}{2r}\geq\frac{\xi(r)}{2r}-c_\varepsilon\varepsilon^{-n}e^{-1/(2\varepsilon)}\mathcal{L}^n(\overline{B}_r(x))(|T_0|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)+1)/(ar_{k_\ell})
	\end{equation*}
	for all $r\in [ar_{k_\ell},1]\cap [0,\bar c]$. Hence, taking $a=1/2$ and $c_2=c_2(t)=2\bar c\in(0,1)$ gives 
	\begin{equation*}
		\frac{|T|_\tau(\overline{B}_{r}(x))}{r}\geq \frac{\lambda}{11}
	\end{equation*}
	for all $r\in [r_{k_\ell},1]\cap [0, c_2]=2([ar_{k_\ell},1]\cap [0,\bar c])$ as desired.
<!-- end proof-16 -->
<!-- thm-5: body_md -->
\label{AllardRect}
	Let $U\subset\mathbb{R}^n$ open and $V\in\mathcal V_1(U)$. Assume that $\|\delta V \|$ is a Radon measure and $\Theta^{*1}(\|  V\|,x)>0$ for $\|  V\|$-a.e.\ $x\in U$. Then $\Theta^{1}(\|  V\|,x)\in\mathbb{R}_+^*$ for $\|  V\|$-a.e.\ $x\in U$ and $V$ is $1$-rectifiable in the following sense \cite[Sec.\ 3.5]{A72}: there exist $\mathcal{H}^1$-measurable $A_i\subset U$ and numbers $w_i\in\mathbb{R}$ such that 
	\begin{itemize}
		\item for all $i$ and compact $K\subset U$ there exists a $(\mathcal{H}^1,1)$-rectifiable\footnote{By \cite[Sec.\ 2.8, Def.\ 4]{A72} we have $\mathcal{H}^1(S)<\infty$ and there exists a sequence $(P_i)\subset G(n,1)$ and $C^1$ maps $f_i:P_i\to\mathbb{R}^n$ with $P_if_i=\textup{id}$ and $\mathcal{H}^1\left( S\setminus\bigcup_if_i(P_i) \right)=0$.} set $S$ with $A_i\cap K\subset S$
		\item and $V=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}w_iV(E_i,1)$.
	\end{itemize}
<!-- end thm-5 -->
<!-- prop-10: body_md -->
\label{Prop5.6}
	For each $i\in\mathbb N$ let $T_i=\theta_i\mathcal{H}^1\mathbin{\vrule height 1.3ex depth 0pt width 0.13ex\vrule height 0.13ex depth 0pt width 1ex} S_i\in\mathcal{R}_1(\mathbb{R}^n)$ be compactly supported. Assume that $\sup_i|T_i|_\tau(\mathbb{R}^n)<\infty$. Define $V_i=v(\tau(|\theta_i|),S_i)\in\mathcal{M}_+(\mathcal{G}_1(\mathbb{R}^n))\subset\mathcal{V}_1(\mathbb{R}^n)$ for all $i\in\mathbb N$. Further, assume that
	\begin{equation*}
		\liminf_i\int\frac{|\Psi_{\varepsilon_i}\ast\delta_\tau T_i|^2}{\Psi_{\varepsilon_i}\ast|T_i|_\tau+\varepsilon_i}\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n<\infty
	\end{equation*} 
	for some sequence $(\varepsilon_i)\subset(0,1)$ with $\varepsilon_i\to 0$. Then there exists a subsequence $(V_{i_m})$ with $V_{i_m}\stackrel{*}{\rightharpoonup} V\in\mathcal{V}_1(\mathbb{R}^n)$ for $m\to\infty$ and $V$ admits a generalized curvature vector field $h_V$\footnote{$h_V$ is $\| V\|$-measurable with $\delta V(g)=-\int g\cdot h_V\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\| V\|$ for all $g\in C_c^1(\mathbb{R}^n;\mathbb{R}^n)$.} satisfying
	\begin{equation}
		\label[ineq]{in1:GenMean}
		\int|h_V|^2\phi\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\| V\|\leq\liminf_{m}\int\frac{|\Psi_{\varepsilon_{i_m}}\ast\delta_\tau T_{i_m}|^2\phi}{\Psi_{\varepsilon_{i_m}}\ast|  T_{i_m}|_\tau+\varepsilon_{i_m}}\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n
	\end{equation}
	for all $\phi\in\bigcup_j\mathcal F_j^+$. In particular, vector field $h_V$ is square-integrable with respect to $\| V\|$.
<!-- end prop-10 -->
<!-- proof-17: body_md -->
We can restrict to a subsequence $(i_m)$ with $V_{i_m}\stackrel{*}{\rightharpoonup} V\in\mathcal{M}_+(\mathcal{G}_1(\mathbb{R}^n))$ for $m\to\infty$ (Banach\textendash Alaoglu theorem).
	                   
		                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                              
		                 
	Take $\phi\in\bigcup_j\mathcal F_j^+$ with $\phi>0$ in $\mathbb{R}^n$.
	As in the proof of \cite[Prop.\:5.6]{KT17}, we define a Hilbert space by
	\begin{equation*}
		H_\phi=\left\{ g\in L_{loc}^2(\| V\|;\mathbb{R}^n)\:\middle|\:|g|_{H_\phi}<\infty \right\},\qquad\textup{where $|\cdot|_{H_\phi}$ induced by}\qquad (g_1,g_2)_{H_\phi}=\int g_1\cdot g_2\phi^{-1}\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\|V\|.
	\end{equation*}
	Note that $C_c^\infty(\mathbb{R}^n;\mathbb{R}^n)$ is dense in $H_\phi$. Take $g\in C_c^\infty(\mathbb{R}^n;\mathbb{R}^n)$. Then $g\in\mathcal F_{j_0}^n$ for some $j_0\in\mathbb N$. Using $V_{i_m}\stackrel{*}{\rightharpoonup} V$ and applying \cref{Prop5.5} (eventually $j_0\leq 2^{-1}\varepsilon_{i_m}^{-1/6}$ and $\varepsilon_{i_m}$ sufficiently small) yields the analogue of \cite[(5.51)]{KT17},
	\begin{equation*}
		\delta V(g)=\lim_m\delta_\tau T_{i_m}(g)=-\lim_m\int h_{\tau,\varepsilon_{i_m}}(T_{i_m})\cdot g\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}|T_{i_m}|_\tau.
	\end{equation*}
	Using the Cauchy\textendash Schwarz inequality and \cref{in1:5.24} we get (as in \cite[(5.52)]{KT17})
	\begin{equation*}
		\delta V(g)=-\lim_m\int h_{\tau,\varepsilon_{i_m}}(T_{i_m})\cdot g\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}|T_{i_m}|_\tau\leq\left( \liminf_m\int\frac{|\Psi_{\varepsilon_{i_m}}\ast\delta_\tau T_{i_m}|^2\phi }{\Psi_{\varepsilon_{i_m}}\ast|T_{i_m}|_\tau+\varepsilon_{i_m}}\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n \right)^{1/2}|g|_{H_\phi},
	\end{equation*}
	which is bounded by assumption and $\phi\leq 1$.
	As in the end of the proof of \cite[Prop.\ 5.6]{KT17}, we can apply the Hahn\textendash Banach theorem yielding a continuous and linear functional $\delta V:H_\phi\to\mathbb{R}$ (extended from $C_c^\infty(\mathbb{R}^n;\mathbb{R}^n)$). The Riesz representation theorem then implies the existence of a unique vector field $f\in H_\phi$ with $\delta V=(f,\cdot)_{H_\phi}$. Then 
	\begin{equation*}
		h_V=f\phi^{-1}\qquad\textup{and}\qquad |f|_{H_\phi}^2\leq \liminf_m\int\frac{|\Psi_{\varepsilon_{i_m}}\ast\delta_\tau T_{i_m}|^2\phi }{\Psi_{\varepsilon_{i_m}}\ast|T_{i_m}|_\tau+\varepsilon_{i_m}}\textcolor{black}{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}^n.
	\end{equation*}
	The last inequality is equivalent to \cref{in1:GenMean}. For $\phi\equiv 0$ \cref{in1:GenMean} is trivially satisfied. For the last statement we can take $\phi\equiv 1$.
<!-- end proof-17 -->
<!-- proof-18: body_md -->
For a.e.\ $t\in\mathbb{R}_+$ we have $c_2=c_2(t)$ and a subsequence $\ell\mapsto j_{k_\ell}$ (depending on $t$) as in \cref{LowerBound}.	
	Now fix any such $t\in\mathbb{R}_+$.
	\Cref{Prop5.6} yields 
	\begin{equation*}
		V_t^{j_{k_\ell}}\stackrel{*}{\rightharpoonup} V_t\in \mathcal{M}_+(\mathcal G_1(\mathbb{R}^n))
	\end{equation*}
